COMPLAINT FOR DECLARATORY JUDGMENT 
AND INJUNCTIVE RELIEF 


1. The Cable Communications Policy Act of 1984, 
47 U.S.C. § 533(b) (“Cable Act”), prohibits The Chesa- 
peake & Potomac Telephone Company of Virginia and its 
affiliate, Bell Atlanic Video Services Company, from 
providing video programming— including television shows, 
movies, sports, news, and political reporting and opinions 
—to subscribers in C&P’s telephone service area. This is 
an action pursuant to 28 U.S.C. §§ 2201 and 2202 to 
declare this prohibition unconstitutional and to enjoin the 
defendants from enforcing it. The government defend- 
ants, who bear the burden of justifying this prohibition, 
have stated that the prohibition is not necessary to serve 
any valid governmental purpose and that it should be 
repealed. Congress, however, has failed to repeal the pro- 
hibition, and the defendants have indicated that they will 
continue to enforce it. 


JURISDICTION AND VENUE 


2. This Court has subject matter jurisdiction of this 
controversy under 28 U.S.C. § 1331. 

3. Venue is proper in this judicial district pursuant to 
28 U.S.C. § 1391 (e). 


PARTIES 


4. Plaintiff The Chesapeake and Potomac Telephone 
Company of Virginia (“C&P”) is a corporation incor- 
porated under the laws of the Commonwealth of Virginia 


8 


and is a wholly-owned subsidiary of Bell Atlantic Cor- 
poration, a publicly held company with over one million 
shareowners. C&P is in the business of providing tele- 
phone service in, among other places, the City of Alex- 
andria, Virginia, and is a “common carrier” within the 
meaning of section 533(b) of the Cable Act. 

5. Plaintiff Bell Atlantic Video Services Company 
(“BVS”) is a corporation incorporated under the laws of 
the Commonwealth of Virginia and is a wholly-owned 
subsidiary of Bell Atlantic Corporation. BVS was incor- 
porated for the purpose of providing video programming 
in, among other places, the City of Alexandria and is 
affiliated with C&P within the meaning of section 533(b) 
of the Cable Act. 

6. Defendant United States of America is a party to 
this action. 

7. Defendant Federal Communications Commission 
(“FCC”) is a federal agency created by Congress pur- 
suant to the Communications Act of 1934 (47 U.S.C. 
§§ 151 et seg.) and is charged with enforcement of sec- 
tion 533(b) and other regulation of cable television pur- 
suant to 47 U.S.C. §§ 401 and 521 ef seq. 

8. Defendant William P. Barr, named in his official 
capacity as Attorney General of the United States of 
America, is charged with enforcement of section 533(b) 
pursuant to 47 U.S.C. § 401. 


Background 


9. In 1984, Congress passed the Cable Communica- 
tions Policy Act, 47 U.S.C. §§ 521 et seq. (“Cable Act”), 
which among other things prohibits telephone companies 
and their affiliates from providing video programming 
within the areas where they provide telephone service. 
Section 533(b) of the Cable Act provides in relevant part 
as follows: 


(b)(1) It shall be unlawful for any common car- 
rier, subject in whole or in part to subchapter II of 


9 


this chapter, to provide video programming directly 
to subscribers in its telephone service area, either 
directly or indirectly through an affiliate owned by, 
operated by, controlled by, or under common control 
with the common carrier. 

(2) It shall be unlawful for any common carrier, 
subject in whole or in part to subchapter II of this 
chapter, to provide channels of communications or 
pole line conduit space, or other rental arrangements, 
to any entity which is directly or indirectly owned by, 
operated by, controlled by, or under common con- 
trol with such common carrier, if such facilities or 
arrangement are to be used for, or in connection 
with, the provision of video programming directly to 
subscribers in the telephone service area of the com- 
mon carrier. 


10. The Cable Act defines “video programming” as 
“programming provided by, or generally considered com- 
parable to programming provided by, a television broad- 
cast station.” 47 U.S.C. § 522(16). 

11. Under section 533(b), it is unlawful for C&P and 
BVS, as affiliates, to provide video programming directly 
to subscribers in areas where C&P provides telephone 
service. It is also unlawful for C&P to provide channels 
of communications to BVS for use in provision of video 
programming in these areas. 


COUNT I 


SECTION 533(b) IS UNCONSTITUTIONAL ON 
ITS FACE UNDER THE FIRST AMENDMENT 


12. The allegations of paragraphs | through 11 are 
incorporated herein by reference. 

13. Video programming is a form of speech protected 
by the First Amendment of the United States Constitu- 
tion. Video programmers own, produce, select, assemble, 


10 


package, and distribute images, ideas, information, opin- 
ions, and entertainment. 

14. The video programming prohibition in section 
533(b) of the Cable Act is, on its face, an unconstitu- 
tional prior restraint on speech. 

15. The prohibition is, on its face, a straightforward 
ban on certain types of constitutionally protected expres- 
sion, The prohibition is directed at speech itself and there- 
fore requires strict scrutiny. 

16. Under the statutory definition of “video program- 
ming,” 47 U.S.C. § 522(16), the applicability of the re- 
striction necessarily turns on the content of telephone 
companies’ speech. Congress has required government 
Officials to decide whether telephone companies are pro- 
viding prohibited speech that is “generally considered 
comparable to” television broadcast programming or 
whether they are providing non-prohibited speech which 
may also involve video images. This statutorily required 
process of official authorization, therefore, involves an 
evaluation of the video images and the context in which 
they are to be presented; a consideration of the impact 
such video images will have upon the viewer; and a deter- 
mination whether such video images will be perceived by 
the general public as a substitute for the forms of video 
speech that have been provided by others over traditional 
cable and broadcasting facilities. All of the foregoing 
involves the government in an investigation of content. 
Consequently, the statutory prohibition is content-based 
and must be subject to strict scrutiny. 

17. Section 533(b) is a speaker-specific restriction on 
protected expression that singles out speech provided by 
telephone companies and imposes a special and targeted 
prohibition on that speech in particular. Strict scrutiny 
is therefore warranted. 

18. Section 533(b) is an audience-specific restriction 
on protected expression that prevents telephone companies 
from providing video programming only to subscribers 


11 


within their telephone service areas. Strict scrutiny is 
therefore warranted. 

19. Section 533(b) violates both the First Amend- 
ment right of telephone companies to provide video pro- 
gramming and the First Amendment right of subscribers 
to receive the video programming which plaintiffs and 
other telephone companies would provide. 

20. The government defendants, which bear the bur- 
den of justifying this infringement of First Amendment 
rights, cannot prove that section 533(b) is necessary to 
serve any compelling governmental interest or even any 
substantial or important governmental interest. 

21. The speech targeted by section 533(b)—television 
shows, movies, sports, news, and political reporting and 
opinions—is not unlawful. 

22. In enacting section 533(b) in 1984, Congress 
stated that it was seeking to prevent the development of 
“local media monopolies.” This objective has not been 
achieved. In 1992, in enacting the Cable Television Con- 
sumer Protection and Competition Act, Congress found 
that “[t]he cable industry has become highly concentrated. 
The potential effects of such concentration are barriers to 
entry for new programmers and a reduction in the number 
of media voices available to consumers.” Congress found 
that “[t]he average monthly cable rate has increased almost 
3 times as much as the Consumer Price Index” since 
1986. “[Mlost cable television subscribers have no oppor- 
tunity to select between competing cable systems. Without 
the presence of another multichannel video programming 
distributor, a cable system faces no local competition. The 
result is undue market power for the cable operator... .” 

23. The government defendants themselves have found 
that section 533(b) is now hindering the objectives it was 
originally intended to promote: 


a. In 1988, defendant FCC tentatively concluded that 
the prohibition “no longer serves the public interest.” In 


12 


1992, on a full administrative record, the FCC found that 
the prohibition should be repealed. Repealing the prohibi- 
tion would “increas[e] competition in the video market- 
place, spur[] the investment necessary to deploy an ad- 
vanced infrastructure, and increas[e] the diversity of serv- 
ices made available to the public.” Separately, the FCC 
reported to the President that introducing competition 
from telephone companies “would result in significant price 
savings for consumers and stimulate provision of a range 
of new information services. Current experience with 
head-to-head competition in cable markets indicates that 
rates would fall about 20% for the entire video package; 
if competition is introduced nationwide, subscribership 
would likely expand by 11.2 million households.” 

b. Defendant Barr in his official capacity as Attorney 
General stated that “[t]elevision viewers will be best served 
by removing barriers to entry by new firms into the video 
services marketplace. The Administration, therefore, sup- 
ports removal of current legislative prohibitions against 
telephone company provision of video programming, with 
appropriate safeguards.” The Department of Justice, act- 
ing under the supervision of defendant Barr, stated that 
telephone companies “should be allowed to own and di- 
rectly provide video programming” because telephone com- 
pany “provision of video programming will have pro- 
competitive benefits that outweigh any anticompetitive 
risks involved.” 

24. The defendants to this action further have con- 
cluded that any valid governmental concerns can be ad- 
dressed by means that do not prohibit telephone com- 
panies and their affiliates from video programming. 

25. Despite their concessions that the video program- 
ming prohibition is not necessary to any governmental 
interest, defendants have indicated that they will continue 
to enforce the prohibition. Consequently, there is an 
actual and justiciable controversy between the parties. 


13 


26. Plaintiffs, as well as consumers who are being 
denied the right to receive video programming which 
plaintiffs and other telephone companies would provide, 
have no adequate or complete remedy at law to redress 
the denial of their First Amendment rights other than by 
seeking the relief sought in this action. Unless defendants 
are enjoined from enforcing section 533(b), plaintiffs and 
consumers will continue to suffer substantial and irrepara- 
ble harm. 

COUNT Il 


SECTION 533(b) IS UNCONSTITUTIONAL AS 
APPLIED UNDER THE FIRST AMENDMENT 


27. The allegation of paragraphs | through 26 are 
incorporated herein by reference. 

28. If permitted to proceed, C&P and BVS will pro- 
vide video programming in the City of Alexandria, Vir- 
ginia. C&P will enhance its telephone network in Alex- 
andria to have the capability of carrying several hundred 
channels of video programming. In addition, C&P’s net- 
work will have the capability to deliver video programs 
on demand, which will allow consumers to select pro- 
grams from video libraries. C&P’s affiliate, BVS, will pro- 
vide some of the video programming on the network, and 
the network will also be available for use by other video 

mmers on a common carrier basis. 

29. Plaintiffs are ready, willing and, but for defend- 
ants’ enforcement of section 533(b), able to provide video 
programming to Alexandria. Consumers in Alexandria 
want to receive this programming. As a result of the 
defendants’ continued enforcement of section 533(b), both 
plaintiffs and consumers will continue to suffer substantial 
and irreparable harm. Plaintiffs have no adequate or com- 
plete remedy at law to redress the denial of First Amend- 
ment rights other than by seeking the relief sought in this 
action. 

30. Although section 533(b)(4) of the Cable Act pro- 
vides that the FCC may in particular circumstances waive 


_— 


14 


the video programming prohibition for “good cause,” this 
waiver provision is inadequate to protect the plaintiffs’ 
and consumers’ right to free speech and is itself a violation 
of the First Amendment. Congress provided no standards 
or time limits by which the FCC must grant or deny a 
“good cause” waiver, but instead admonished the FCC 
that this “waiver authority should be narrowly construed.” 
The FCC itself has not adopted any standards or time 
limits. As a result, the FCC has unbridled discretion in 
determining whether and when, if ever, a “good cause” 
waiver may be granted. 

31. As applied to the facts and circumstances of the 
video programming services that plaintiffs would provide 
in Alexandria, section 533(b) violates plaintiffs’ right of 
free speech under the First Amendment, as well as the 
First Amendment right of consumers in Alexandria to re- 
ceive plaintiffs’ video programming. 


COUNT Ill 


SECTION 533(b) VIOLATES EQUAL PROTECTION 
BOTH ON ITS FACE AND AS APPLIED 


32. The allegations of paragraphs | through 31 are 
incorporated herein by reference. 

33. Section 533(b) is a legislative classification that 
interferes with the plaintiffs’ exercise of the fundamental 
right of freedom of speech. Section 533(b) discriminates 
against telephone companies that wish to provide video 
programming while permitting other, similarly situated en- 
tities to engage in this activity. 

34. Section 533(b) does not apply at all to other 
regulated utility companies, including utilities owning poles 
and conduits, that are permitted to provide video program- 
ming in their service areas. 

35. Section 533(b) also does not apply to the cable 
TV companies which Congress found have “undue market 
power.” As interpreted by the FCC, section 533(b) 


f 


15 


applies only in one direction: against telephone companies 
that seek to provide video programming, and not against 
cable TV companies that seek to provide telephone service. 
Cable TV companies and their affiliates are taking ad- 
vantage of this one-sided prohibition by providing long 
distance telephone services, cellular radio services, per- 
sona! communications services, competitive access services, 
aad other telephone services in the same areas where they 

36. Section 533(b)’s discrimination against telephone 
companies is not necessary to serve any compelling gov- 
ernmental interest. 

37. Section 533(b) violates the equal protection prin- 
ciples embodied in the Fifth Amendment to the United 
States Constitution, both on its face and as applied to 
the video programming that plaintiffs would provide in 
Alexandria. 

38. Plaintiffs have no adequate or complete remedy 
at law to redress the denial of their Fifth Amendment 
rights other than by seeking the relief sought in this 
action. Unless the defendants are enjoined from enforc- 
ing section 533(b), plaintiffs will continue to suffer sub- 
stantial and irreparable harm. 


COUNT IV 


SECTION 533(b) VIOLATES PLAINTIFFS’ RIGHTS 
TO USE THEIR PROPERTY FOR EXPRESSION 


39. The allegations of paragraphs 1 through 38 are 
incorporated herein by reference. 

40. Plaintiffs propose to use the existing C&P tele- 
phone network, together with certain enhancements, to 
provide video programming in Alexandria. 

41. Section 533(b) violates the rights of plaintiffs to 
harness their property's potential for constitutionally pro- 
tected expression, in violation of both the First and Fifth 
Amendments. 


16 


PRAYER FOR RELIEF 


WHEREFORE, plaintiffs pray that the Court enter 
an Order: 


1. Declaring that section 533(b) of the Cable Act 
is unconstitutional, void, and unenforceable on its face 
as a violation of the right to free speech guaranteed by 
the First Amendment to the United States Constitution; 

2. Declaring that section 533(b) of the Cable Act 
is unconstitutional, void, and unenforceable under the 
First Amendment to the United States Constitution as 
applied to plaintiffs’ provision of video programming in 
Alexandria; 

3. Declaring that section 533(b) of the Cable Act is 
unconstitutional, void, and unenforceable as a violation 
of the right to the equal protection of the laws under the 
Fifth Amendment to the United States Constitution; 

4. Declaring that section 533(b) of the Cable Act is 
unconstitutional, void, and unenforceable under the First 
and Fifth Amendments to the United States Constitution 
as a violation of plaintiffs’ rights to use their propery 
for expression; 

5. Permanently enjoining defendants from enforcing 
section 533(b) of the Cable Act in any criminal, civil, 
or administrative proceedings; and 

6. Granting plaintiffs such further relief as the Court 
may deem just and equitable.

FCC POLICY ON CABLE OWNERSHIP: 
A STAFF REPORT 


CONCURRING STATEMENT 
of 
COMMISSIONER JOSEPH R. FOGARTY 


In Re: Issuance for Public Comment of FCC Staff Re- 
port on Cable Cross-Ownership Policies 


I concur in the Commisison’s decision ordering that 
the Report of the FCC Office of Plans and Policy staff on 
cable cross-ownership policies be issued for public com- 
ment. It should be emphasized, however, that the staff 
report is just that—a staff report—and at this point does 
not enjoy Commission approval. 

In my view, the staff report presents a decidedly mixed 
bag of policy assessment and pronouncement. I have the 
same fundamental difficulty with this report that I had 
with its predecessor on Direct Broadcast Satellite policy: * 
proposed finding of abundant program diversity and as- 
sumptions of “workably competitive” markets in video 
technology and services must be supported by pragmatic 
fact as well as general economic theory. In finding that a 
“workably competitive” video media market now exists, 
the staff report’s analysis lumps existing broadcast out- 
lets and cable systems together with subscription tele- 
vision (STV), muti-point distribution systems (MDS), 
direct broadcast satellites (DBS), low power television, 
VHF drop-ins, video cassettes and video discs. It is far 
from clear, however, that cable television service is inter- 
changeable with over-the-air broadcast television service. 
This substitutability issue is critical to the validity of the 


1 Notice of Inquiry into the Development of Regulation Policy 
in regard to Direct Broadcast Satellites for the Period following 
the 1983 Regional Administrative Radio Conference, Docket 80- 
603, 45 F.R. 72719 (November 3, 1980), Separate Statement of 
Commissioner Joseph R. Fogarty. 


25 


staff report’s policy recommendations and warrants full 
public comment. It also appear to me to be little more 
than wishful thinking to predicate a finding of “workable 
competition” on new technology and services (e.g., DBS, 
Low Power TV, and fledgling STV and MDS) which are 
more on the horizon than with us here and now. In the 
final analysis, this Commisison must have more than 
merely the assumption of a “workably competitive” mar- 
ketplace as the basis for policy making. 

In addressing the specific issues of broadcast television 
station-and network-cable cross-ownership, the staff report 
does make certain observations which deserve serious con- 
sideration. The report may be correct when it suggests 
that such cross-ownership may in fact increase diversity 
in both economic and First Amendment terms. In this 
regard, I agree that while a cable system could control 
a substantial share of the total video outlets in a com- 
munity, the relevant policy question is not “How would 
a cross-owned system behave,” but “Would its perform- 
ance or that of the co-owned broadcast station deteriorate 
under cross-ownership?” It may be, as the staff report 
suggests, that natural profit-making and investment incen- 
tives, as well as oversight and pressure from the local 
franchising authority, make it unlikely that cross-owned 
systems would be any less dedicated to the fullest possible 
development of cable’s potential. 

I also think that the staff report is excellent in its dis- 
cussion of the benefits of vertical integration which may 
be lost to consumers as a result of overly-restrictive Com- 
mission ownership rule and policy.* The price to be 


? While it is refreshing to see this acknowledgment of the bene- 
fits of vertical integration in the context of an assessment of cable 
ownership policies, it is regretable that this sensitivity has not 
been more manifest in other areas of Commission “dereculatory” 
endeavor. See Computer Inquiry I], Final Decision. Statement of 
Commissioner Joseph R. Fogarty, Dissenting in Part. 77 FCC 2d 
504, 508-511 (1980). The degree of structural separation prescribed 
by the Final Decision for the dominant carrier subsidiary’s pro- 


26 


paid for a reflexive anti-cross-ownership policy may be 
not only diminished diversity but no service at all.’ In 
the final analysis, it is the result, and not the theory, 
that counts. 

While the staff report purports to be responsive to 
these pragmatic principles in its recommendations regard- 
ing broadcast television station-and network-cable cross- 
ownership, it unfortunately balks at the consistent appli- 
cation of those principles to the telephone company-cable 
cross-ownership issue. Although the report acknowledges 
that considerable benefits of vertical integration would 
inhere in such cross-ownership, it nonetheless argues for 
retention of the existing prohibition based on the assump 
tion that cable may become a direct competitor of the 
telephone company’s local loop and local exchange serv- 
ices. While the report concedes that this competitive 
scenario is speculative, it insists that rescission of the 
cross-ownership prohibition would be “irreversible” in 
terms of forfeiting whatever benefits of inter-modal com- 


vision of customer premises equipment and enhanced services re- 
flected an analysis in which 


“all assumptions pertaining to the benefits of separation are 
treated as givens whereas any countervailing arguments per- 
taining to the benefits of vertical integration are treated as 
unproven hypotheses subject to considerable doubt.” 


Id. at 508. 


* This observation has proved all too true with regard to the 
impact of the cable-telephone company cross-ownership prohibition 
on the provision of cable service to rural America. While the 
Commission has now adopted an outright exemption from the 
cross-ownership ban for telephone company rural cable service, 
Elimination of the Telephone Company Cable Television Cross- 
Ownership Rules for Rural Areas, ——— FCC 2d ——— (Adopted 
Nov. 5, 1981), cable service that could have been provided to rural 
America by telephone companies was long denied in favor of 
protecting a vague future possibility of service from independent 
operators. See, id., Separate Statement of Commissioner Joseph 
R. Fogarty and other Statements cited at n. 1 supra. 


27 


petition might occur as a result of future independent 
cable system development. 

I have previously stated my opposition to the telephone 
company-cable cross-ownership prohibition‘ and will not 
repeat those views in full detail here. However, I think 
the line of argument in the staff report borders on halluci- 
nation. It is inanely wishful thinking to suggest that in 
the foreseeable future independent cable system operators 
will install the switching equipment and capacity neces- 
sary to duplicate and compete with the local telephone 
exchange. In terms of either economics or technological 
demand and investment, the staff report's scenario is sheer 
fantasy. 

The staff report’s “irreversibility” argument is even 
more troublesome for sound analysis and policy. The 
decision which will be “irreversible” here—and pernici- 
ously so—is the one recommended by the staff: the con- 
tinued and perhaps permanent foreclosure of telephone 
company participation and competition in the broadband 
telecommunications marketplace. Indeed, continuation of 
the prohibition on telephone company-cable  cross- 
ownership will seriously retard, if not completely pre- 
clude, the introduction and deployment of broadband. 
fiber-optic technology in the local telephone exchange. 
It will be the American consumer who will suffer the 
loss of telecommunications benefits directly attributable 
to this myopic policy recommendation. 

New and evolving fiber optic telecommunications tech- 
nology offers the potential for economic replacement of 
copper wire with glass fiber as the transmission medium 
in the local loop of the telephone exchange. This poten- 
tial is enhanced as research and development continues 
to lower both the cost and signal attenuation of fiber

FCC POLICY ON 
CABLE OWNERSHIP 


A Staff Report by 


KENNETH GORDON 
JONATHAN D. Levy 
ROBERT S. PREECE 


November 1981 


FEDERAL COMMUNICATIONS COMMISSION 
OFFICE OF PLANS AND PoLicy 
WASHINGTON, D.C. 20554 


32 
Part I 


Chapter 1 
Introduction and Summary of Conclusions 


A. Origin and Scope of The Study 


In October 1980 the Federal Communications Com- 
mission directed its staff to study the Commission’s rules 
on ownership of cable systems.’ Five issues were to be 
examined.” The first was the rule barring the cross- 
ownership of cable systems by a telephone company serv- 
ing the same area.* This investigation considers cable- 
telco crossownership generally and goes beyond other 
recent Commission investigations and rulemakings on 
crossownership.* The second issue was whether to allow 
the ownership of many local cable systems by the same 
company. Multiple system operators (MSO’s) are a prom- 
inent feature of the cable industry.’ Currently, the Com- 
mission has no rules limiting the number of systems an 
MSO may operate. The third issue was whether cross- 
ownership between television stations and cable systems 
within the Grade B broadcast contour should be per- 


1 See FCC News Release October 22, 1980. The proceeding from 
which this study emanate is CC Docket No. 78-219. The instant 
concern of the Commissiou was with waiver policy standards for 
rural cable-telephone company cross ownership (see 47 CFR 63.55). 
Since the rural issue is being examined elsewhere in the Commis- 
sion (CC Docket 78-219) we do not cover it in this study. 


2 Since that time questions have also arisen about the desira- 
bility of foreign ownership of cable. An examination of the issues 
presented in foreign ownership is under way, and its conclusions 
will be available to the Commission in the fall of 1981. 


'47 CFR 63.54. 
*CC Docket 78-219. 
5 See Chapter 6, infra. 


33 


mitted.* The fourth issue was the prohibition against 
cable ownership by the major commercial networks.’ The 
fifth issue was “separations,” i.e., whether cable operators 


corresponding ; 
Part II of the study provides information important for 
an understanding of each of the ownership issues ex- 
amined. Therefore Chapters 2 and 3 are intended to be 


7 Ibid. 


* Separations policy is indirectly related. See Report to the 
President (supra, n. 7) for the argument that at least some of the 
media crossownership prohibitions are uunecessary in the presence 
of a separations policy. (Recommendation 3. at 31-33.) Thie con 
clusion is not extended to telephone companies however (See 
Recommendation 4, at 33-34 


34 


read in conjunction with any or all of the chapters that 
deal with the specific ownership issues in Part Ill. 

Chapter 2 contains background on the cable industry. 
The basic technical characteristics of cable are outlined 
and recent developments likely to affect cable’s “capa- 
bilities” are reviewed. A brief historical survey of the 
industry, including the effects of regulation on cable de- 
velopment, is provided. Finally, the chapter examines in 
some detail the various markets that cable serves or has 
the potential to serve, and identifies the sources and 
nature of competition in these markets. Some discussion 
of related markets, such as programming supply, that 
might be affected by cable ownership rules is included. 

Chapter 3 presents general issues about ownership rules 
or a separations requirement for cable. The meaning of 
consumer welfare, in various dimensions, is explored in 
an attempt to provide an understanding of the benefits 
and costs of the present rules and of proposed changes 
in those rules. More specifically, the chapter begins by 
examining the economic aspects of consumer welfare, in- 
cluding the question of what forces determine the services 
provided by cable, how they are produced, as well as how 
they are distributed—that is—who gets them. Paralleling 
the economic concerns are considerations of First Amend- 
ment rights and their relation to cable access. The latter 
issue is also analyzed in Chapter 3 and is linked to the 
economic aspects of ownership policy. 

Part III of the report contains the chapters that analyze 
each of the five issues and provide support for specific 
policy recommendations. When read in conjunction with 
Part II of the report, each chapter may be used by 
itself. 

Chapter 4 covers the broadcast-cable crossownership 
prohibition. Chapter 5 examines the growth of multiple 
system operators (MSO’s) to determine whether or not 
the growth of large firms within the cable industry itself 


35 


presents problems that the Commission should address. 
Chapter 6 covers the crossownership issue for the three 
commercial television broadcast networks. 

Chapter 7 examines the advantages and disadvantages 
of a separations policy for cable. Although separations 
(severing the link between the ownership and operation 
of a cable system and the determination of what services 
it carries) is not an ownership policy as such, proponents 
have argued it may eliminate some of the problems asso- 
ciated with crossownership. 

Finally, Chapter 8 examines the telephone-cable cross- 
ownership problem. A number of special features, in- 
cluding rate of return regulation, ownership of pole and 
conduit space, and a local switched service monopoly 
position characterize the telephone system and raise prob- 
lems not shared by other cases of crossownership. 

Throughout, the report attempts to minimize the use 
of technical economic terms and arguments. Except where 
absolutely central to the discussion, such arguments have 
been placed in the footnotes or, in cases where the treat- 
ment is both technical and extended, in an appendix. 


C. Summary of Conclusions 


We find that the underlying goals of the Commis- 
sion’s ownership policies—the promotion of competition 
in the economic marketplace and in the marketplace of 
ideas—are met when a wide range of media, both video 
and non-video, is available. Our analysis suggests that 
the market for video programming and many of the other 
services that cable can provide is workably competitive 
and in most cases not susceptible to monopolization. We 
see little danger arising from horizontal control, either 


© See Chapter 3 for a discussion of this term. The Commission 
has, from time to time, found it relevant to assess the competi- 
tiveness of submarkets such as video program delivery or radio 
services. 


36 


in local video markets or in national markets (i.e., the 
franchising and programming markets) associated with 
cable." Therefore, we believe that in general a policy 
extending free entry into cable to all interested firms is 
most likely to contribute to a rapidly growing, technolog- 
ically dynamic industry that meets consumers’ needs. Con- 
sumers’ preferences can best determine what cable firms 
provide, and the incentives of entrepreneurs are more 
likely to shape an efficient industry structure than a pre- 
determination by government policy-makers. Changes in 
the structure of the mass communications industry since 
the cable ownership rules were adopted have removed 
whatever need the Commission once saw for such rules. 
These changes will have even more impact in the future. 
Therefore, we make the following specific recommenda- 
tions: 
(1) The rule that prohibits broadcasters from own- 
ing cable systems in their own areas should be 
rescinded.” 


(2) The provisions in that rule that prohibit cross- 
ownership of cable by the major commercial 
networks should also be dropped.” 


(3) The Commission should not place any limita- 
tions on the number of cable systems a multiple 


system operator May Own or on the number 
of subscribers it may serve. 


We are aware that some cases of market power may 
exist or that unforeseen problems may arise in the future. 


11 Horizontal control refers to the ability to directly affect the 
price and other terms of exchange in a particular market. 


1247 CFR 76.501. See Chapter 4. 
13 Jbid. See Chapter 6. 


14 At the current time there is no rule on how many local cable 


systems a single firm may control. The issue is discussed in Chap- 
ter 5. 


37 


These do not appear to be so numerous, so comprehen- 
sive, or so likely that either comprehensive prohibitions 
such as the cross ownership rules, or flat numerical re- 
strictions on ownership, are desirable. Rather, reliance 
on enforcement of the anti-trust laws can remedy those 
few situations that are unacceptably lacking in competi- 
tion. In this fashion the cable industry will be free to 
Shape itself in the patterns that best meet the differing 
needs of individual markets, and consumers will obtain 
the benefits of an efficient industry organization. 

Because most of the markets in which cable operates 
are workably competitive,“ because the cable industry 
is still in a stage of rapid growth and experimentation, 
and because cable entrepreneurs have strong incen- 
tives to respond to and provide for the overall needs 
of cable consumers, we conclude that cable operators 
should retain the Primary responsibility for determining 
what cable programming and other services should be 
available on their systems. Therefore: 


(4) We recommend that the Commission not enact 
a separations requirement for cable operators 
in most circumstances. An exception to this 
conclusion would arise in the event of telephone 
company ownership, and is discussed below." 


Cable operators do not now exercise total control over 
what is carried over their facilities. FCC rules mandate 
carriage of all significantly viewed local broadcast signals, 
and local franchise authorities can and do require cable 
operators to provide a wide variety of facilities, services 
and program options as a condition of the franchise. 
Even more important than these requirements are the 
profitmaking pressures on the operator for providing a 


© This term is defined in Chapter 8, at page 33. See n. 9. 


16 See recommendation (5) on page 8. The general issue is 
expored in Chapter 7. 


38 


high quality, diverse, and reasonably priced set of offer- 
ings to consumers. 

Revisiting the separations question would be appropri- 
ate should cable become the means by which of a sub- 
stantial majority of communications services is provided. 
At present a monopoly outcome seems far from certain. 
Therefore, we recommend no general separations policy. 

Finally, we believe that, for the most part, the same 
conclusions about TV markets reached in connection with 
broadcasters, networks and MSO’s apply to telephone 
ownership and operation of cable systems. But in the 
market for non-video services, the potential for effective 
competition is less imminent. 

Elimination of the cable-telephone crossownership ban 
before there is some likelihood of competition (or at least 
potential competition) for local loop services would seri- 
ously limit consumer alternatives.”” While cellular, XTEN, 
and perhaps other technologies offer potential completion 
in local services, their future roles remain uncertain. In 
addition, cable crossownership now may add significantly 
to the difficulty of achieving cost based telephone rates. 
We would like to stress that these problems are a conse- 
quence of the exclusive franchise, rate base regulated 
nature of the telephone industry and do not stem from 
cable. 

Although the objective of deregulation is to allow the 
marketplace to provide the services desired by consumers, 
this goal requires freedom of entry by potential suppliers. 
In most local telephone and cable markets, free entry 
does not now exist. Therefore deregulation of crossown- 
ership is not yet possible. We do believe, however, that 
as long as the services people desire are provided at rea- 
sonable cost, it should not be the role of government to 
select the organization that provides them. Thus the 
crossownership ban may not always be necessary. Two 


17 The term “local loop” refers to the pair of copper wires that 
connects each telephone subscriber to the central office or switch. 


39 


developments could ease the problem: (1) competition 
for the local loop could arise from new technologies or 
(2) as narrowband cable technology stabilizes it may 
become possible to insure access to, and competition in 
that narrowband technology. In these circumstances cross- 
owned systems may become feasible. Chapter 8 describes 
such a regime (separations), and explains why it cannot 
be relied upon to protect competition given the present 
state of technological knowledge. 

We are mindful, however, of the expertise the telephone 
company may be able to bring to cable development. If 
the crossownership ban were to retard technological change 
in either industry, the cost to consumers might be high 
over the long run. Therefore, the Commission should be 
flexible in granting waivers from the crossownership ban 
whenever either a telephone or a cable company proposes 
an experiment that requires closely integrated cable- 
telephone operations. 

Finally, leaseback arrangements, which are currently 
available, but not extensively used, could provide a route 
for taking advantage of any economies resulting from 
joint operations. 

In sum we further recommend that the Commission: 


(5) Retain the basic features of the current cable- 
telephone crossownership ban, 


(6) expand and encourage the use of leaseback 
arrangements to take advantage of whatever 
joint economies may be available, 


(7) expand and encourage the use of waivers of the 
rule when applicants propose innovative cable 
or telephone technologies that require joint 
operation, and 


(8) continue to monitor both competitive and tech- 


nological developments that may make possible 
ending the ban entirely.

Chapter 8 
Telephone Ownership of Cable 


A. Introduction 


Telephone companies and cable systems both provide 
electronic information services over wire. Many services 
can be provided by either one. With adequate switching 
and consumer premises equipment cable companies can 
do just about anything that a telephone company can. 
With some changes in telephone systems, telephone com- 
panies can do virtually anything that a cable company 
can.’ The potential for competition with the local tele- 
phone monopoly is central to arguments to ban cross- 
ownership of cable and telephone facilities and joint oper- 
ation of these facilities.” 

To encourage flexibility in the provision of new services 
and to protect cable interests, the Commission has ruled 
that a local telephone company may not operate a cable 
system in its service area. Other reasons for the ban 
that were cited by the Commission in its crossownership 
prohibition are the possible cross-subsidy between cable

Segregation of digital carrier systems can allow the telephone 
company to provide high speed data and even video transmission 
over existing local loop between cable and plant telephone facili- 
ties. (See “New technique could allow video transmission over 
phone lines,” Bell Laboratories Record, 503-503, October 1980.) 
New telephone technologies, especially local use of fiber optics may 
eliminate any remaining distinctions.

provision of local service. These include private microwave 
(XTEN), cellular radio, satellite, and others. Whether these serv- 
ices become competitive depends on cost considerations and may 
also be affected by Commission decisions.

and telephone services; the argument that cable is an “in- 
fant industry,” which can develop fully only if protected 
from telephone control; and the problems rising [sic] from 
telephone companies’ ability to deny access to independ- 
ent cable systems through control over poles and con- 
duits.* The prohibition on crossownership is waived only 
in cases where cable service could not otherwise be pro- 
vided, or where “other good cause”* can be shown. 

Because of the high fixed costs and low variable costs 
of cable provision, cable facilities are likely to be provided 
by a single firm. This situation is reinforced by state and 
local franchise regulations. Therefore, if telephone owner- 
ship of cable facilities becomes prevalent, it is unlikely 
that a competitive cable system will develop. While there 
are a number of competing sources of entertainment broad- 
casting,” there are currently few substitutes for local tele- 
phone service.

or reduced by the development of the cable industry and by legis-

is no longer appropriate to think of it as an infant. Likewise, pole 
attachment legislation has given independent cable operators re- 
course if the telephone company is limiting access to poles at rea- 
sonable rates.

are defined as satisfying the “other good cause” criterion in the

second, if cable does not develop these two-way services (i.e., if 
it does not become an actual or viable potential competitor with the 
telephone company in basic telephone services), many prospective 
benefits arising from the ban will not develop.

Section B of this Chapter discusses the advantages of 
crossownership. Section C examines the disadvantages 
of crossownership. Section D considers several alternative 
regulatory policies including (1) dropping the ban upon 
appropriate state regulation; (2) a regulatory approach 
that relies on cost manual type cost allocation and on a 
“partial separations” policy for telephone company owned 
cable systems; and (3) maintaining the present ban. We 
conclude that the ban must be retained for the time 
being. 

The Commission now bans crossownership and opera- 
tions between CATV systems and telephone companies 
in the same service area.* Telephone companies however, 
are allowed to own and operate cable systems outside of 
their own service areas and may also own cable facilities 
if they are leased back to an independent cable system.’

Finally, allowing telephone companies to compete in-

and telephone entities. Far more important advantages 
may result if crossownership leads to technological ad-

crossownership has very high costs if its stifles technologi-

Crossownership could either limit or encourage the de-

If crossownership significantly accelerated the develop-

powerful argument in favor of dropping the ban. Con-

strong reason for retaining the ban. A prediction of the 
direction of this effect would be valuable, but such predic- 
tions are difficult to make, and crossownership is likely 
to affect technological advances in more than one way. 

A crossowned system might have more incentives to 
innovate because it would be able to utilize fully any 
new techniques developed in either service.” Independ- 
ent provision might mean that technological advances 
would be under-utilized or even inapplicable (because, 
for example, these advances rely on a “one-wire” type 
system or might have to be shared with another firm). 

The counter-argument is that there will be less innova- 
tion if competitive pressures do not compel the firm to 
seek lower costs and better techniques.”" 

Evidence on the conditions that foster technological 
progress in other industries is ambiguous.” Moreover, 
the evidence that is available does not indicate how local 
market dominance will affect innovation in the presence 
of nationwide competition for those local services.

4. Conclusion 


There are some potential advantages to joint owner- 
ship of telephone and cable facilities. At a minimum, the 
degree of cooperation and coordination between these 
services will be higher where they are operated jointly. 
Moreover, joint ownership may encourage discovery or 
use of new techniques that reduce the costs of service.” 

Our discussion of the potential advantages of cross- 
ownership is necessarily somewhat theoretical. There are 
three reasons for this. First, the crossownership prohibi- 
tion has been strictly enforced;™ thus the data on pos- 
sible economies in urban areas are severely limited. Sec- 
ond, there have been dramatic changes in the nature of 
the cable business over the past several years. Third, 
the existing cost advantages or disadvantages of the tele-

munications News, September 1978, pp. 72-78 and R. Kachulak, 
“A Rural Integrated Distribution Trial with Fiber Optics,” /EEE

dynamic cost economies that interaction between cable 
and telephone service may be able to offer. While the 
economies from joint construction may be small where 
the telephone plant is already constructed, in the future, 
whenever new telephone facilities are needed or old facili- 
ties must be replaced, far more substantial economies 
may be realized. 

Cost economies need not be proven as a condition for 
crossownership. Rather, the market should be relied on 
unless this will lead to problems that cannot be dealt 
with without government interference. The problems that 
crossownership may entail, and suggested means for deal- 
ing with them, are the subjects of the following sections. 

As we argued above, there is a presumption that firms 
should not be prevented from entering markets. Those 
who wish to argue against crossownership should be ex- 
pected to make a strong case. The fact that economies 
of joint operation may generally be low is not a sufficient 
argument to ban crossownership. Nevertheless, the fol- 
lowing section argues that there are major problems asso- 
ciated with unrestricted crossownership. 


C. Disadvantages of Crossownership 


1. Introduction. 


The disadvantages of crossownership largely derive 
from the fact that telephone companies are regulated ex- 
clusive franchise monopolists in their local area. These 
disadvantages can be classified into four groups. 

First, crossownership may allow a telephone company 
partially to avoid rate-of-return regulation on its tele- 
phone service. It can do so by attributing costs to the 
regulated telephone division and revenues to an unregu- 
lated cable division.” 


26 J. B. Duvall and M. D. Pelcovits discuss this problem in the 
case of private line telecommunications services, which they argue 


53 


Second, cable service can compete with telephone serv- 
ices in the areas of alarm systems, data transmission, 
meter reading, and even plain old telephone service. 
Telephone companies may be able to forestall this facili- 
ties based competition in two-way communications by 
owning the cable system. Crossownership or cooperative 
operations will also allow joint-monopoly profit maximiza- 
tion. 

Third, the size of some telephone companies relative 
to the cable industry could allow one or two telephone 
companies to gain a very large share of the national 
market in cable.™ 


can be deregulated. (“Reforming Regulatory Policy for Private 
Lines Telecommunications Services: Implications for Market Per- 
formance”, Office of Plans and Policy Working Paper Number 4, 
December 1980.) The problem is considerably smaller in that case 
since private line service is a nationwide telephone offering, and 
since there exists an interim cost allocation manual to prevent 
this type of cost-shifting. A major policy difference between the 
use of cost allocation for deregulation of private line services and 
for control of cross-owned cable systems is that private line serv- 
ices are already provided with joint or common facilities. This is 
not the case with cable facilities, but might become the case if 
crossownership is allowed. 


27 Joint-monopoly profit is the profit earned by two firms acting 
as a single monopolist. Such cooperation is likely to lead to greater 
monopoly profits than would competitive behavior. This joint-profit 
maximization is constrained. Alarm systems can be independent 
of both cable, and telephone; XTEN type service or direct satellite 
service like that provided by SBS may eventually offer reasonable 
substitutes for phone or cable data services. Nevertheless, the use 
of a satellite for local transmission is not likely to be effective 
or cheap. Interactive alarm systems are likely to be more reliable 
than are stand-alone systems, and electronic meter reading is 
likely to be cheaper than having meter readers check meters 
several times a day. Therefore some market power is likely to 
remain. 


28 See Comments of the National Cable Television Association, 
Inc. in CC Docket 80-767 (at. 34-39) (1981). 


54 


Fourth, telephone companies control substantial con- 
duit and pole space. Unrestricted control over cable ac- 
cess can give the telephone company a powerful lever 
to gain control of the cable system or to charge mo- 
nopolistic rates for access. Rate or pole price regulation 
can limit exploitation of telephone company monopoly 
power unless crossownership is allowed.” 


2. The Allocation of Costs 


Effective rate-of-return regulation may make it profit- 
able for telephone companies to undertake enterprises 
that would be unprofitable in the absence of regulation. 
If telephone companies arbitrarily allocate costs to regu- 
lated facilities and revenues to unregulated facilities, they 
may partially avoid the rate-of-return limitations that 
regulation attempts to impose. If the telephone company 
bases a large part of its behavior on avoiding regulation, 
the most efficient organization of production is unlikely. 


Telephone regulation determines an allowable rate of 
return and permits the telephone company to earn this 
return on allowed capital, after allowed expenses. As 
long as regulation effectively limits the telephone com- 
pany’s rate of return, the company has an incentive to 
attribute costs to the regulated sector. If cable cross- 
ownership allows regulated telephone companies to shift 


2° Rate regulation gives two motives for crossownership. First, 
even if the industries were non-competitive, crossownership may 
enable the telephone company to attribute cable costs to the tele- 
phone company rate base or telephone companies revenues to the 
cable subsidiary. Second, if the cable system and the telephone 
companies are natural competitors, crossownership allows joint 
profit maximization and joint monopoly pricing. Because the tele- 
phone company supplies a vital commodity to the cable ( pole at- 
tachments), it could charge a high enough price to absorb most 
of the cable profits itself and force the cable to charge the joint 
monopolistic prices to be able to pay for the input. Since it is 
rate regulated, the telephone company can keep these profits only if 
they can be attributed to a cable subsidiary. 


55 


costs, a powerful incentive toward crossownership 
(stronger than and in addition to the incentive to in- 
crease capital discussed by Averch and Johnson™) is 
created.” 


* H. Averch and L. Johnson “Behavior of the Firm Under Regu- 
latory Constraint”, 52 American Economic Rev. 1058-69 (Decem- 
ber 1962). Averch and Johnson discuss distortions caused by rate 
regulation over the entire entity. Thus, if a telephone company 
can claim certain capital expenditures as part of the rate base, 
it may wish to undertake this investment even if it adds nothing 
to sales or to the attractiveness of the output. When part of the 
entity escapes regulation, any costs generated in the unregulated 
sector that can be shifted to the regulated sector represent pure 
profits. 


*! Assume, for example, that both a telephone company and a 
cable system can be supported by a community, that the capital 
stock of the telephone company is $100 million, and that the capital 
cost of a cable company is $50 million. Make the further simpli- 
fying assumption that operating costs are zero for both the tele- 
phone and cable operation (all costs are capital costs), that the 
allowed rate of return is 10 percent, and that a protected telephone 
monopolist would earn 20 percent if rate regulation were totally 
ineffective. In this example, each dollar of cable costs that can 
be attributed to the telephone system will justify a ten cent per 
year increase in allowed telephone revenue, up to the monopoly 
level. If all cable costs can be attributed to the telephone system, 
but all cable revenues can still be attributed to the cable sector, 
the telephone company could raise its telephone price by 50 per- 
cent and earn a 15 percent real rate of return on actual telephone 
capital. This assumes that the regulatory commission is willing 
to allow rate increases and that it believes the telephone company’s 
attribution of cable facility costs to the telephone company rate 
base. If the regulatory commission refuses to grant rate increases 
for political reasons, the incentives for misallocation of costs are 
reduced. Such a regulatory policy is of dubious legal or economic 
value since it may preclude new investment and because it may 
not allow the recovery of real costs. Depending on the ability of 
the telephone company to allocate cable costs to telephone opera- 
tions, very significant increases in apparent telephone revenue 
requirements may be possible without any change in the underly- 
ing costs. When costs can be shifted, the value of the cable sys- 
tem to the telephone company is substantially greater than it would 


56 


When costs can be shifted, rate of return regulation 
may make joint ownership of cable and telephone facili- 
ties profitable to the telephone company even if joint pro- 
vision of cable and telephone service is more costly than 
independent provision. 

This rationale for a crossownership ban is dependent 
on the assumption that rate of return regulation has held 
telephone rates below the monopoly level (though not 
necessarily below the level that could result from compe- 
tition and contestability), and that the regulators respond 
to higher costs by allowing higher rates. If regulators 
ignore reported costs and set rates on other grounds (such 
as a simple inflation adjustment to historical rates), the 
incentive to shift costs would likewise vanish, although 
other problems would develop.” 

Current Commission regulation limits crossownership 
only within the telephone service area.” As long as costs 
can be attributed geographically, the current ban appears 
sufficient to eliminate this danger of crossownership.™

3. Potential Competition 


Cable and telephone suppliers can already compete in the 
provision of many two-way services. Where cable is inde- 


88 Pelcovits and Duvall make this argument. (See n. 25 supra). 
They are discussing an already regulated service. It might be

pendently owned, the threat of cable entry into two-way 
markets may restrict the ability of the telephone company 
to behave as a monopolist. If the facilities were jointly 
owned, this constraint would not exist. Telephone com- 
panies have other actual competitors and potential com- 
petitors; nevertheless, cable has more potential to provide 
telephone-like services in the near future than does any 
other carrier. 

Cable systems provide competition with the local tele- 
phone company both in two-way services and in access 
to the increasingly competitive interstate telecommunica- 
tions market. In some cities, for example, Satellite Busi- 
ness Systems (SBS) proposes to rely on cable facilities 
for origination and termination of some of its transmis- 
sions.** Without competitive access to the local consumer, 
the potential for interstate competition is limited.” 

The problems of cost-shifting are present in any tele- 
phone company expansion into service areas; in the cable 
area the likelihood that potential competitors will be elim- 
inated is even more serious. Cost-shifting is a result of 
monopoly and of partially effective regulatory control. If 
potential competition or actual competition is prevalent, 
cost-shifting will be highly limited. 

Cable systems are not the only possible competitors for 
local loop type service. The appearance of other entrants 
would reduce the need for cable competition to constrain 
telephone company market power. Some argue that com- 
petition in local loop facilities could develop rapidly, 


*1 See M/A-Com, Inc., Annual Report at 14-16 (1980). See also 
“Belt-Tightening Gotham to Use Manhattan Cable for Data Send- 
ing,” Variety at 42 (July 8, 1981). 


“In this case, the difference in potential damages from high 
levels of AT&T involvement in the provision of cable services (as 
opposed to other telephone companies) becomes important. AT&T 
is the dominant carrier in interstate carriage as well as in local 
service. Because of this, it has more reason to wish to monopolize 


local loop services than would an independent telephone company. 


60 


and that the traditional bottleneck could be circumvented.“ 
If new technologies fulfill their promise, the need for 
ownership restrictions may be eliminated. Nevertheless, 
at present the ability of these new technologies to provide 
competition with the full range of telephone services, if 
they develop at all, remains largely speculative. Cable 
has more immediate prospects for providing some com- 
petitive services, and long run prospects for eliminating 
the “local bottleneck” altogether.* The local monopoly 
held by telephone companies and the ability of cable to 
provide similar services, not any special attributes of cable 
entertainment, makes telephone control over the cable 
medium undesirable. 


4. Special Issues Involving AT&T 


Much of the opposition to cable-telephone crossowner- 
ship is motivated by a fear of AT&T.“ AT&T serves 
most of the larger metropolitan areas; these areas have 
become most attractive for cable systems. AT&T’s sheer 
size, its nationwide operations, and its vertically inte- 
grated nature raise serious concerns about Bell entry into 
new industries.“ 


This Report argues that cable concerns are largely 
local. A telephone company, whether AT&T or the small- 
est independent, has extensive market power in its fran-

4 Ibid.

chise areas. AT&T differs from an independent telephone

Limits on AT&T may be further modified by Congress. 
The National Cable Television Association (NCTA) has 
argued that under AT&T’s proposed interpretation of the 
decree, Bell would be allowed to enter the cable market.” 

If the consent decree does not prohibit Bell entry into 
cable, the Commission should treat Bell like any other 
telephone company unless it can specify special circum- 
stances that justify the difference in treatment. The size 
differential by itself is not central. Both Bell and inde- 
pendent telephone companies have much the same incen- 
tive to enter cable; protection of their local monopoly 
power. Because cable provides competition to local facili- 
ties, concerns about local monopoly and access to inter-

5. Restricting Access 


The Commission cited the ability and apparent willing- 
ness of telephone companies to deny access to independent 
cab'= companies as one justification for the crossowner- 
ship ban.” 


*7U.S. v. Western Electric et. al., Consent Judgment, January 
24, 1956, U.S. District Court for New Jersey, 18 RRLN3: 1956 
Trade Cases 71, 134. 


** U.S. v. Western Electric Inc., and American Telephone and 
Telegraph Company, U.S. District Court for the District of New 
Jersey Civil No. 17-49, September 5, 1981. 


** NCTA comments, supra, pp. 49-50. 
* 1970 Report and Order (21 F.C.C. 2d) at 308, 323-824. 


62 


Power to deny access takes several forms. Of most im- 
mediate concern is telephone company ownership of poles 
to which cable may be attached and conduit through 
which cable may run. The Commission has authority 
(under the Pole Connection Act) to “regulate the rates, 
terms, and conditions for pole attachments” except where 
a state assumes regulatory responsibility.” In spite of 
this law, cable companies argue that problems of access 


remain substantial.” 


The problem of pole access does not by itself justify 
banning crossownership. If the telephone company were 
allowed to sell access to independent cable companies it 
could, in principle, develop a price schedule that exactly 
exhausted all cable monopoly profits and transfer these 
to the telephone company with no loss in consumer wel- 
fare.* Unless a telephone company believed it was able 
to achieve lower costs through direct ownership, it would 
be content to charge high prices for independent access. 
Where cable and telephone companies offer competitive 
services, however, access pricing could be used to reduce 
the cost of acquiring cable companies (by reducing cable 
profitability and hence the value of the system). The 
telephone company, therefore, would jointly operate cable 
and telephone facilities to maximize total profits. 

The ability of telephone companies to control access 
means that lease-back agreements must be carefully scru- 


51 Cable Connection Act, P.L. 95-234. As of January 28, 1981, 
19 states plus Puerto Rico, had assumed responsibility for regulat- 
ing pole attachments. 


52 Their arguments are three. First, the pole attachment statute 
expires five years after its enaction, i.e., in 1983; second, many 
states have preempted federal authority without providing an ade- 
quate substitute; and third, the enforcement process is suffiicently 
slow that pole access remains a substantial barrier. (See, for ex- 
ample, NCTA Comments supra at 13-14.
