data over time and across companies, establish benchmarks 
for comparison, identify disproportionate cost allocations 
indicative of potential cross-subsidization, and target on- 
site audits to carriers whose allocations appeared prob- 
lematic. 


For several reasons, ARMIS has not generated the compari- 
sons that would have allowed Fcc to substitute “com- 
puterized auditing” for some on-site audits. First, ARMIS 
does not break out cost pool data—that is, report regulated 
and unregulated costs for individual subaccounts—because 
cost pools vary from one carrier to another. Therefore, 
only aggregate (and less useful) comparisons are possible. 
In addition, each carrier’s cost allocation manual contains 
different procedures for allocating certain costs, and car- 
riers change these procedures throughout the year with 
rcc’s approval. According to the Chief of Accounting 
Systems of Fcc’s Common Carrier Bureau, FCC is cur- 
rently studying ways to make the cost allocation manuals 
uniform. He also said that if the cost allocation manuals 
had more uniform standards for cost pools and cost allo- 
cation procedures, then the automated reporting system 
could be more useful in identifying possible instances of 
cross-subsidy. 


Despite its limitations, ARMIS did enable Fcc to detect 
“inside wire” misallocations amounting to over $200 mil- 
lion. Charges for time spent by service representatives 
locating trouble with indoor telephone wires were in- 
appropriately allocated to regulated services. After de- 
regulating inside wiring and making homeowners responsi- 
ble for repairs to it, FCC was expecting the regulated 
account balances to decrease. When ARMIS data did not 
reflect the anticipated decrease, FCC auditors made on-site 
visits to determine the extent of the misallocation. Accord- 
ing to FCC officials, this was the only instance in which 
ARMIS data had enabled them to firmly identify a cost 
misallocation for follow-up by Fcc auditors. There have 


133 


been instances in other regulatory areas where ARMIS was 
used to identify problems. 


Through on-site audits, FCC auditors have identified cost 
misallocations that neither Cpa audits nor FCC’s reviews 
of CPAs’ workpapers and audit reports had disclosed. For 
example, FCC auditors have found cases of misallocations 
totaling over $300 million in which carriers charged 
expenses to the regulated side of their businesses and car- 
riers’ affiliates had overcharged regulated carriers for serv- 
ices and supplies. 


On-site audits have enabled Fcc auditors to detect not 
only cost misallocations but also deficiencies in carriers’ 
cost allocation procedures and in cpas’ audit practices and 
procedures. For example, in reviewing one carrier’s 
time reporting, FCC auditors found that for a 3-year 
period the carrier’s employees had not followed the proper 
procedures for allocating their time to regulated and un- 
regulated services. The CPA was aware of the carrier’s 
procedures, but the cpa believed it was in compliance 
with Fcc rules. FCC auditors disagreed and required the 
carrier to make the appropriate changes. Because the 
carrier had not followed Fcc’s procedures and the CPA 
firm had not enforced Fcc’s procedures, about $130 mil- 
lion had been charged erroneously to the regulated services. 
Following the on-site audit, Fcc auditors required the car- 
rier to reallocate the costs that it had improperly charged 
to the regulated services and to revise its procedures to 
conform with Fcc’s policies and procedures. 


Conclusions 


In 1987, we reported that Fcc had insufficient staff to 
ensure that consumers were protected from  cross- 
subsidization. Since that time, FCC's responsibility for 
Overseeing carriers’ cost allocations have continued to 
grow, but the staff resources allocated to this function 
have declined rather than increased. We believe the num- 


134 


ber of FCC auditors remains inadequate to provide a posi- 
tive assurance that ratepayers are protected from cross- 
subsidization. 

Increasing the Fcc staffing level, along with the appropri- 
ate travel funds, would have little or no impact on the 
federal budget because the government would be reim- 
bursed for its on-site audits and reviews of CPA audits 
through fees Fcc is authorized to collect from carriers. 
Given this trend of increasing work load and decreasing 
resources, the potential exists for ratepayers to be more 
vulnerable to inappropriate charges resulting from cross- 
subsidization in the future. Although CPA audits, FCC’S 
reviews of these audits, and ARMIS can give FCC useful 
information for monitoring, they have not identified over 
$300 million in cross-subsidization. Further refinements 
to FCC audit guidance and greater uniformity in reporting 
cost data for ARMIS may improve FCC’s information for 
oversight, but expanded coverage through additional on- 
site audits by FCC would give ratepayers and the Congress 
greater assurance that cross-subsidization is not occurring. 


Recommendation to 
the Chairman, Federal 
Communications 
Commission 


We recommend that the Chairman, FCC, continue to pur- 
sue the Commission’s quest for additional resources to 
improve assurance that cross-subsidization is not taking 
place. 


Matter for 
Congressional 
Consideration 


The Congress may wish to consider authorizing additional 
permanent positions for auditors and the appropriate travel 
funds, recognizing that the funds expended would have 
little or no impact on the federal budget. 


135 


Agency Comments 
and Our Evaluation 


We discussed the information contained in this report with 
the Deputy Chief of Fcc’s Common Carrier Bureau and 
his staff. They generally agreed with the factual informa- 
tion in the report, and we have incorporated their com- 
ments where appropriate . As requested, we did not ob- 
tain written agency comments on a draft of this report. 


The Commission’s position is that all the safeguards 
taken together are an effective deterrent against cross- 
subsidization. However, as pointed out in this report, FCC 
audit staff continue to find significant problems in the audits 
that they have been able to conduct. It is our view that 
the Fcc audit staffing level is not adequate to oversee cost 
allocations, and the additional safeguards FCC employs 
have not been effective in detecting all cases of cross- 
subsidization. Consequently, we continue to believe that 
our recommendation that FCC continue to seek the addi- 
tional resources is valid. 


To update our 1987 report and determine whether Fcc 
had increased its oversight of carriers’ operations as we 
had recommended, we reviewed Fcc’s efforts to obtain 
additional audit staff and examined Fcc’s implementation 
of nonstructural accounting safeguards. We performed our 
work in accordance with generally accepted government 
auditing standards from September 1991 through Septem- 
ber 1992. Appendix III contains further discussion of our 
objectives, scope, and methodology. 


As arranged with your offices, unless you publicly an- 
nounce its contents earlier, we plan no further distribution 
of this report until 30 days after the date of this letter. 
At that time, we will send copies of this report to interested 
congressional committees and subcommittees: individual 
Members of Congress; the Chairman, Federal Communi- 
cations Commission; the Director, Office of Management 


136 


and Budget; and other interested parties. We will make 
copies available to others on request.

M. Mead, Director, Transportation Issues, who can be 
reached at (202) 275-1000 if you or your staff have any 
questions. Major contributors to this report are listed in 
appendix IV.

AFFIDAVIT OF ROBERT W. LUCKY 
Robert W. Lucky deposes and says: 


1. I am currently Vice President, Applied Research, at 
Bell Communications Research (Bellcore). I have a 
Ph.D in electrical engineering from Purdue University. 
I spent three decades at Bell Labs, where I was respon- 
sible for, among other things, research on the methods 
and technologies for future communications systems, 
including optical fiber technology, data networks, mobile 
communications, image processing, and broadband com- 
munications technologies and services. I have written 
numerous books and articles, and received numerous 
awards, including the Marconi Prize for contributions to 
data communications. In 1993, I was inducted into the 
New Jersey Inventors Hall of Fame at the New Jersey 
Institute of Technology. I have served as President of 
the Communications Society of the IEEE (Institute of 
Electrical and Electronics Engineers), and as Vice Presi- 
dent and Executive Vice President of the parent IEEE 
itself. 

2. Most cable systems today are operated in the 
broadcast mode: one centralized transmitter dispatches 


138 


information to thousands of identical receivers. By con- 
trast, voice telephone service is two-way and interactive. 
Any subscriber can communicate personally with any 
other subscriber. 

3. Until very recently, video television signals were 
delivered to households over a form of metal wire called 
“coaxial cable.” Coaxial cable was the best medium avail- 
able for distributing one-way video signals to consumers. 
The broadcast networks deployed by cable companies 
were not suitable for providing two-way voice telephone 
service. Telephone service was provided, instead, over 
“twisted pair” copper wires. Cable and telephone wires 
were physically different. Coaxial cable consisted of a 
metal wire running down the middle of cylindrical metal 
sheath. “Twisted pair” telephone wiring consisted of two 
identical copper wires twisted together in a double helix. 

4. Both telephone companies and cable companies are 
now replacing metal wires with fiber-optic glass cable. 
Signals are translated into digital form (as a series of on- 
off pulses) and transmitted as flashing laser light through 
thin strands of very pure glass. Fiber optic communica- 
tion systems handle video, voice, and data in exactly the 
same way. The information is all digital. It is trans- 
mitted over the same facilities, in the same way, whether 
it represents a rerun of “I Love Lucy,” a phone call on 
Mother’s Day, or a stock quote from Wall Street. 

5. For example, East Buchanan Telephone in Win- 
throp, Towa, has about 600 cable and 1,300 telephone 
subscribers, served over a single backbone network that 
was upgraded to fiber in Spring 1992.' The FCC has like- 
wise permitted GTE to build and maintain an integrated, 
high-capacity test network in Cerritos, California,’ which

way of fiber, coaxial, and copper wire, to 5,000 homes.

6. Even as they deploy glass fiber, both cable com- 
panies and telephone companies are deploying new tech- 
nologies to upgrade the capabilities of their existing metal-

7. Recently developed new technologies permit tele- 
phone companies to transport full-motion color video 
programs directly to subscribers over the existing tele- 
phone network. A new technology known as ADSL

has evolved dramatically in the last decade. A decade

AFFIDAVIT OF WILLIAM E. LEE 


1. My name is William E. Lee. I am a Professor in 
the Henry W. Grady College of Journalism and Mass 
Communication at the University of Georgia. I earned a 
Doctorate degree in Mass Communications at the Univer- 
sity of Wisconsin in 1977 and have been on the faculty at 
the University of Georgia since 1977. I have taught 
courses on the structure and practices of the mass media 
and have published extensively on mass communication. 
I have testified as an expert on mass communication at 
seven trials and have provided expert testimony through 
affidavits or depositions in seven other lawsuits. The mat- 
ters set forth and the opinions expressed herein are known 
or personally held by me and if called as a witness, I 
could testify competently to them. 

2. Based on my background in the field of mass com- 
munication, I make three principal points in this affidavit. 
First, the creation, selection, arrangement, packaging and 
provision of video programming for multichannel delivery, 
whether it is performed by a cable company or by Bell 
Atlantic Video Services (“BVS”), involves editorial deci- 
sions that are functionally indistinguishable from those 
involved in newspaper editing. Second, today video com- 
munication is the most important mass medium in Amer- 
ica. Third, the ban against telephone company provision 
of “video programming” at issue in this lawsuit cannot 
be understood or enforced without reference to, and gov- 


143 


ernment scrutiny of, the content of individual programs 
or services. 


[. Editorial Decision-Making By Multichannel Video 
Programmers and Newspapers 


3. Based on my review of the pleadings in this case 
and on discussions with employees of BVS, I have gained 
an understanding of the nature of the services that BVS 
intends to provide. Specifically, BVS intends to provide a 
multichannel package of video programming directly to 
consumers. Although BVS will use the common carrier 
telephone network of The Chesapeake and Potomac Tele- 
phone Company of Virginia (“C&P”) to deliver this pro- 
gramming, BVS itself will not act in any sense as a com- 
mon carrier. Rather, BVS will exercise complete editorial 
discretion over the package of programming it will pro- 
vide, whether BVS decides to create programming of its 
own, or selects, assembles, edits and arranges video pro- 
gramming obtained from others. In this respect, BVS 
will perform the same editorial functions performed by 
cable operators in selecting and packaging the program- 
ming provided over their cable systems. As I will explain 
below, moreover, the editorial functions performed by 
BVS and other multichannel programmers are indistin- 
guishable from those performed by newspapers. 


Multichannel Video Programmers 


4. At the outset, it is important to point out that 
multichannel video programmers exercise editorial discre- 
tion in two separate respects. First, these programmers 
exercise editorial discretion in choosing the programming 
they will provide. Second, once this initial selection is 
made, multichannel programmers exercise editorial dis- 
cretion in arranging and packaging this programming for 
sale to consumers. 1 address each of these functions 
below. 


144 


5. Multichannel video programmers must choose 
among available programming options because of both 
technical and financial constraints. Currently most multi- 
channel video programming is delivered over cable sys- 
tems that have limited channel capacity, and the supply 
of available programming exceeds the capacity of vir- 
tually all cable systems. Even apart from these capacity 
limitations, however, multichannel video programmers 
also face financial constraints on the number and types 
of video programs for which consumers will pay. As a 
result, multichannel programmers must exercise discretion 
to choose among the available programming options. 

6. Multichannel video programmers make program- 
ming decisions based upon two distinct strategies. Some 

ming services, such as broadcast stations, are 
selected because of their appeal to broad segments of the 
audience. Other programming services, like Black Enter- 
tainment Television, are selected because they appeal to 
narrow racial, ethnic, age, or interest groups. Some pro- 
gramming services are identified with particular subjects 
and viewpoints. For example, the Family Channel offers 
a different view on sexual morality than does the Playboy 
at Night service. The interest of audience segments in 
certain topics and viewpoints influences the selection of 
programming. The primary programming options for 
multichannel video programmers are briefly described 
below: 

a. Multichannel video programmers select which 
broadcast stations to carry. The selection of broadcast 
stations is affected by the number and nature of stations 
in the local and adjacent markets, and by whether ( and 
how much) a multichannel provider must pay for “re- 
transmission” rights for a broadcaster's signal. Since the 
most-watched sources on cable are the major network 
(ABC, CBS and NBC) broadcast affiliates, multichannel 
video programmers naturally retransmit the signals of 
local affiliates. However, a multichannel video program- 


145 


mer may wish to carry two affiliates of the same network 
located in adjacent markets. Although the network pro- 
gramming on both stations will be duplicative, the addi- 
tional station is selected because of factors such as audi- 
ence interest in news from the adjacent market. Multi- 
channel video programmers must also determine which 
local independent stations to retransmit. For example, a 
suburban cable system may prefer not to carry an inde- 
pendent television station that broadcasts in a foreign 
language that is of interest only to urban residents. Every 
broadcast signal that is carried reduces the options the 
programmer has for other types of programming. 

b. Multichannel video programmers also select 
among cable programming services such as the 75 plus 
basic cable network, 42 regional networks, 7 pay cable net- 
works, 7 pay-per-view networks, 10 text services, and 11 
audio services. (Attachment A lists the available cable 
programming services.) While some of these services 
seek to reach broad audiences, others are “niche” services 
that appeal to a very limited age or interest group. In 
1991-92, 43% of viewing time in cable households was 
devoted to basic and pay cable services. See Stipulation 8. 
The audience for each service, therefore, represents a very 
small portion (often less than 1%) of the total audience: 
selection of the appropriate mix of services entails the 
identification of small groups whose interests are not 
served by other sources. 

c. Currently, approximately forty-one percent of 
cable systems in the United States carry locally originated 
programming. TV & Cable Factbook, Services Volume at 
1-68 (1993 edition). The types of local programs in- 
clude sports, comedy, children’s, talk, music, and news. 
Because a cable system serves a more limited geographic 
area than the multi-country region served by a television 
station, a cable system is able to provide programming 
more closely tailored to the interests of a particular town. 
For example, one of the most significant recent develop- 
ments in local cable programming is the coverage of local 


146 


news. A broadcast television station’s news necessarily 
focuses on the major city in the television market, such 
as Washington, D.C., or on stories of regional interest, 
but provides little coverage of the many smaller com- 
munities located within the station’s market. A cable sys- 
tem, however, can fill in the news “niches” left untouched 
by broadcast television news. Thus, local cable news is 
merging in places like Long Island, New York and 
Orange County, California that are largely ignored by 
broadcast television news. These cable news operations 
range in size from the twenty-four-hour service in Long 
Island, to daily half-hour newscasts in communities such 
as Ithaca, New York, to brief inserts of local news in 
CNN Headline News. The cost of producing local news 
coverage, however, is a major obstacle to the develop- 
ment of these services. See, e.g., “Local News Stymies 
Many Cable Firms,” Wall Street Journal, June 15, 1991, 
at B1. To control costs, multichannel video programmers 
are entering cooperative arrangements with local broad- 
casters and newspaper publishers. For example, some 
broadcasters prepare coverage of elections just for dis- 
semination on cable. See, e.g., “KYW-TV Election Cov- 
erage: Via Cable Systems,” “Broadcasting, November 
4. 1991, at 48 (describing Philadelphia, Pennsylvania 
television station’s arrangement with local cable systems). 
Some multichannel video programmers allow either local 
broadcasters or newspapers to insert a brief summary of 
local news in a cable program service such as Headline 
News or a weather update in the Weather Channel. See, 
e.g., “System Teams Up with Local Paper, Broadcaster,” 
Multichannel News, August 20, 1990, at 14 (describing 
Rock Hill, South Carolina cable system’s arrangement). 

7. In addition, once the multichannel video programmer 
selects the programming it will provide, it must package 
the programming for sale to consumers. In doing so, the 
programmer exercises editorial discretion both in terms 
of determining where particular programming is posi- 
tioned in the channel lineup, and in terms of determining 


147 


which programming to include in particular packages. 
For example, the programmer must decide which pro- 
gramming to include in a “basic” package, in more in- 
clusive packages, and as “premium” services. 

8. In summary, whether deciding to carry material 
produced by others, whether and how to cover local 
events, or how to arrange and package programming for 
sale, multichannel video programmers make choices about 
the programming content offered to subscribers. Such 
choices are affected by audience demographics, audience 
interests, ideology, and technological and financial con- 
straints. The process of editorial choice engaged in by 
multichannel video programmers is no different than that 
engaged in by newspapers. 


Newspapers 


9. As in the case with multichannel video pro- 
grammers, newspaper publishers also face constraints on 
the amount and type of news content included in their 
papers. The most important source of newspaper reve- 
nue is from the sale of advertising space; 78% of a 
newspaper's revenue comes from advertising, with the re- 
maining 22% from subscriptions. The Veronis, Suhler 
& Associates Communications Industry Forecast at 39 
(1992). Consequently, the amount of news and feature 
content is usually determined by the number of advertise- 
ments that have been sold. The typical newshole—the 
space allocated to news and features—is about 40% of 
a newspaper’s content. J. Dominick, The Dynamics of 
Mass Communication at 203 (2nd ed. 1987). On a 
heavy advertising day, the newshole may be only 25% 
of the paper’s contents. D. Pember, Mass Media in 
America at 59 (4th ed. 1983). Newspaper editors, thus. 
must carefully select the newshole content from among 
the available options. 

10. Like multichannel video programmers, contem- 
porary newspapers provide information of interest to a 


148 


general audience as well as information of interest to 
segments of the audience, such as cooking, science, and 
finance. Through readership studies, editors seek to iden- 
tify both the general and special interests of the audience; 
news content is a product designed to be purchased by 
readers. Newspaper editorial costs typically range from 
14 and 22% of total costs. J. Udell, The Economics of 
the American Newspaper at 122 (1978). Because edi- 
tors must stay within a budget, the relative cost of 
various types of content is a factor in selecting material. 
For example, the use of syndicated or news service ma- 
terial may cost less than material written by the news- 
paper’s staff. 

11. The primary newshole options available to a 
newspaper are briefly described below: 

a. With the exceptions of very large circulation 
newspapers, typical newspapers rely upon news services 
such as the Associated Press for national and international 
news. Information from these services can be rewritten 
by the newspaper’s staff, or carried without alteration. 
A 1986 study found that Associated Press member papers 
fill 37% of their newsholes with AP copy. C. Fink, 
Strategic Newspaper Management at 167 (1988). There 
are approximately 142 news and picture services available 
to newspapers. Editor & Publisher International Year- 
book at V-1-6 (1992). 

b. Newspapers also rely upon feature syndicates for 
material such as cartoons, editorial cartoons, sports box 
scores, weather maps, and columnists. This material is 
provided to the newspaper in a form that is ready to 
print, and is generally carried without editing by the 
newspaper. Syndicated material may fill up to 18% of 
the newshole. Since hundreds of newspapers buy from 
a syndiate, the cost to a newspaper for these features 
is relatively low; the cost of hiring a columnist to write 
solely for one newspaper is greater than the cost of buy- 
ing a syndicated column. There are approximately 417 


149 


entities offering syndicated material to newspapers. Edi- 
tor & Publisher International Yearbook at V-7-12 (1992). 

c. Many Sunday newspapers include preprinted in- 
serts such as comics sections, magazines like Parade, and 
television listings such as TV Log. Newspapers make no 
alteration of the content of these sections; the material 
is prepared and printed elsewhere and merely included 
within the newspaper. The choice the newspaper makes 
is whether to have such a section, and if so, which of 
the available services should be selected. A newspaper 
serving a community that is heavily Hispanic may chose 
Vista—The Hispanic Magazine over Parade, but does not 
alter or edit the selected magazine. There are 12 comics 
sections, and 10 magazines available to newspapers. Edi- 
tor & Publisher International Yearbook at V-12-17 
(1992). 

d. Editors may assign reporters to cover local events. 
News policies, such as avoiding negative references about 
major advertisers, influence coverage. See L. Solely & R. 
Craig, “Advertising Pressures on Newspapers,” 21 J. of 
Advertising 1 (1992). The perception of what is news, 
based on analysis of factors such as timeliness, proximity, 
prominence, consequence, and human interest, also de- 
termines what events the newspaper assigns reporters to 
cover. Cost also influences local coverage; some stories, 
such as those involving lengthy investigations, cost more 
to prepare than others. 

12. In summary, like multinational video programmers, 
newspapers frequently carry material prepared by others. 
The newspapers nonetheless exercise editorial discretion 
by determining in the first instance whether the material 
should be published, and also in determining the edi- 
torial arrangement of the content. Whether selecting and 
arranging material provided by others, or deciding how 
to cover local news, newspaper content is affected by 
audience demographics, audience interests. ideology, and 
technological and financial constraints, just as the content 


150 


of a multichannel video programmer’s package of pro- 
gramming is affected by these same factors. 


BVS’s Services 


13. Based on the understanding I have gained of the 
nature of the services BVS intends to offer, it is also clear 
that BVS’s editorial process is comparable to that of 
newspapers and of other multichannel video program- 
mers. It merits noting that my opinion expressed in this 
affidavit is not affected by the content of particular pro- 
grams BVS intends to offer. Instead my opinion is based 
on the kinds of activities in which BVS is currently en- 
gaged. 

14. In preparing its programming plan, BVS con- 
ducted research concerning audience demographics, and 
completed its own proprietary marketing survey of Alex- 
andria residents. In addition, the company acquired re- 
ports of meetings of the Alexandria Cable Television 
Advisory Commission, as well as the results of a survey 
of cable subscribers conducted by Jones Intercable, the 
incumbent cable operator. 

15. Through this research BVS learned that consum- 
ers are interested in specific services not offered by Jones 
Intercable. For example, in 1992 Jones deleted all of the 
Baltimore television stations it carried to make room 
for four cable program services. BVS intends to offer 
those Baltimore stations to its subscribers. BWVS also 
learned of subscriber interest in WGN, a Chicago super- 
station, and as a result intends to offer it to subscribers. 
In response to consumer interest in greater variety, BVS 
will offer more local and adjacent-market broadcast sig- 
nals. basic cable networks, and premium cable networks, 
than does Jones. Finally, BVS found that there was 
significant audience interest in video-on-demand, a service 
that permits consumers to order any one of potentially 
hundreds of programs from a library of stored videos 
for viewing at any time. This is also a service that is not 
offered by Jones. 


151 


16. Although BVS seeks to offer diverse programming, 
the company has established editorial standards govern- 
ing the portrayal of sex and violence. The community ac- 
cepts the level of sex and violence offered on services 
such as HBO. More explicit material, however, will not 
be offered. Based on these standards, the company has 
decided not to carry Spice or Playboy at Night, two 
sexually explicit programming services. The standards 
governing sex and violence will also be used in the selec- 
tion of material to be included in the video library. 
Standards governing other types of content will be de- 
veloped in conjunction with the community as the com- 
pany gains experience with video programming. BVS 
intends to sell advertising time and will have standards 
on advertising content similar to those of local broad- 
casters. 

17. In creating the video library for its video-on- 
demand service, BVS intends to arrange the programming 
it chooses for inclusion into six categories: entertainment, 
living, learning, buying, communication, and informa- 
tion seeking. Each segment is further divided into a 
number of subdivisions. For example, within the enter- 
tainment segment are subdivisions for movies, sports, and 
video games. The video library provides consumers with 
a significant increase in programming options. However, 
“shelf space” within the library is not infinite. Thus, BVS 
must carefully select the programming included in the 
video library to reflect audience interests, community 
standards, and creative judgments. 

18. BVS’s editorial discretion concerning the develop- 
ment of the video library operates at several levels. First, 
the company selected the segments to be included. Sec- 
ond, it selected the specific programming services, or indi- 
vidual programs, to be made available to consumers. 
Third, BVS is arranging Categories, subdivisions, and navi- 
gational aids to guide the consumer in a manner similar 
to that in which a newspaper lays out its sections and 


152 


pages. Fourth, to aid the user, BVS is prioritizing menu 
listings. For example, within a subcategory such as action- 
adventure films, BVS will offer a listing of “top” films, as 
well as a more inclusive listing. Fifth, BSV is packaging 
programs according to themes, and is combining programs 
for “best of” presentations. Thus, while some viewers may 
be interested in all of the episodes of “Cheers,” others may 
prefer BVS’s compilation of what it regards as the best 
episodes. Sixth, reflecting its concern for parental control 
over children’s use of the library, BVS is organizing ma- 
terial according to age categories and is offering parents 
the ability to “lock-out” certain segments, subdivisions, and 
program sources. Seventh, because there is community 
interest in specific types of local programs, BVS will make 
equipment and studios for the production of local pro- 
gramming available; this programming will be archived in 
the video library. 

19. In summary, BVS’s editorial decision-making is the 
same as that of other multichannel video programmers or 
newspapers. The company’s decisions concerning which 
programming services to offer are based on market studies 
that reveal consumer interests unmet by the incumbent 
cable operator. Moreover, the company has adopted stand- 
ards, based on community attitudes, concerning depictions 
of sex and violence. BVS is also exercising editorial 
discretion in the selection and packaging of the video 
library. 


Il. The Significance of Video Programming As A 
Medium of Mass Communication 


20. The importance of video programming as a me- 
dium of mass communication today is beyond dispute. 
Video programming is now the dominant means by which 
people obtain news, information and entertainment. 
Whether one thinks in terms of television coverage of im- 
portant events such as the Watergate hearings, the Gulf 


153 


War or political campaigns, or in terms of video entertain- 
ment including movies such as “Dances With Wolves” that 
combine gripping visual imagery with important social 
messages, video programming communicates messages and 
ideas in a manner that cannot be fully duplicated through 
use of print or audio media alone. Moreover, while these 
are not matters that are subject to any dispute, the im- 
portance of video programming can be illustrated by 
reference to empirical data. 

21. For example, the importance of video program- 
ming as a medium of communication is illustrated by 
comparing the amount of time people spend with various 
media, the amount of money people spend on various 
media, and the amount of money advertisers spend on 
various media. Specifically, Tables I-IV in Attachment B 
reveal that in 1991 consumers spent more time with broad- 
cast and cable television than any other medium, and spent 
more money on cable subscriptions than on the purchase 
of any other media product. While newspapers remain the 
largest single advertising vehicle, in 1991 the total adver- 
tising and consumer expenditures on broadcast and cable 
television, $41.8 billion, exceeded the total advertising and 
consumer expenditures on daily newspapers, $39.6 billion, 
by $2.2 billion. The amount spent on video tape rentals 
and purchases in 1991 exceeded box office revenues by 
$6.1 billion. 

22. It is also important to note a number of trends 
concerning media usage. First, the percentage of adults 
reading newspapers daily has been declining, and was at 
62% in 1991. The Veronis, Suhler & Associates Com- 
munications Industry Forecast at 171 (1992). Second, 
cable penetration as a percentage of television households 
has increased markedly to over 60% today. Accompany- 
ing the increase in cable penetration has been a shift in 
viewing patterns. Broadcast stations’ share of total tele- 
vision viewing has declined from 87% in 1986 to 73.5% 
in 1991. In the same period basic and pay cable services’ 


154 


share of total television viewing has increased from 13% 
to 26.5%. Id. at 77. In households that subscribe to 
cable, moreover, 43% of viewing time was devoted to 
cable-originated programming as of 1991-92. Third, the 
percentage of television households with a VCR has in- 
creased from 37.8% in 1986 to 73.7% in 1991. Id. at 
127. Expenditures on the rental and purchase of pre- 
recorded video tapes have increased from $5 billion in 
1986 to $10.9 billion in 1991. Id. 

23. Television has become the dominant news source 
for most Americans. Television is a main news source for 
66% of Americans, compared to 36% for newspapers 
and 14% for radio. The Roper Organization, America’s 
Watching: Public Attitudes Toward Television at 18 
(1987) (respondents were allowed to mention more than 
one medium as a main news source, thus the percentages 
add up to more than 100). The increasing reliance on 
television news is explained in part by its credibility. 
Since 1961 television has been perceived as the most 
credible news medium, and its lead continues to increase. 
Id. at 5. A February 1993 poll revealed that the Cable 
News Network (CNN) was the most believable news 
source, followed closely by ABC, CBS, and NBC. Times 
Mirror Center for the People and the Press, Times Mirror 
News Interest Index at 1 (March 3, 1993). Daily news- 
papers were considered to be less believable than CNN 
and the major broadcast networks. /d. Significantly, al- 
though NBC News was damaged by the revelation that 
it staged the explosion of a General Motors truck, it was 
still regarded as more believable than daily newspapers. 
Id. 

24. Television is especially important as a means of 
political communication. It has become the dominant 
source of both national and local political news. The 
Roper Organization, America’s Watching: Public Attitudes 
Toward Television at 6-7 (1987). It has altered how 


155 


campaign resources are expended, how campaign organi- 
zations are structured, and how compaigns are conducted. 
Because of the heavy expenditures on television advertis- 
ing by politicians, the costs of campaigning have increased. 
In fact, the largest category of expenditure for House, 
Senate, and Presidential campaigns is for television ad- 
vertising. See, e.g., S. Fritz & D. Morris, Handbook of 
Campaign Spending: Money in the 1990 Congressional 
Races at 53-57 (Cong. Q. 1992). See generally E. Dia- 
mond & S. Bates, The Spot: The Rise of Political Advertis- 
ing on Television (1988 ed.). Nominating conventions 
and campaign activities are organized for television cov- 
erage. Campaign staffs typically include consultants who 
advise the candidate on his or her television image. See 
M. McCubbins, ed., Under the W stchful Eye: Managing 
Presidential Campaigns in the Television Era (1992). 
Although the impact of these developments on voting 
behavior is not clear, the behavior of candidates reveals 
that they regard television as the critical means of culti- 
vating voter awareness. 

25. While providing news and entertainment, television 
functions as a socialization agent by transmitting values, 
presenting role models, and communicating acceptable be- 
haviors. The relative importance of television as a socializ- 
ing agent is underscored by the medium’s pervasiveness 
and accessibility, and by the amount of time people spend 
viewing. Ninety-eight percent of all American homes have 
television and the average home has 2.1 sets. U.S. Dept. 
of Commerce, Bureau of Census, Statistical Abstract of 
the United States 1992 Table 884 (112th ed. 1992). Tele- 
vision is accessible to all income and educational groups. 
In contrast, newspaper consumption requires literacy, and 
increases as education and income levels increase. Id. at 
Table 885. Television’s accessibility is also enhanced by 
its low hourly costs to users; even when cable subscriptions 
are included, total television viewing cost only 6 cents per 
person per hour in 1991. By comparison, newspapers cost 


156 


29 cents per hour, magazines cost 38 cents per hour, and 
books cost 69 cents per hour. The Veronis, Suhler & 
Associates Communications Industry Forecast at 14 
(1992). And as Table I illustrates, people spend more 
time with television than any other medium. No medium 
in our society transmits as many social cues, to as many 
people, as frequently, as does television. 
26. The combination of sight and sound in video pro- 
ing provided by television make it a distinctive com- 
munications medium. Television can depict the real 
world, but it also can create images and sounds that are 
“unreal.” Television’s visual images change rapidly, 
through techniques such as cutting between cameras, Zooms 
and pans; through the use of special effects; and through 
the image manipulation made possible by advances in 
computer technology. Television dialogue may be life-like, 
but the audio portion of television may also include en- 
riching features like musical accompaniment and sound 
effects. These audio and visual techniques sustain in- 
terest in the program, but also convey meaning. For 
example, children quickly learn that a fade or dissolve 
signals a change in time or location. Researchers have 
posited that this combination of visual and auditory com- 
ponents make television distinct from other media. See, 
e.g., A. Huston & J. Wright, “Children’s Processing of 
Television: The Informative Functions of Formal Fea- 
tures,” in J. Bryant & D. Anderson, eds., Children’s Un- 
derstanding of Television (1983). The communicative 
features of the video programming that is provided over 
television are unmatched by print, photographs, or radio. 
27. A multichannel video service, such as that proposed 
by BVS, provides a unique communication environment. 
The programmer may employ distinctive programming 
strategies, such as seeking to reach both broad and narrow 
audience segments. As a result, audience members may 
employ a range of information-seeking strategies, driven 
by general or highly specialized interests. In addition, the 


157 


Proposed video-on-demand feature enables the consumer to 
access an extraordinary range of video information with- 
out the time constraints posed by broadcast station or 
cable channel scheduling. Most importantly, video-on- 
demand permits viewers to more efficiently seek informa- 
tion relevant to them. Video-on-demand transforms the 
customary television-viewing question from “What’s on 
tv?” to “What can / put on tv?” 


Ill. Enforcing the Video Ban Requires Government 
Scrutiny of Program Content 


28. When Congress created the ban on “video pro- 
gramming” in 1984, it defined the subject of its prohibi- 
tion to include video programming “provided by, or gen- 
erally considered comparable to” video programming 
provided by broadcast stations. This prohibition can only 
be understood or enforced by reference to, and govern- 
ment scrutiny of, the content of individual programs or 
services that telephone companies provide. 

29. The very language chosen by Congress emphasizes 
the need for government officials interpreting and enforc- 
ing the ban to exercise their subjective judgment to deter- 
mine if a particular program is forbidden “video pro- 
gramming.” The ban does not apply to all “video images.” 
Nor does its apply to particular technologies, such as 
over-the-air broadcast technologies. Rather, the ban fo- 
cuses on the content of the service being provided. If, in 
the judgment of the appropriate government officials, the 
program would be “generally considered comparable to” 
programming provided by broadcasters, then it is for- 
bidden. If in their subjective judgment the program 
would not be considered comparable, then it is permitted. 

30. In today’s environment of rapidly changing tech- 
nologies, government officials will frequently be called 
upon to exercise their judgment as to what is and what 
is not “video programming.” Unlike 1984, when voice, 


158 


data and video were largely discrete services delivered 
using separate technologies, these services are no longer 
discrete, nor are they delivered using different technologies. 
For example, services available today can mix voice, data, 
text and video together in a variety of combinations. 
These services range from relatively simple videotext serv- 
ices such as airline guides that can be displayed either on 
television or personal computer screens, to complicated 
multi-media services that are fully interactive. But whether 
these services, or other new services that are being or will 
be developed, qualify as “video programming” turns upon 
a subjective view of whether the content of the service is 
“comparable to” programming provided by broadcasters. 

31. Moreover, the concern that the statute will set 
government officials up to act as censors of what is and is 
not permitted is not a speculative one. The scope of the 
ban has been the subject of considerable dispute, and has 
been extensively litigated before the FCC. Without regard 
to whether the FCC’s chosen interpretation of the scope of 
the ban is correct or incorrect, the efforts of the FCC to 
grapple with the problem serve to illustrate the basic point 
that the ban can only be understood through reference to 
the content of particular programs. 

32. Specifically, in its 1992 “Video Dialtone” order, 
the FCC was called upon to address whether some of the 
new services containing video images qualify as “video 
programming.” As an initial matter, however, the Com- 
mission had to determine the appropriate reference point 
for comparing these services to programming provided by 
broadcasters. Based on the legislative history, the Com- 
mission interpreted the statutory language to mean that 
“Congress intended to prohibit only telephone company 
provision of programming comparable to that provided by 
broadcast stations in 1984.” Telephone Company-Cable 
Television Cross-Ownership Rules, 7 FCC Red 5781, 
5820 (1992). See also 130 Cong. Rec. H12235 (daily 
ed. Oct. 11, 1984) (the House notes that the term “video 


159 


programming” refers to programming generally provided 
by a television broadcast station today). 

33. In deference to the subjective nature of the statu- 
tory ban, the FCC adopted what it described as a “severa- 
bility” standard. This standard considers whether a 
particular service is interactive, but does not consider this 
factor to be determinative. According to the FCC, “to the 
extent a service contains severable video images capable of 
being provided as independent video programs comparable 
to those provided by broadcast stations in 1984, that por- 
tion of the programming service will be deemed to consti- 
tute ‘video programming’... .” 7 FCC Red at 5821. 
As a result, telephone companies are permitted to provide 
the underlying technical capabilities that make an inter- 
active service work, but may still be barred from providing 
the actual programming content for the service if, in the 
FCC's subjective opinion, it is comparable to program- 
ming provided by broadcasters. 

34. The Commission elaborated on its “severability” 
test by offering some examples: 


—According to the PCC, a shopping service or 
“video catalogue” that allows consumers to “elec- 
tronically request specific goods and services would 
not constitute prohibited video programming, even if 
lie service incorporated video images. In such a 
case, the video images would not be severable from 
the interactivity.” /d. at 5822. But home shopping 
programs, even those enabling the consumer to order 
a product electronically, would be prohibited if they 
resemble home shopping programs broadcast in 
1984. Id. 

—Likewise, an educational multimedia presenta- 
tion containing video images is generally permissible 
as long as it includes enough textual or graphic infor- 
mation and interactivity that the content demon- 
strably differs from what broadcasters provided in 


160 


1984, but “the simple inclusion of some textual in- 
formation” would not transform a “severable video 
program into a permissible multimedia program.” 
Id. at 5822 n.196. 

—Providing the video programming content for 
interactive pay-per-view and video-on-demand serv- 
ices is prohibited because the programming is sever- 
able from the function of ordering programming; but 
strictly one-way services such as videotext news serv- 
ices, stock market information, on-line airline guides, 
and downloading of video games and computer soft- 
ware are permissible because the content of these 
services is sufficiently different from programming 
provided by broadcasters. Jd. at 5822-23 & n.199. 


35. None of these distinctions between permissible 
and impermissible programming can be maintained by 
reference to technical considerations. For example, there 
is not technological basis for distinguishing one form of 
multimedia tele-shopping from another. Video and audio 
information can be delivered over telephone lines, cable, 
and other media, displayed on a personal computer or a 
television receiver. All formats offer instant ordering 
capability. Consequently, a ban on home-video shopping 
of the kind provided by television in 1984 can be enforced 
only by a highly subjective comparison of the content of 
contemporary programs with programs from 1984. The 
same subjective comparison occurs with other types of 
services, and the need for these subjective comparisons 
will increase. In 1984, a video news program with occa- 
sional text captions was recognizably different from a 
videotext service like NEXIS that was provided over a 
telephone line. But with today’s merging media formats, 
it is possible to deliver text, pictures, sound, and inter- 
active search-and-selection capabilities through a variety 
of delivery systems. In short, the only way the FCC can 
distinguish between impermissible “video programming” 
and permissible programming is to examine the content 


161 


frame by frame, sound bite by sound bite, caption by cap- 


tion, with the kind of programming offered on television 
in 1984. 


S/ Wm. E. Lee 
WILLIAM E. Ler

AFFIDAVIT OF RICHARD A. ALSTON 


1. I am Vice President—Operations & Engineering for 
The Chesapeake & Potomac Telephone Company of Vir- 
ginia (“C&P”). I have had extensive experience in net- 
work operations in my career over the past 21 years with 
C&P and with other parts of the former Bell System. 

2. C&P is proposing to enhance its telephone network 
in Alexandria, Virginia to have the capability of carrying 
video programming upon the successful outcome of this 
litigation. 

3. If C&P made these enhancements today, C&P 
would use a fiber optic system supplied by BroadBand 
Technologies, Inc., which carries both telephone calls and 
video signals on an integrated basis. C&P currently is 
using this system to carry the video programming of third 
parties at the Cascades development in Loudoun County, 
Virginia. A sister company of C&P, the New Jersey Bell 
Telephone Company, has announced that it will use this 
system to carry the video programming of third parties 
in several areas in New Jersey. 

4. Using the BroadBand Technologies, Inc. system, 
C&P’s network in Alexandria would have the Capacity to 
provide several hundred channels of video programming 
to its telephone subscribers. 

5. In addition, C&P’s network would have the capa- 
bility to deliver video programs on demand. This will 
allow consumers to select one of potentially hundreds of 


170 


movies or other programs from video libraries to view at 
any given time of the consumer’s choosing. 

6. If permitted by this Court, C&P would make these 
capabilities available to its affiliate, Bell Atlantic Video 
Services Company (“BVS"), so that BVS can provide 
video programming to subscribers in Alexandria. C&P 
would also make these capabilities available for use by 
other video programmers under tariff on a common car- 
rier basis. 

7. In providing these services, C&P will not exercise 
editorial control over the unaffiliated programmers that 
use its network—with the exception of the possible exclu- 
sion of pornography or other offensive material to the 
extent that such exclusion is permitted under common 
carrier regulation. 

8. C&P, to the extent permitted by the FCC, may also 
offer some limited video programming of its own such 
as introductory menus or welcoming screens. 

I declare under penalty of perjury under the laws of 
the United States of America that the foregoing is true 
and correct. Executed on April 27, 1993. 


/s/ Richard A. Alston 
RICHARD A. ALSTON

AFFIDAVIT OF DR. VIVIAN M. HORNER 


1. I am Director of Bell Atlantic Video Services Com- 
pany (“BVS”) and part of the team that is developing 
video programming for BVS to provide in Alexandria and 
elsewhere. 

2. I have more than thirty years of programming and 
communications experience, primarily in start-up ventures. 
Among other positions, I was formerly Vice President, 
Program Development, of Warner Amex Cable Com- 
munications, where I created several highly-successful 
video programming services, including Pinwheel (a chan- 
nel for preschool children), Sight on Sound (the precur- 
sor of MTV), and Nickelodeon. 

3. If permitted at the end of this case, BVS will 
provide video programming in Alexandria using the C&P 
telephone network. BVS will purchase capacity from 
C&P on the same tariffed terms as offered by C&P to 
others. 

4. BVS will select and package some of its program- 
ming from the numerous cable programming networks 
that are available; it will also select some programming 
from various film libraries and from independent pro- 
ducers. 

5. In addition, some programming may be produced 
exclusively for BVS by producers working under contract 
with BVS or by BVS employees themselves. Bell Atlantic 
has three broadcast-quality television studios located in 


172 


Silver Spring, Philadelphia, and Newark which BVS may 
use to produce programming. 

6. BVSalso will carry broadcast programming. 

7. The programming that BVS will provide in Alex- 
andria is based on a review of audience demographics and 
interests, community standards, and BVS’s and my own 
editorial judgments. BVS will exercise editorial control 
over the programming that it provides. 

8. BVS is currently engaged in discussions with a 
number of suppliers of video programming. No supplier 
that BVS has talked to has refused to supply program- 
ming. To the contrary, suppliers are eager to open up an 
alternative distribution channel. 

9. BVS plans to provide programming that is not 
currently available in Alexandria. For example, BVS 
plans to carry several Spanish language channels such 
as Canal SUR, Galavision/ECO, Telemundo, and Uni- 
vision. According to the 1990 census, 9.7% of Alex- 
andria residents are Hispanic. The Alexandria Public 
Schools offer instruction in English as a second language, 
the largest block of students in these programs speak 
Spanish as their primary language. Jones carries only one 
Spanish language broadcast station (W48AC) and none 
of the other available Spanish language programming. As 
another example, BVS plans to carry WGN, a Chicago 
broadcast superstation that carries Cubs baseball games. 
According to a 1992 survey Jones conducted, Alexandria 
cable subscribers would like to receive WGN. Jones, how- 
ever, does not carry it. 

I declare under penaity of perjury under the laws of 
the United States of America that the foregoing is true 
and correct. Executed on 4/28/93. 


/s/ Vivian M. Horner 
Dr. Vivian M. HORNER

AFFIDAVIT OF THOMAS w. HAZLETT 
Thomas W. Hazlett deposes and says: 


1. I am an Associate Professor at the University of 
California, Davis, where I teach economics and public 
policy. During the 1991-1992 academic year I served as 
Chief Economist of the Federal Communications Com- 
misison. During the 1990-1991 academic year I was a 
Visiting Scholar at the Columbia Institute for Tele- 
Information at the Columbia University Graduate School 
of Business. 

2. I received my Ph.D. in economics from UCLA in 
1984 and have taught a variety of courses in micro- 
economics, finance, and public policy at the graduate and 
undergraduate levels. I have researched and written nu- 
merous academic articles on competitive and regulatory 
issues in the cable and broadcast television industries. 
The Appendix to this affidavit includes a list of my pub- 
lications relevant to this affidavit. 


Summary 


3. In this affidavit I explain the fundamental changes 
that have transformed the cable industry in the years 
since the FCC promulgated the CATV ban in 1970 and 
even since 1984, when Congress codified it. The original 
ban dates back to a time when cable was still a fledgling 
industry, thought to be highly susceptible to dominance 
by telephone companies through their control of utility 


174 


poles and other rights-of-way needed for coaxial cable 
used to provide cable service. But today, following the 
passage of the Pole Attachment Act and two decades of 
sheltered growth, the cable industry has become a power- 
ful monopolist in its own right, exercising market power 
over viewers and extracting monopoly profits that reduce 
the general flow of video programming. In these circum- 
stances, permitting entry by the telephone companies can 
only lead to lower prices and increased output (i.e., to 
more consumer choice and additional communication 
services ). 


The Existing Cable Companies Have Monopolized 
Local Video Distribution 


4. Cable television systems generally operate as 
monopolies in the local markets in which they serve. 
These monopolies developed into dominant distributors 
of video programming largely over the last decade. 

5. The cable industry was born in Mahanoy City, 
Pennsylvania in 1948. In the 1950s, the FCC twice 
declined to regulate cable systems as common carriers.’ 

6. In 1970, the FCC decided to shelter the infant 
cable industry from telephone company competition. Ac- 
cordingly, the FCC prohibited telephone companies from 
providing CATV service. The FCC also took steps to 
facilitate cable company access to telephone company poles 
and conduits.’ 

7. Congress followed with the federal Pole Attachment 
Act of 1978.2 This legislation authorized the FCC, in 
combination with state regulation, to regulate the rates 


1See Lucas Powe, Jr., American Broadcasting and the First 
Amendment 218-220 (1987). 


2See generally Siegel, The History of Cable Television Pole 
Attachment Regulation, 6 Comm. and the Law 9 (Dec. 1984). 


Pub. Law No. 95-284, Communications Act Amendments of 
1978 (Feb. 6, 1978), codified in 47 U.S.C. § 224. 


175 


cable companies were charged for pole access.‘ The Com- 
mission established a mathematical formula for determin- 
ing “just and reasonable” attachment rates,® and set up a 
procedure to encourage private resolution of most dis- 
putes.” Access remains, in principle, a matter of contract 
negotiation.’ But mutually satisfactory contracts were soon 
hammered out.* 


8. Cable television developed rapidly in the late 1970s 
and early 1980s. Major cable networks such as ESPN 
(1979), Nickelodeon (1979), and CNN (1980) were 
launched. Cable subscribership began to grow rapidly. 
Table 1. 


9. A further impetus to investment in cable plant and 
in satellite networks was provided by the 1984 Cable 
Act, which deregulated cable rates, fully unleashing the 
industry to compete against broadcast television. Con- 
gress also incorporated into the Act a revised version of 
the FCC’s ban on cable service. 


*“[{T]he Commission shall regulate the rates, terms, and condi- 
tions for pole attachments to provide that such rates, terms, and 
conditions are just and reasonable * * *.” 47 U.S.C § 224(b) (1). 
The Aet also provided that a state would not be considered to be 
regulating pole attachments unless it had issued rules and was 
acting promptly on complaints. /d. at § 224(c) (8). 


® Subpart J. Pole Attachment Complaint Procedures, 47 U.S.C. 
§ 1.1409(c).

* See FCC v. Florida Power Corp., 480 U.S. 245, 251 n.6 (1987). 
“CATV industry representatives” had testified “that access to 
utility poles does not in itself constitute a problem.” §S. Rep. 
No. 580, at 16. 


® National Cable Television Ass’n Vv. FCC, 747 F.2d 1508, 1505, 
1506 & n.1 (D.C. Cir. 1984). See also Telephone Company-Cable 
Television Cross-Ownership Rules, §§ 63.54-63.58, Telco Notice of 
Inquiry, 2 F.C.C. Red 5092, 5093-5094 (1987) (discussing ameliorat- 
ing effect of the Pole Attachment Act). 


176 


10. The growth in distribution plant has also been 
dramatic. In 1970 cable was available to less than 10 
percent of all American television households.’ By 1984 
it passed approximately 72 percent of all television house- 
holds. Cable now passes 96 percent of television house- 
holds. Subscribership to cable increased from less than 7 
percent of all U.S. homes in 1970 to nearly 44 percent 
in 1984. By 1992, about 60 percent of U.S. households 
subscribed.” Programming distributed principally over 
cable systems began in 1972 with HBO. There were four 
national cable networks in existence in 1976. By 1984 
this number had increased to 49 and by 1992 to 78. 
Table 2. 

11. The cable industry benefitted enormously from de- 
regulation. Capital values in the industry grew; average 
selling prices for cable systems rose from an average of 
$948 per subscriber in 1984 to $2,291 per subscriber 
in 1989. Table 3. While macroeconomic factors flattened 
asset values generally in 1990-1992, recent 1993 cable 
transactions indicate that per subscriber transactions are 
again well over $2,000. 

12. It is now clear that by the late 1980s the cable 
industry had acquired substantial market power over the 
local distribution of video programming. The best evi- 
dence of this monopolistic profitability is found in the 
economic “Q-ratios.” In a competitive industry, buyers 
won't pay much more to buy facilities than it would cost 
to build them from scratch. Thus, in a competitive in- 
dustry, the market value of an asset is generally approxi- 
mated by its replacement cost of capital. The Q-ratio 
is an economic measure based on this logic. The ratio 
is defined as: 


Q—Market Value/Replacement Cost of Tangible 
Capital 


* Stip. 17. 
© Stip. 17. 


177 


13. In the cable television industry, Q-ratios have 
been estimated at between 2.5 and 6." These estimates 
are supported by basic facts. The typical cable network 
costs about $619 per subscriber to build.“ Yet investors 
buying cable assets over the past five years have paid 
about $2,000 per subscriber, or about three times the 
price that a highly competitive firm would attract in the 
marketplace. In fact, the average Q-ratio for the firms 
listed on the New York Stock Exchange in February 
1990 was only .85."% A 1990 Brookings Institute study 
of 20 industries over the 1961-1985 time period found 
an average Q-ratio of 1.28 throughout the 25-year time 
frame, and just one industry with a Q-ratio of more than 
2. And, in a study of Q-ratios conducted for the United 
States Telephone Association, Paul MacAvoy estimated 
that Q-ratios for GTE and the seven Regional Bell Op- 
erating Companies (including their cellular holdings ) 
ranged from 1.1 to 1.3, with a weighted average of 
ae

178 


14. When cable companies face competition in their 
service areas, their monopoly power dissipates. Over the 
1988-1992 period, there are twenty-one recorded sales 
transactions for cable companies in competitive (duopolis- 
tic) markets. They average 15-20 percent less on a per 
subscriber basis than contemporaneous sales of cable sys- 
tems in monopoly markets. Table 4. 

15. In response to widespread dissatisfiaction with ris- 
ing cable prices, poor customer service, and complaints 
concerning monopolistic practices, the U.S. Congress en- 
acted a cable re-regulation bill in 1992.” 

16. The likely effects of this bill can be debated.” 
What is abundantly clear, however, is that virtually all the 
major policy discussion about the video distribution in- 
dustry now centers on how to constrain cable’s market 
power. Public concerns about cable's competitive viabil- 


ity have, due to changing market conditions, become en- 
tirely obsolete. 


Head-to-Head Cable Rivalry Lowers Prices 
and Increases Output for Consumers 


17. Compelling evidence of cable’s power to raise rates 
due to a monopoly market structure comes from those 
few communities that have permitted and enjoyed head- 
to-head cable competition. There, prices are reliably 
lower and output (measured by subscribership or num- 
ber of channels offered) is higher. In data released by 
the FCC in February 1993, 46 cable systems in head-to- 


16 Pub. Law No. 102-885 (Oct. 5, 1992), codified in 47 U.S.C. 
§ 521. 


17] have argued elsewhere that the “reregulation” portion of 
the 1992 Act is not likely to be effective, although the rules 
attempting to open up local markets to competition are a step 
in the right direction. See T. Hazlett, In Cable War, Consumers 
Get Snagged, Wall St. J., Oct. 2, 1992 (op-ed page) ; T. Hazlett, 
Cable Reregulation: The Episodes You Didn't See on C-SPAN, 
Regulation Magazine, 1993 (forthcoming). 


179 


head competitive markets were found to have charged 
basic prices of just $0.41 per channel, contrasted to a 
rate of $0.65 per channel on monopoly systems. Cable 
systems that faced competition featured both lower basic 
cable rates and more channels. Table 5. 

18. Several earlier surveys have found similar results. 
In 1990, the FCC surveyed a number of competitive sys- 
tems, and found that their rates were 34 percent below 
monopoly system prices on a per channel basis.“ The 
Commission was led to conclude that “direct competition 
has resulted in reduced per channel rates for cable ser- 
vice.” "" In a 1990 econometric study, Levin & Meisel 
found basic cable prices from 22 percent to 30 percent 
lower for a service which “typically includes more chan- 
nels.” And, while difficult to measure, narrative ac- 
counts strongly suggest that customer service in competi- 
tive markets is also superior.” 

19. Data from Paul Kagan Associates further sup- 
port the price-lowering effect of head-to-head rivalry. In 
an April 1992 survey of 103 systems competing in 50 
separate cable markets and serving 1.13 million house- 
holds (about 2 percent of the U.S. cable total), the aver- 
age revenue per subscriber was $25.08,” while the aver- 
age number of channels offered on basic was 40. The 


% FCC Cable Report, 5 F.C.C. Red 4962, at App. H-2. 
19 Td. at 5014. 


2 Stanford L. Levin & John B. Meisel, Cable Television and 
Telecommunications : Theory, Evidence and Policy, Telecommuni- 
cations Policy, Dec. 1990, at 519-528. 


*1Daniel R. Levine, Behind the Cable Rip-off: It’s Time to Pull 
the Plug on Price-Gouging Monopolies and Get a Little Healthy 
Competition into the Picture, Reader's Digest, June 1992; Mark 
Lewyn & Julie Ampano Lopez, More Choice for Cable TV?, Busi- 
ness Week, May 13, 1991. 


*2 Kagan breaks down the data into “incumbent” and “over- 
builder” categories. The figures used here are equally weighted

average monthly revenue per subscriber for all U.S. cable 
systems in 1992 was $32.12 (Table 1) and the number 
of basic channels offered was under 35 (Table 5), im- 
plying that competitive systems were pricing their total 
service package about 21.9 percent lower, while provid- 
ing greater viewing choices. Table 6. 

20. Incumbent cable companies respond dramatically 
to new competition. When an entrant begins constructing 
a competing system, the incumbent typically cuts sub- 
scriber rates just in those areas (literally, the blocks) 
where the competitor has placed its lines. Prices have 
been dramatically lower in areas where rivalry has been 
in evidence, a sign that prices prior to entry were above 
competitive levels. Telesat Cablevision, for example, en- 
tered the cable market against entrenched competitors 
in Orange County, Florida. In April 1992, the prices 
charged by the two firms against which Telesat competed 
(in different areas of Orange County) were far lower 
than the prices they charged in their monopoly markets. 
Table 7. 

21. In sum, the evidence is overwhelming that a 
monopoly market structure allows cable firms to charge 
consumers higher prices than otherwise. Cable’s Q- 
ratios, prices in cross-sectional studies, prices in time 
series, and system sales prices under competitive vs. mo- 
nopoly market structures all point in exactly the same 
direction: cable firms that face no direct competition 
from head-to-head cable competitors have significant mar- 
ket power which they utilize to raise rates and restrict 
output from competitive levels. 


Jones Intercable Enjoys a Monopoly Position 
in Alexandria, Virginia 


22. Jones Intercable enjoys a market position in Alex- 
andria, Virginia typical of cable franchisees elsewhere in 
the country. As the sole cable television system in the 
city, it enjoys a Q ratio significantly above 1. In fact, 


181 


in an intracompany transfer in 1992, Jones paid $2,134 
per subscriber.” The purchase price is clearly in excess 
of capital replacement costs, particularly considering that 
the density of households is more than twice the national 
average (thus reducing plant investment per subscriber ). 

23. Jones’ rates in Alexandria, where it does not face 
head-to-head cable competition, are significantly higher 
than in Anne Arundel, Maryland, a market in which 
Jones does face a direct cable competitor.“ The monthly 
price of the basic cable package in Alexandria is $24.65, 
and includes 44 channels of programming. In Anne 
Arundel, Jones charges $21.20, and offers subscribers 
either 43 or 53 channels, depending upon where they 
live.” This indicates that prices in the competitive market 
are between 8 percent and 29 percent lower on a per- 
channel basis. Such downward pressure on prices reliably 
expands the consumption of any product. The relative 
cable subscribership rates in Alexandria and Anne Arun- 
del are consistent with this. Cable subscribership in Alex- 
andria is only 57 percent, which is less than the national 
average. In Anne Arundel County, cable subscribership 
is over 70 percent, well above the national average.” 

24. A comparison of the prices Jones charges in Alex- 
andria with national averages compiled by the FCC (see 
Table 4) reveals that Jones charges about 37 percent 
more per channel for basic services in Alexandria than 
do cable companies that face head-to-head competition. 
This despite the fact that the density of households in 


*8 Dean Witter Reynolds, Company Report—Jones Intercable 
Investor, L.P. 4 (Oct. 22, 1992). The report claims that the 
“(p)rice paid for Alexandria Systems seems low.” Ibid.

*5 Stip. 34.

Alexandria is more than twice the national average, which 
substantially reduces plant investment per subscriber. 


Direct Cable Competition is Financially Viable 

25. Cable monopolies dominate local video markets 
not due to an efficient market structure, which realizes 
scale economies for the benefit of consumers. Quite the 
contrary: cable companies currently charge prices far in 
excess of their average costs, as well as the likely costs 
of competitors. New entrants could charge lower prices 
and still realize profits. Head-to-head cable competition 
is viable in most local cable markets at existing prices, 
and even at prices much reduced from current monopoly 
levels. 

26. Video programming currently available vastly ex- 
ceeds the carrying capacities of existing cable systems. 
There are roughly 80 national programming networks 
currently available.” In total, over 150 channels of cable 
programming are now available.” (There would be an 
even larger number of such services on the market if 
cable were more competitive at the local distribution 
level.) Yet, of the 11,083 cable systems operating nation- 
wide, only 1,152 systems or 10 percent of these systems 
carry more than 53 channels; over half the systems offer 
30-53 channels; the remaining 35 percent offer fewer 
than 30 channels.” 

27. My 1992 financial analysis of “overbuild” com- 
petition in cable sought to gauge the financial viability 
of competition in a typical U.S. cable market. I esti- 
mated expected rates of return for a new competitive 
cable system entering a market already served by one 
cable system, and possessing the following characteristics: 


27 NCTA, Cable Television Developments 7-A (Mar. 1993). 
28 Extra!, Satellite Orbit Magazine, Mar. 1993. 
2° NCTA, Cable Television Developments 10-A (Mar. 19938). 


183 


50,000 homes; density (homes per mile of cable plant) 
of the U.S. average; revenue per subscriber of the U.S. 
average; subscribership of the U.S. average (with no in- 
crease attributable to competition); aerial to underground 
wiring ratio equal to the national average; revenue growth 
of 5 percent per year.” I assumed the firm’s capital costs 
to be those listed as standard in a 1986 paper from the 
U.S. Department of Justice (the quality-adjusted cost of 
physical capital has been declining over time), operating 
costs to be 57.1 percent of revenues (the national aver- 
age), and that the new entrant would garner a 50 percent 
market share but that no additional households would 
subscribe to cable (i.e., the new firm would serve 50 per- 
cent of existing cable customers). Based upon this analy- 
sis, @ new competitor could expect a return of 18 percent 
at average U.S. rate levels existing in 1991. Even allow- 
ing for a 20 percent fall in rates due to competition (and 
a corresponding increase in subscribership of 20 percent), 
a new entrant earns 16 percent. Since the model uses 
conservative assumptions,” and both numbers are above 
the cost of capital, this indicates that the typical cable 


market is financially capable of supporting two rival cable 
firms. 


Direct Cable Competition is Financially Viable 
in Alexandria, Virginia 


28. Alexandria, Virginia is particularly attractive from 
the standpoint of cable competition. The key variable to 
focus on in assessing financial viability is revenue per 
mile of plant: cable entrants must be able to anticipate 


” Thomas W. Hazlett, Teleco Entry Into Video, paper presented 
to the Public Utility Research Conference, University of Florida 
(Apr. 1992). 


*! The cable industry, for instance, anticipates revenue growth 
above 5 percent; competition does increase subscribership, etc. 


184 


sufficient cash flows to sustain their fixed investments, 
largely constituted by laying the transport grid (i.e., 
cables). Alexandria cable consumers now pay an aver- 
age of $34.92 per month, well above the 1992 U.S. aver- 
age of $32.12 (see Table 1). Moreover, the density of 
the market, 244 homes per mile, is two and a half times 
the U.S. average of 93. These two factors combine to 
make this particular market a likely candidate for head- 
to-head cable competition. 

29. The 1990 Annual Report of Jones Intercable says 
virtually as much itself, in describing its Alexandria, Vir- 
ginia market in glowing terms: 

Alexandria, Virginia * * * is a relatively stable, 
affluent community with excellent demographics, in- 
cluding a high rate of employment and one of the 
highest levels of population density in the Washing- 
ton metropolitan area. The current population of 
Alexandria is approximately 112,000, with a median 
household income above the national average at ap- 
proximately $42,600. More than 75 percent of Alex- 
andria residents are employed in white-collar occu- 
pations, with 40 percent of those employed working 
in managerial and professional positions. Histori- 
cally, these demographics have proven to be highly 
favorable for cable subscriptions and penetration 
rates.” 


Telephone Companies are Important Potential 
Competitors in the Cable Market 


30. Competition is economically possible in cable mar- 
kets, but cable companies have proven very adept at 
blocking it. As noted above, favored cable strategies 
include aggressive and highly selective price discrimina- 


32 Jones Intercable, 1990 Annual Report 6 (Dec. 31, 1990). 


, 


185 


tion, exclusive contracts with programmers, and monop- 
olization of the franchising process.“ 

31. In a market occupied by such powerful and ag- 
gressive incumbents, widespread competition must feature 
new entrants which begin with both technical sophistica- 
tion and substantial financial resources. The telephone 
companies are the most likely such competitors. Certainly 
this has been the view of the cable incumbents, which 
have routinely targeted retention of the telco video pro- 
gramming ban as their top legislative priority.” 

32. There are no technical barriers to head-to-head 
competition in the provision of video programming be- 
tween telcos and cable providers. Cable does not utilize 
airwaves that are parcelled out by a regulatory body. 
Competing cables can be laid side by side. In fact, on a 
common carrier network such as C&P has indicated that it 
intends to build, competing video programs can be carried 
on the same wire. 

33. Moreover, the 1978 Pole Attachment Act has 
fulfilled its purpose. U.S. cable markets have been wired 
by independent (non-telco) companies, including the Alex- 
andria, Virginia market. Most utility poles and conduits 
are owned not by telephone companies but by other utili- 
ties,” and these utilities may and do provide cable tele- 


* A litany of abusive practices employed by cable incumbents,

re Competition, Rate Deregulation and the Commission’s Policies 
Relating to the Provision of Cable Service, MM Dkt. No. 89-600 
(F.C.C. Mar. 1, 1990).

Franchising, Aug. 31, 1988, at 2.

telephone companies.”); in Alexandria, Virginia, C&P provides 


186 


vision service themselves,” in competition with cable com- 
panies. Municipalities, which control all public rights 
of way over and under the streets, are likewise permitted 
to operate their own cable systems,” and many do.” None- 
theless, independent cable companies have successfully 
deployed their own cables, even against this class of favor- 
ably positioned potential competitors. 


Competition Against Cable Monopolists Would 
Lower Price, Increase Output, and Increase 
Diversity in Video Programming 


34. There is today no plausible economic scenario 
according to which telco competition against incumbent 
cable monopolists would raise price or reduce the total 
amount of video programming offered to consumers. Com- 
petition reliably lowers prices for cable customers and 
thereby enlists greater numbers of subscribers; this con- 
stitutes an increase in diversity of programming as cable 
subscribers have access to many more video choices than 
non-cable customers. | 


35. In 1970, when the FCC first prohibited telephone 
companies from providing CATV service, cable was an 
infant industry. The FCC assumed (not unreasonably 
at the time) that by sheltering that industry from telco 
competition it could promote the development of a com- 
peting distribution notework. 


2,744 poles and VEPCO, the local electric utility, provides 4,639 
poles. Stip. 24.

36. The new network has since developed, more suc- 
cessfully than the FCC might have imagined in 1970. 
Indeed, the cable industry has passed competitive matur- 
ity: it now monopolizes local video distribution. 

37. As technology advances, the separation of tele- 
phone and video service becomes increasingly artificial. 
The marketplace surely reflects this, as cable companies 
are now actively seeking to provide voice telephone ser- 
vice in competition with existing telephone companies. For 
example, cable companies are rapidly building alliances 
with “competitive access providers” (CAPs), who run in- 
dependent, usually fiber-optic, networks, typically through 
major urban centers, connecting businesses to each other 
and to long distance carriers. Overall, cable interests now 
control over 50 percent of CAP revenues.” At the same 
time, cable companies account for more of the experi- 
mental “personal communications service” (PCS) licenses 
issued by the FCC than all seven Regional Bell com- 
panies combined.“ Jones Lightwave Ltd., an affiliate of 
Jones Intercable, has FCC experimental licenses for PCS 
trials in Alexandria, Virginia, and in Illinois, Florida,

Stip. 49.

through a cable television system in February 1992; many cable 
companies are using FCC experimental licenses to test PCS. The
