and Colorado.** TCI, the nation’s largest cable operator, 
predicts that cable systems will be capable of providing 
telephone service by the mid to late 1990s, using up- 
grades to cable networks that are already being deployed. 

38. With the current baseline of monopoly prevailing 
in the overwhelming number of cable markets, as well 
as in the specific market of Alexandria, Virginia, allowing 
a new entrant to compete cannot lessen consumer choice. 
This is true even if one assumes the worst: after some 
period of intense competition, a telco entrant eventually 
displaces the current monopolist. While highly unlikely 
(and remediable by several alternative means other than 
a blanket ban on video programming by telcos) even this 
worst case entails expanded consumer choice during the 
rivalrous stage, and no lessened choice in settling back 
to a monopolistic Square One. Because of the prevalence 
of sole supplier cable markets, we cannot do worse than 
we presently do. 

39. The only relevant questions, then, are: How 
much will choice improve with new entry? And for how 
long? 

40. The evidence on the first question is unmistakable: 
wherever and whenever competition does come about, 
consumer choices improve dramatically. First, prices fall, 
allowing more consumers to avail themselves of the video 
choices of the new technology. Second, competition sup- 
plies more video programming, more choice, more diver- 
sity. This is revealed in the substantially higher number 


‘1 Stip. 56; FCC Experimental License Nos. 2429-EX-PL-92, 
2430-EX-PL-92, 2431-EX-PL-92, 2482-EX-PL-92. 


42 Joanne Kelley, Tele-Comm Unfurls $2.0 Billion Network Plan, 
Reuters, Apr. 1, 1993. One cable company executive even claims 
that cable companies will soon offer basic cable service free to 
households that would otherwise decline to subscribe in order to 
promote the sale of telephone and other services. Record CAB 
Attendance, Communications Daily, Apr. 8, 1992, at 3 (quoting 
Amos Hostetter, Chairman of Continental Cablevision).

i 
: 
é

189 


of program channels which competitive cable systems 
provide. 

41. The cable industry’s argument is that these bene- 
ficial effects will not endure in the long run. That is 
uncompelling in that some competitive period is preferred 
to none. Moreover, both economic theory and real world 
experience indicate that competition can and will endure. 

42. The incumbent cable monopolists have invested in 
long-lived physical plant. Many are upgrading to fiber, 
which will last even longer. In economic jargon, these 
investments are already “sunk.” Once the money has 
been spent, it is largely unsalvageable. Looking forward, 
then, the future cost of these facilities is close to zero. 
New entry by telcos cannot possibly deter competition by 
others when that long term commitment has already been 
made. The only realistic exit from the market, once 
significant costs have been sunk, is via merger—and that 
would take an explicit approval by the antitrust authori- 
ties. 

43. Cable industry reaction to possible telephone 
company entry into video offers powerful testimony to the 
likely long-lived impact of telco-cable competition. Na- 
tionally, the cable industry is quickly upgrading its facili- 
ties, particularly with fiber optic investments, to prepare 
for the possibility of the telephone company competition 
they fear. The cable companies are thus signalling that 
they believe the entry of telcos into the market would 
result in more competition which justifies the sinking of 
durable capital. Indeed, since the filing of the lawsuit of 
which this affidavit is a part, Jones has had informal dis- 
cussions with the City of Alexandria wherein it has of- 
fered to make additional investments in channel capacity 
in Alexandria if it receives a 20-25 year non-exclusive 
franchise.“ 

44. We cannot do any worse than monoply. There 
can be a debate over the magnitude of consumer im- 


#8 Stip. 39. 


190 


provement with new entry, but not of the direction. 
Prices will fali some amount, for some period. Choices 
will increase, by some amount for some time. There is 
simply no dispute: when one begins with monopoly, new 
entry can only increase the amount of consumer choice.

JOINT STIPULATIONS OF FACT 


For purposes, and solely for purposes, of this litigation, 
the parties, through undersigned counsel, hereby stipulate 
to the following facts: 


A. Stipulations Proffered By Plaintiffs ' 


1. The Chesapeake and Potomac Telephone Company 
of Virginia (C&P) is a wholly owned subsidiary of Bell 
Atlantic Corporation. Bell Atlantic is privately owned 
and has over a million shareholders. 

2. C&P is one of the telephone companies in the 
United States that is subject to common carrier regula- 
tion under title II of the Communications Act of 1934 
and also state laws. 

3. Fiber optic technology is capable of providing tele- 
phone service and transmitting video programming on an 
integrated basis directly to subscribers. 

4. In 1984, no telephone company provided transport 
of video programming to subscribers over its telephone 
lines. 

5. C&P carries the video programming of third par- 
ties at the Cascades, a development in Loudoun County,

been identified, the parties acknowledge that each stipulation herein 
was subject to negotiation and modification by all parties prior to 
agreement.

pursuant to the FCC’s Order and Authorization, File No.

W-P-C-6834 (Mar. 25, 1993). 

6. As an information service provider, C&P provides 
its own voice and data messages, and messages of its 
affiliates, to the public. These services include, for ex- 
ample, time of day and weather services, Enhanced 911 
service, and home shopping services. 

7. Bell Atlantic Video Services Company (BVS) is 
an affiliate of C&P, incorporated in Virginia on Septem- 
ber 24¢1992> for the purpose of providing video pro- 
gramming to the public. 

8. In 1991-1992, 43 percent of viewing time in house- 
holds that subscribe to cable was devoted to basic cable 
program networks and pay cable services.

works in existence; in 1984, there were 49 national cable 
video networks; in 1992, there were 78 national cable 
video networks. In 1984, expenditures by cable systems 
to obtain programming were approximately $1.74 billion; 
in 1992, cable system expenditures to obtain program- 
ming were approximately $3.8 billion. 

10. According to FCC, Office of Plans and Policies, 
Broadcast Television in a Multichannel Marketplace,

daily newspapers.

12. Statistics compiled by the Census Bureau show

14. According to Sara Fritz & Dwight Morris, Hand-

gressional Races 53, 54 (Congressional Quarterly 1992), 
in the 1990 election, 29 percent of the money spent by 
Congressional candidates went for radio and television 
advertising and media consultants. On average, radio 
and television advertising accounted for 35 percent of the 
money spent by Senate candidates and 23 percent of the 
money spent by House candidates. Congressional candi- 
dates spent about $119 million on broadcast advertising 
in the 1990 election.

1984 violate its First Amendment rights. Time Warner 
Entertainment Corp., L.P. v. FCC, Civil Action No. 
92-2494 (D.D.C.).

Alexandria, which included 27 channels of programming,

Company-Cable Television C ross-Ownership Rules, 7

FCC Red 5781, 5848 (1992), and the Census Bureau,

holds. Cable now passes approximately 96% of televi- 
sion households, and approximately 60% of television 
households subscribe. 

18. In Alexandria, Jones Intercable, which is pres-

subscribe. 

19. Currently, the supply of available video program- 
ming exceeds the available channel capacity of almost 
all cable systems. 

20. Cable systems presently have an average capacity 
of about 40 channels. In 1992, 88% of cable systems 
had fewer than 54 channels; however, approximately 35 
percent of all subscribers were served by systems with 54 
or more channels. 

21. In Alexandria, Jones Intercable presently has a 
capacity of 51 channels. 

22. In October 1979, C&P entered into an agreement 
with a predecessor to Jones which provided for the place- 
ment of cable facilities on C&P poles and in C&P con- 
duit in Alexandria. This agreement was assigned to 
Jones when it acquired the cable system in 1986. The 
Alexandria cable system Jones acquired had been de- 
ployed sometime prior to Jones’s acquisition; C&P has 
not provided any additional pole attachments for this 
system since 1984; and, since Jones acquired the system 
in 1986, Jones has not asked C&P to provide any new 
pole attachments.

do telephone companies.

U.S. Of these communities, 38 also have a municipally- 
owned electric system. 

26. Florida Power & Light Co. currently operates a 
cable system in the same locality where it provides elec- 
tric utility service. 

27. According to the Census Bureau, annual cable 
operator revenues in 1970 were $345 million. In 1976, 
annual cable operator revenues from subscriber services 
including advertising were approximately $933 million. 
In 1984, annual cable operator revenues were approxi- 
mately $7.8 billion. In 1992, annual cable operator reve- 
nues were approximately $21.5 billion. 

28. Of the approximately 10,000 communities served 
by cable, as of 1991, 53 communities had more than one 
competing cable system in the same locality. 

29. Some cable systems serving individual communi- 
ties are owned by national or regional chains, known as 
multiple system operators (MSOs). The five largest 
MSOs combined serve 41.4% of all cable subscribers. 

30. According to the Newspaper Association of Amer- 
ica, Facts about Newspapers "92 at 26 (1992): Some 
newspapers serving individual communities are also 
owned by national or regional chains. Daily newspapers 
are published in 1,513 cities. One hundred and thirty- 
three newspaper groups own 1,213 daily newspapers, or 
about 75% of all U.S. daily newspapers. Eighty-two 
groups own five or fewer daily newspapers; 51 groups 
own six or more daily newspapers. There is competition

40. Currently, both coaxial cable and optical fiber 
are used for wireline transmission of cable system video 
signals. Previously, only coaxial cable was used for this 
purpose. In general, multiple lines of coaxial cable or 
fiber optics can be placed next to each other without 
mutual interference to video signals they carry. This 
means competing cable transmission systems can exist 
side-by-side without such interference. 

41. Originally, cable systems were primarily designed 
to provide one-way video signals. The newest cable Sys- 
tem architecture is capable of supporting two-way tele- 
phone and data services. 

42. According to the NCTA, looking solely at sub- 
scriber plant (i.e., not counting connections between ex- 
changes or adjacent cities), in 1991 the regional Bell tele- 
phone companies installed approximately 8685 miles of 
fiber optic cable, while cable companies installed approx- 
imately 6408 miles of fiber optic cable. In a speech at 
the 1992 NCTA annual convention, the President of the 
NCTA stated that “Cable is now installing optical fiber 
at an annual rate that is 74 percent of what the RBOCs 
are doing. And we're gaining fast.” 

43. In a speech at the 1992 NCTA annual convention, 
the President of the NCTA stated that “[t]he biggest tele- 
communications story of the decade may turn out to be 


202 


not telcos’ creating broadband networks, but cable tech- 
nology and architecture proving to be the most efficient 
means of delivering the next generation of communica- 
tions services.”

46. In 1990, approximately 30% of the operating rev-

distance carriers at the originating and terminating ends 
of long distance calls. 

47. So-called Competitive Access Providers (“CAPs”) 
have entered the interstate access market in recent years, 
serving the needs of large, communications-intensive busi- 
nesses, predominantly in metropolitan centers, that gener- 
ate high volumes of long-distance telephone traffic. CAPs 
generally deploy fiber-optic facilities or, in some cases, 
microwave systems. It is estimated that the CAPs have 
invested hundreds of millions of dollars in their facilities. 

48. Metropolitan Fiber Services, a CAP, competes with 
C&P in portions of Northern Virginia for the provision 
of interstate access services to the types of customers re- 
ferred to in Stipulation 47.

52. The FCC licenses wireless telecommunications 
services. In the 1980's, the FCC licensed cellular mobile 
telephone services which overcame the need for wired 
connections between a subscriber and a telephone com- 
pany switching center. The FCC licensed radio spectrum 
to two competing providers in each cellular service area. 
By regulation, one of the two competing providers in- 
itially was the local wireline telephone exchange carrier. 

53. There are now 11 million subscribers in the United 
States to cellular service and their number continues to 
grow. In 1992, the number of cellular subscribers grew 
by over 40 percent. The cellular industry is now convert- 
ing to digital technologies that will expand the carrying 
capacity of their systems several-fold.

89. ADSL technology, which was developed in ap- 
proximately 1991, has never been used on a commercial 
basis to provide video programming directly to subscribers. 

90. As far as the parties are aware, ADSL’s capa- 
bility to provide video programming directly to subscrib- 
ers has never been tested under real-world conditions over 
a telephone network, prior to C&P’s test being conducted 
in Northern Virginia pursuant to the FCC’s Order and 
Authorization, File No. W-P-C-6834 (Mar. 25, 1993). 

91. Currently, ADSL technology requires that an 
entire program be digitally coded and compressed before 
it can be made available for viewing by subscribers. 

92. Digital compression and encoding equipment is 
used to prepare video programming for transmission over 
ADSL-equipped copper loops. Currently, C&P has no 
equipment capable of compressing and encoding video 
programming on a real-time basis. This means, for ex- 
ample, that using ADSL, C&P currently could not pro- 
vide live sports or news programs to subscribers at the 
same time such programs would be available to viewers 
of broadcast or non-ADSL dependent cable television. 

93. Currently, one telephone line using ADSL tech- 
nology can deliver only one video signal into the home 
at a time. If more than one television set is hooked up 
to a single ADSL-equipped telephone line, all sets will be 
limited to showing the same program. Additional ADSL 
equipped telephone lines to the home are required in 
order to watch different programs on different sets at the 
same time. With conventional cable technology, a single 
coaxial cable carries multiple channels of programming 


214 


into the home; multiple television sets can show different 


programs. 

94. ADSL technology is not currently capable of pro- 
viding high-definition television. 

95. C&P may or may not charge a fee to subscribers 
for installation of commercial ADSL service. 

96. ADSL transmission technology, the specifications 
for which were developed by Bellcore, is available to 
Regional Bell Operating Companies other than Bell 
Atlantic.

Justice antitrust suit and resulting divestiture was the

Bell monopoly.* The heart of that monopoly was the

(MFJ) dealt with that problem in two basic ways: first, 
it separated the local exchange service from nonlocal 
(interLATA) telephone services; second, it prohibited the 
local exchange companies—the Bell Operating Compa- 
nies (BOCs) from providing competitive information

“Ijn the instance of the Regional Companies [ Bel] Operating 
Companies], their market power stems from their sti 
almost complete domination over the “last mile” of the tele

switches without which few, if any, competitors can reach

costs, and they could raise such costa by virtue of the depend 
ence of their rivals’ information services on local networt’

ference that the problem is extraordinarily challenging— 
sufficiently so that reasonable minds (in and out of Con-

deal with it. The slightest glance at the rules, and the 
Commission’s seemingly endless outpouring of opinions 
explaining them,” will show that monitoring this mare’s 
nest of complex regulations will require close regulatory 
attention. 


21 Under ONA, the BOCs are required to offer an unbundled set

rules, and nondiscrimination reporting requirements. Network

11. My own experience as a participant in the Com- 
mission’s efforts to wrestle with these problems in the 
mid-1970’s was that regulation achieved only limited suc- 
cess in preventing AT&T from practices that handicapped 
access to iis facilities, and not much more success in 
preventing cross-subsidization of its services. And that 
success, such as it was, was achieved only at a large cost, 
to the agency, to competitors and ultimately to the pub- 
lic. It is difficult to convey a sense of this frustration 
merely by referring to particular controversies over, say, 
access arrangements or competitive pricing disputes.” 
The problem was too pervasive to be captured by any 
single illustration. In part the problem was one of AT&T 
constantly seeking to test if not bend the limits of agency 
standards, the effect of which was to put the Commis- 
sion under a constant burden of surveillance. The prob- 
lem of coping with the sheer volume of information 
(much of it unreliable) and argument (much of it 
rhetoric) was exacerbated by a basic uncertainty as to 
how the standards should be implemented.”

Execunet dispute; see MCI Telecommunications v. FCC, 561 F.2d 
365 (D.C. Cir. 1977) (reversing FCC decision); and the disputes 
over competitive service tariffs, such as the Hi-Lo tariff, AT&T 
Co., 58 F.C.C.2d 362 (1976). Despite an abundance of rules and 
rulings these types of disputes were a continuous source of vexa- 
tion to the Commission, and to the parties adversely affected by 
them, until Computer II.

12. The regulatory environment of the 1990's offers a 
replay of the 1970’s. As in the 1970's there is, first, the 
task of ensuring that competitive service providers have 
nondiscriminatory access to basic network services and 
information controlled by the telcos, and, second, that of 
ensuring a “level playing ficld” for all information service 
providers by preventing cross-subsidization by the telcos 
of competitive information services. ONA purports to 
deal with the first task but, as the Ninth Circuit recently 
held, does nothing to prevent the second.” 

13. The cross-subsidization problem was an important 
concern in 1970, and in 1984; it remains one today. My 
experience with the Commission’s efforts to deal with the 
problem in the mid1970’s leads me to challenge the FCC’s 
claim to be able to police this problem with account- 
ing rules” as a means of preventing cross-subsidization. 
The current cost allocation methodology is essentially 
the same (with greater liberalization in the definition of 
cost categories) as that adopted in 1976, when it first 
mandated fully distributed cost allocations.** Whether or 
not fully distributed cost methods are appropriate account- 
ing conventions—as to which there is much dispute—

factor. Yet unless the rules are quite simple and straightforward, 
the Regional Companies will be able to contend, again and again, 
that any particular rule is not applicable to whatever anticompeti- 
tive practices they may be engaged in, and to tie up enforcement 
almost indefinitely in administrative proceedings and in litigation.”

what is important in this context is that, even using the 
costing methodology most restrictive of manipulations 
promoting subsidization, the Commission did not eliminate 
the threat of cross-subsidization in the 1970's. At bottom 
the problem was one of implementation, involving detailed 
scrutiny of cost information supplied by AT&T. The 
Commission was not able to carry out this task effectively. 
It admitted as much by adopting the structural separa- 
tions requirement in Computer 11.” 

14. The reasons why the Commission’s detailed cost 
allocation system, developed after years of proceedings, 
failed to work in practice are not difficult to understand, 
and there is little reason to believe that today’s circum- 
stances differ in any significant manner. First, during the 
1970's, the Bell System often failed to provide the cost 
data required by the Commission’s rules and engaged in 
almost continuous litigation over what those regulations 
required. Second, determinations of the legality of AT&T's 
rates were hung up in administrative proceedings for 
years, with potentially harmful consequences to competi- 
tors and ratepayers. 

15. The FCC’s monitoring problem has not dis- 
appeared. If anything it has increased. Contrary to some 
popular wisdom, the agency's difficulties in monitoring 


28In adopting its price-cap regime for AT&T the Commission 
claimed it would ameliorate the problem of cross subsidization

235 


AT&T's responses to the emerging competition in the 
1970’s were only slightly a product of the latter’s huge 
size, or the disparity in resources between the regulator 
and regulated. The main cause of regulatory burden was 
the number of different transactions that had to be moni- 
tored and the number of different players who were enter- 
ing the market. Neither the transactions nor the players 
have diminished in number. The mere increase in the 
number of different regulated entities entails a nontrivial 
increase in regulatory burden. Overseeing one AT&T 
with seven regional divisions is not the same as over- 
seeing seven independent firms, each with distinctive 
services, pricing policies and corporate goals. More im- 
portant than the increased number of regulated entities, 
however, is the greater variety of active services, which in 
turn generates the need for new access arrangements be- 
tween telcos and other independent service providers, 
which in turn generates more regulatory monitoring. 

16. While the burden of the monitoring task has in- 
creased in the post-divestiture era, the FCC’s ability to 
carry that burden has not. A 1987 General Accounting 
Office study of the FCC’s plans and capabilities for con- 
trolling telco cross subsidies between competitive and 
regulated services concluded that the Commission did not 
have the requisite resources to deal with the problem. 
After noting that the elimination of structural separations 
safeguards (under the Computer III decision) put in- 
creased reliance on oversight methods, such as accounting 
audits, the GAO observed that the FCC’s staff was in- 
adequate to perform that oversight. Given agency staff 
levels and inspection schedules it was calculated that the 
major carriers or regional holding companies would be 
subject to audit “only once every 16 years.” ” The situa- 


2° GAO, TELEPHONE COMMUNICATIONS: CONTROLLING Cross SuUB- 
sIDY BETWEEN REGULATED AND COMPETITIVE SERVICES (Oct. 1987), 
p. 58. 


236 


tion has not improved since the time of the 1987 report: 
in February of this year the GAO revisited the issue and 
found no acceleration in auditing schedules an actual 
decrease in available auditors.” It should not be assumed, 
incidentally, that the slack in agency auditing is made up 
by the audits performed by CPAs hired by the telcos. 
The 1993 GAO report indicates that on-site audits by 
FCC auditors discovered “cases of misallocations total- 
ling over $300 million in which carriers charged expenses 
to the regulated side of the business and carriers’ affiliates 
had overcharged regulated carriers for services and sup- 
plies.” *' If $300 million in misallocated costs are dis- 
covered by an auditing capability that can audit carriers 
“only once very 16 years,” one can only imagine the 
magnitude of misallocations that would be discovered by 
an adequately staffed agency. This is not a small problem, 
and one must doubt that misallocations of this order of 
magnitude can be dismissed as mere bookkeeping error. 


IV. RELIABILITY OF STATE REGULATION 


17. Even assuming a more effective FCC regulatory 
policy than has been witnessed in the past years, the 
problem of guaranteeing fair competition between the 
telcos and cable systems does not rest with the FCC alone, 
as the Ninth Circuit decision in California v. FCC ™ had 
occasion recently to remind the FCC when it overturned 
the agency’s blanket preemption of state regulation of 
intrastate enhanced services. In fact the FCC is not even 
in charge of the larger part of the business that is of 
immediate concern to this case: only 30% of all local 


9% GAO, TELECOMMUNICATIONS: FCC’s OVERSIGHT EFFORTS TO 
CONTROL CROSS-SUBSIDIZATION (Feb. 1993), p. 4. 


31 Jd. at 12. 
82.905 F.2d 1217 (9th Cir. 1990). 


237 


exchange costs are regulated by the FCC; the rest is 
regulated by the states.” 

18. The division of authority between the FCC and 
the state regulators has been hotly contested for many 
years, and it still has not produced clear and stable juris- 
dictional boundaries with respect to the terms and condi- 
tions of competitive access to basic telco services or the 
pricing of telco provision of enhanced services. Sorting 
out regulatory responsibility between state and federal 
regulators has been a vexing problem. In the most ideal 
conditions of harmonious cooperation between state and 
federal regulation defining regulatory boundaries is a 
difficult matter. As it happens, however, telecommunica- 
tions regulation has not been characterized by such 
harmony. Throughout the 1970’s the FCC fought a 
two-front war against AT&T on one front and state regu- 
lators on the other over the key issues of competition. 
In part the conflict between the FCC and state regulators 
was one of regulatory turf, but beneath the turf fight was 
a more basic clash over regulatory policy. The uneasy, 
and uncertain, relationship between federal and state 
regulators increases the uncertainty of regulation gen- 
erally. In all events the unquestionably larger role that 
states now play in the telecommunications field means 
introduces new complexity to the question of how much 
reliance can be placed on regulatory oversight. 

19. It is often remarked that state regulators have 
become more sophisticated and much better endowed with 
the necessary resources since the 1970’s. I have no doubt 
this is true. If my recollection of state regulation during 
that period can be trusted, there was much room for 
improvement. But however much improvement there has 
been it does not alter the fact that regulatory policy 
across the nation is a highly variable, highly unreliable 


%3 Policy and Rules Concerning Rates for Dominant Carriers, 
4 FCC Red 2873, 2882 n.7 (1989) (First Report and Order, adopt- 
ing price caps for AT&T). 


238 


product. A survey of state regulators conducted in Octo- 
ber 1990 offers a glimpse of the problem. Of forty-five 


what more encouraging with 23 states having regulations 
designed to prevent cross subsidization.“ Of course, hav- 
ing programs to deal with the problem is meaningless 
unless the regulators have the resources to implement 
them. The resources are as variable as the programs: 
the 1990 survey found that staff assigned to telephone 
regulation ranged from one (Mississippi) to more than 
250 (New York).” Most of the states fall closer to the 
low than to the high end of this range. 


V. REGULATION, COMPETITION AND SPEECH 


20. It is not the purpose of this affidavit to discuss 
the application of First Amendment standards to eco- 
nomic regulation, but it is pertinent to take note of what 
should be obvious: that any judgment about the con- 
stitutionality of these regulations entails a close examina- 
tion of the government purposes they are intended to 
serve, not simply their incidental effects.” Plaintiffs have

businesses, as the plaintiffs contend. But the fact that 
other threats can be imagined does not alter the fact 
that, based on historical experience, only the telcos have

AFFIDAVIT 


DisTRICT OF COLUMBIA, 8.5. 
Bruce M. Owen, being duly sworn, deposes and says: 


. 

1. I am the president of Economists Incorporated, a . 
Washington DC economic consulting firm, and visit- 
ing professor of economics at Stanford University’s 
Washington DC campus, where I teach courses on 


communications. From 1979-1981 I was Ctief 
Economist at the Antitrust Division of the U.S. L'e- 
partment of Justice. Previously I was Chief Econo- 
mist at the White House Office of Telecommunica- 
tions Policy. I testified as an expert on behalf of 
the government in United States v. Western Electric, 
the antitrust lawsuit that led to the dissolution of the 
Bell System in 1984. A copy of my curriculum 
vitae appears as Appendix A to this affidavit. 


3. I believe that the restrictions on telephone company 
provisions of video services (practically, of cable 


242 


television service) were economically justified at the 
time of their adoption by the FCC in 1970, and 
remained justified when the restrictions were codified 
by Congress in 1984. Further, although the condi- 
tions justifying these restrictions are less compelling 
today than in 1984, the restrictions remain sound 
and will continue to be so for some time to come. 
My conclusions are based on, and limited to, the 
economic role of the restrictions in the context of 
United States competition policy. 


_ Restraints on the competitive activities of firms are 
unusual and should be imposed only in abnormal 
and convincing cases. Such restrictions should have 
a sound conceptual basis and be supported by 
empirical evidence that they are necessary to pre- 
serve competition in particular circumstances. I be- 
lieve such restrictions are appropriate in this case, 
however, to prevent likely anticompetitive behavior 
on the part of local telephone companies in cable 
television. This view is based not only on analysis 
of the ability and economic incentives of regulated 
local telephone companies to engage in anticompeti- 
tive behavior but also on the ample evidence that 
local telephone companies have actually engaged in 
monopolistic practices in the past. 


_ lL assume for purposes of my analysis that local tele- 
phone companies now have the ability to offer reg- 
ulated common carrier facilities for the transmission 
of video programming provided by third parties. The 
restriction in § 533(b) applies to the business of 
packaging and marketing programming, not transmit- 
ting it. Thus, if it made economic sense for tele- 
phone companies to provide the underlying trans- 
mission facilities for cable television, they could do 
that now. Indeed, virtually all of the alleged com- 
petitive benefits from telephone company entry into 
video programming that Professor Hazlett discusses 


243 


in his affidavit would flow from video transmission 
competition by telephone companies currently per- 
missible under § 533(b). That is, if telephone com- 
panies provided an alternative competing local trans- 
mission facility for video programming, competing 
program packagers and producers could readily 
enter the market using those facilities to provide 
video services to consumers. [If such facilities-based 
competition made economic sense, it should already 
be occurring and § 533(b) does nothing to prevent 
it. If new technology makes such competition feasi- 
ble in the future, §533(b) will not stand in the 
way.] Moreover, if telephone companies—because 
of their resources and technical know-how—are able 
to provide better services or prices than incumbent 
cable operators, nothing stops telephone companies 
from competing on equal terms with cable operators 
outside their telephone service areas. 


. Telephone companies may claim there is insufficient 
incentive for them to provide underlying video trans- 
misison facilities without the ability also to offer 
video programming services. This is an extraor- 
dinarily dubious claim. For more than one hundred 
years, the business of telephone companies has been 
to provide common carrier telecommunications fa- 
cilities for the use of others. Telephone companies 
do not originate the conversations that give rise to 
a demand for their transmission service. Just as with 
telephone conversations, there are no obvious econo- 
mies of scope or of vertical integration arising from 
both laying fiber optic cable and producing or buy- 
ing and then packaging video programming. Why 
should the cost of providing transmisison service be 
lower because the telephone company provides video 
programming? The case of cable television com- 
panies is distinguishable, because at the time cable 


244 


companies developed there was an insufficient supply 
of video programming for cable television. By in- 
tegrating vertically, cable companies expanded the 
demand for their services. Today, there is a very 
substantial video programming industry already in 
existence and ready to supply service at competitive 
rates. 


. The danger in permitting telephone companies to 
provide video services in competition with other local 
sources of video programming involves sequential 
telephone company encroachment on two related 
activities. First, telephone companies are likely to 
exploit their ability to engage in cross-subsidization 
to eliminate competition in the local transmission of 
video services. Monopolization of underlying facili- 
ties to transmit video services will result in an in- 
crease in the price of local telephone service. Having 
eliminated competing means of access to viewers, 
telephone companies are then likely to displace more 
efficient providers of, and to tend to monopolize, the 
provision of video services as a further means to 
evade the regulatory constraint they face as facilities- 
based common carriers. 


Competition Policy and Regulation 


. Competition has a pivotal role in our private market 
economy, and has long been a central goal of public 
policy. The Supreme Court has characterized our 
national policy of competition as follows: 


Antitrust laws in general, and the Sherman Act 
in particular, are the Magna Carta of free en- 
terprise. They are as important to the preserva- 
vation of economic freedom and our free enter- 
prise system as the Bill of Rights is to the 
protection of our fundamental personal free- 


9. 


10. 


11, 


245 


doms. United States v. Topco Associates, 405 
596 at 611 (1972). 


Competition protects the interests of consumers in 
efficient prices, cost-effective production, and tech- 
nological progress through its effects on the economic 
incentives facing sellers. Competition assures that 
sellers who serve consumer interests survive and pros- 
per; those who do not fail and are replaced by 
others. 


The process of competition sometimes fails, calling 
for public policy responses. Sellers, for example, 
may form cartels, or seek through merger to achieve 
monopolies. In addition, monopolists may attempt 
to exploit their market power in unacceptable ways, 
or to extend it to other markets. These threats to 
the process of competition are regulated through the 
antitrust laws. Many conimunications laws, in fact, 
including § 533(b), are simply industry-specific ap- 
plications of the broader principles underlying the 
antitrust laws. In addition, the pricing and other 
decisions of certain monopolies are regulated directly 
by public utility commissions. 


A familiar problem in competition policy is that of 
“leveraging,” which occurs when a monopolist in one 
market seeks to leverage its market power into a 
second market. Such problems may arise, for ex- 
ample, in the case of tying. See generally, Eastman 
Kodak Company v. Image Technical Services 112 
S.Ct. 2072 (1992). Leveraging is most likely to be 
a problem where circumstances prevent a monopolist 
from exploiting its economic power fully in the pri- 
mary market; in such cases, encroachment upon a 
second market may enable the monopolist to extract 
additional profits from its primary market. Compe- 
tition policy condemns this for two reasons. First, 
customers in the primary market are injured by being 


246 


required to pay more for the monopolist’s goods. 
Second, the monopolist injures competition in the 
second market by displacing more efficient competi- 
tors, and perhaps even by monopolizing the second 
market. Thus, consumers are harmed in both mar- 
kets. 


12. It has been recognized for more than thirty years 
that one species of leveraging exists because classic 
public utility rate regulation creates incentives to 
engage in anticompetitive behavior.’ Traditional 
public utility regulation requires a monopolist to 
price on the basis of the cost of providing service, 
to which is added a fair rate of return on invested 
capital, If the resulting price is less than the monop- 
olist’s profit-maximizing price, such regulation creates 
incentives for the utility to avoid, evade, or ease its 
regulatory constraint. In certain circumstances, this 
evasion can be achieved by invading a second mar- 
ket and displacing more efficient competitors in that 
market. Professor Kahn describes regulated monop- 


olists’ 


. . . tendency to reach out for additional busi- 
ness, inside or outside the sphere of their fran- 
chised public-utility operations, if need be at 
rates below incremental costs... . [T]his kind 
of behavior is most likely to be encountered in 


1 Averch and Johnson, Behavior of the Firm Under Regulatory 
Constraint, 52 Amer. Econ. Rev. 1052 (1962). For a fuller develop- 
ment of the theoretical issues raised by cost-of-service-regulated 
monopoly firms participating in competitive activities, see Bren- 
nan, Why Regulated Firms Should Not Be Allowed in Unreg- 
ulated Markets: Understanding the Divestiture in U.S. v. AT&T, 
82 Antitrust Bull. 741 (1987); Brennan, Cross-Subsidization and 
Cost Misallocation by Regulated Monopolists, Journal of Regulatory 
Economics (1990) 37, and Noll and Owen, United States v. AT&T: 
The Economic Issues, in J. Kwoka and L. White, eds., The Anti- 
trust Revolution, (1989, rev. ed. 1993). 


247 


competitive situations, because it is there that 
the elasticity of demand may require unre- 
munerative rates if the regulated company is 
to obtain the business and in this manner to 
increase its rate base. A. Kahn, 2 The Eco- 
nomics of Regulation: Principles and Institu- 
tions 54 (1971) [footnote omitted]. 


13. Invasion of secondary markets helps a regulated 
monopolist evade regulatory constraint by creating 
opportunities to inflate the apparent costs of provid- 
ing its primary monopoly service. If services in the 
second market are provided using common items of 
equipment or personnel, or if the services produced 
in the second market are used in the primary busi- 
ness, Opportunities generally exist to engage in be- 
havior that has the effect of increasing the apparent 
costs of providing the primary monopoly service. 
Professors Laffont and Tirole describe two such 
opportunities: 


i] 
- 
fs 
A 
by 
a 
+ 
é 
v 
. 


A. Accounting arbitrage. The firm engages in 
accounting manipulations. Labor, raw mate- 
rial, and other inputs are imputed to any activ- 
ity for which they are not used. The transfer 
prices for intermediate goods traded between 
divisions is set at the wrong level. 


B. Allocative arbitrage. The general office al- 
locates its effort or attention to those activities 
that face the most powerful incentive schemes 
rather than to minimize total cost. To these 
activities are dedicated the best engineers or in- 
puts. There is no imputation of expenses of one 
activity on another, but rather more attention 
is paid to certain activities. J.-J. Laffont and 
J. Tiorle, A Theory of Incentives in Procure- 
ment and Regulation 188 (1993). 


248 


14. Encroachment on the second market, because it 
generates additional monopoly profits in the first, 
may be profitable even when pricing below cost is 
required in order to make such inroads. This anti- 
competitive “cross-subsidization” may amount to in- 
definite predatory pricing in the competitive market. 
Such predatory pricing is profitable to the regulated 
firm from the outset. (In contrast, in traditional 
predatory pricing, the monopolist must eventually 
raise prices in order to recoup losses and enjoy the 
fruits of its predation. Matsushita Elec. Indus. Co v. 
Zenith Radio Corp. 475 U.S. 574 (1986) at 588- 
89.) Similarly, activities, even costly activities, that 
reduce the market share of rivals in the second mar- 
ket by increasing rivals’ own costs, may be profitable. 
In either case the effect is an anticompetitive (i.c., 
injurious to consumers) partial or complete fore- 
closure of the market to competitors. 


15. A second antitrust problem arises when there is a 
vertical relationship between the regulated monopoly 
and the unregulated or less strictly regulated com- 
petitive activity. Two entities are vertically related 
when one supplies a product that is used by the other 
as an input in production. Suppose a firm has a 
regulated monopoly in an “upstream” or input activ- 
ity (for example, transmission facilities for video 
programming) that is essential for an unregulated 
downstream activity (for example, video program- 
ming services).” The vertical relationship affords 


2In the context of video programming services, it is ambiguous 
which activity (programming or transmission) is an “input” be- 
cause it depends on which entity is ultimately offering service to 
the public. If 533(b) were abolished and telephone companies 
offered integrated video programming over their own transmission 
facilities, programming would be an input, and therefore “up- 
stream”, of the final integrated service sold to consumers. Al- 
ternatively, transmission can be regarded as an input for a com-

249 


such a firm opportunities to exercise increased mar- 
ket power in transmission and to realize the resulting 
higher profits in the unregulated activity. By dis- 
criminating against competing packagers of video 
services needing access to its monopoly transmission 
facility, the unregulated vertically-integrated video 
program supplier may be able to sell programming at 
inflated prices to retail customers. Further, in some 
circumstances the integrated monopolist can sell pro- 
gramming to itself at inflated prices in order to earn 
excessive profits. 


The problems described above are quite general; 
they are not limited to telephone companies, or with- 
in telephony to the issue of cable television. For this 
reason, for example, the effects on horizontal com- 
petition to vertical acquisitions in regulated indus- 
tries are a subject of special scrutiny under the Clay- 
ton Act. See United States Department of Justice 
Merger Guidelines, issued June 14, 1984 at § 4.23. 
(The 1984 Guidelines were superseded by the De- 
partment of Justice and Federal Trade Commission 
Horizontal Merger Guidelines issued April 2, 1992. 
However, according to a statement accompanying 
the 1992 Guidelines, various provisions of the 1984 
Guidelines, including § 4.23, remain in effect.) 


For example, the California Public Utilities Com- 
mission not long ago turned down a request by 
Southern California Edison to acquire a neighboring 
electric utility, San Diego Gas and Electric. The 
CPUC concluded that the proposed merger raised 
serious competitive problems because it would facili- 


peting video packager that uses common carrier transmission 
facilities to deliver programs to the public. In this affidavit, I use 
the convention of describing programming as an upstream input 
because I am concerned with the dangers presented by a vertically 
integrated telephone monopolist. 


18. 


19, 


20. 


250 


tate evasion of regulation as contemplated by § 4.23 
of the 1984 Merger Guidelines. Southern California 
Edison and San Diego Gas and Electric, California 
Public Utilities Commisison Decision 91-05-028, Ap- 
plication 88-12-035, (1991) at 79. 


Similarly, the Department of Justice recognized that 
regulated electric utilities might engage in monopoly 
behavior with regard to markets for coal in order to 
evade regulatory constraint. Competition in the Coal 
Industry: Report of the U.S. Department of Justice 
Pursuant to Section 8 of the Federal Coal Leasing 
Amendments Act of 1976 for Fiscal Year 1979 
(1980) at 62ff. 


Where problems such as those described in the ex- 
amples above are found as isolated instances, they 
are treated under the antitrust laws or regulatory 
statutes on a case-by-case basis. Where they are 
pervasive and predictable consequences of regulation 
and industry structure, they are best dealt with 
through prophylactic rules. 


The case of local telephone utility provision of video 
programming services is a particular instance of the 
problem that I have been describing. In traditional 
antitrust leveraging analysis, incursions by telephone 
companies into cable television could be treated as 
simple extensions of monopoly based, for example, 
on the market power of telephone companies over 
“bottlenecks” such as essential inputs required to 
provide cable service; poles and conduit space are 
examples. Modern economic analysis emphasizes as 
the basis for this incursion the opportunity it creates 
for a telephone monopolist to evade regulatory con- 
straint and thus to enjoy a greater fraction of the 
potential unregulated monopoly profits from the pro- 
vision of telephone service. The incentive to engage 


21. 


251 


in anticompetitive conduct in a second market is not 
eliminated by regulation of that market. Differences, 
either in regulatory treatment, e.g., allowed rate or 
return, or in the elasticities of demand in the two 
markets, are generally sufficient to make it profit- 
able for a regulated telephone company to expand in 
order to evade regulation of its telephone profits. 
Thus, while control of essential facilities is an ele- 
ment of the raising-rivals’-costs branch of the theory, 
it is by no means a necessary element. Profitable 
cross-subsidization does not require that the regu- 
lated utility control essential facilities. 


In the next section, I describe in more detail the 
conditions that give rise to concern for competition 
when local telephone companies are permitted to 
enter local markets for video programming. 


Anticompetitive Incentives of Local Telephone Companies 
22. The expansion of a cost-of-service regulated local 


telephone company into the provision of video pro- 
gramming services threatens competition because 
such a combination is likely to allow the telephone 
company to evade the constraint that regulators have 
placed on its exercise of market power. For example, 
by allocating costs for both regulated monopoly tele- 
phone services and video transmission service to the 
regulated telephone operations, the firm may obtain 
regulatory approval for an increase in prices for its 
telephone services. Telephone companies’ use of 
common facilities to provide both telephone service 
and video transmission services distinguishes tele- 
phone companies from, for example, electric utilities 
with regard to the provision of video services. Elec- 
tric utilities would not for the most part provide 
video services on the same facilities used to deliver 
electric power, and hence would have substantially 
less opportunity to engage in cross-subsidization. 


23. 


24. 


25. 


252 


A price increase due to cross-subsidization involves 
an exercise of market power. The firm realizes in- 
creased profits at the expense of consumers. While 
the increased profits actually come from the regu- 
lated activity, from an accounting point of view they 
appear to accrue to the competitive activity. Simi- 
larly, having wholly or partly foreclosed competitors 
for transmission of video programming through such 
cross-subsidization, the regulated telephone company 
can foreclose competing video programming sup- 
pliers from its facilities and earn even higher profits 
on its video offerings. The incentive to foreclose 
competition flows from the constraints regulators 
place on the profits from the underlying transmission 
monopoly. 

As I have already noted, the exercise of additional 
market power in the regulated telephone market, that 
is, evasion of rate regulation, is not the only problem 
that arises from the expansion of regulated monopoly 
telephone firms into competitive activities. This 
cross-subsidization of the competitive activity can 
inflate artificially the regulated telephone company’s 
market share in the competitive market and eliminate 
more efficient competitors there. 


The foreclosure of efficient video transmission com- 
petitors through cross-subsidization is exacerbated to 
the extent that local telephone companies also con- 
trol bottleneck facilities. It is plaintiffs’ contention 
that cable television systems are local monopolies. 
Hazlett Affidavit at 94 4-16. Cross subsidization by 
a telephone company which forecloses competition 
from cable companies would give the telephone com- 
pany a transmission monopoly. The telephone com- 
pany that provided program services would have the 
ability and incentive to discriminate against its video 
programming packager competitors who as a result 


26. 


27. 


28. 


253 


of the transmission monopoly may need access to its 
facilities to reach consumers. 


The early history of cable television involved tele- 
phone companies’ attempts either to deny access to 
cable companies or to charge them exorbitant fees 
for use of telephone facilities. Actual anticompetitive 
behavior on the part of telephone companies against 
cable television companies in the 1960s provided a 
sensible basis for the FCC to prohibit telephone com- 
panies from providing cable television services in 
1970. In addition, the Pole Attachment Act of 
1978, 47 U.S.C. § 224, was a partial response to 
these problems and provided regulatory oversight of 
charges for use of telephone facilities by cable com- 


panies. 


If discrimination such as the’ described succeeds in 
raising rivals’ costs, that will .ncrease prices for video 
programming services above competitive levels, per- 
mitting the regulated telephone company to earn 
above-competitive profits in ‘video programming 
services. Attempts by regulators to control the 
prices and profits telephone companies might earn in 
video programming services do not eliminate the 
incentives on the part of vertically-integrated tele- 
phone companies to exclude more efficient providers 
of video programming services. Due both to likely 
differences in regulatory treatment and in the elas- 
ticities of demand for telephone services versus video 
programming services, telephone companies generally 
will find it profitable for foreclose efficient competi- 
tors. The net effect is to raise costs and prices in 
both markets. 


Finally, telephone companies’ incentives to exclude 
cable companies from the market and to monopolize 
video programming services are magnified because 
cable companies with sophisticated fiber networks pre- 


29. 


254 


sent a potential competitive threat to local monopoly 
telephone services. See Stipulations 43 and 45; Plain- 
tiff's Memorandum at 12; Hazlett Affidavit at { 37. 


The extension of a regulated monopoly into other 
markets may be harmful to competition in a variety 
of circumstances. The particular conditions that give 
rise to this danger in the case of local telephone 
company provision of video services are: 


Market Power: Telephone companies characteristi- 
cally have market power in the provision of local 
telephone services so that, absent regulation, they 
would be able to increase profits by raising telephone 
prices above the competitive or regulated level. 


Regulation: Local telephone companies characteris- 
tically are subject to cost-based regulation that con- 
strains prices to be below the prices that would 
maximize profits and that these firms would charge 
in the absence of regulation. In other words, local 
telephone companies have unexercised market power 
as a result of regulation. As I indicate infra, newer 
forms of regulation such as price caps still leave 
telephone companies with the incentive and ability to 
evade regulatory constraints. 

Common Facilities: Local telephone companies 
have a substantial opportunity to engage in cross- 
subsidization when, as here, both the competitive video 
transmission service and the monopoly service are 
provided on common facilities with common costs. 
Stipulations 87, 88, and 97; Hazlett Affidavit at q 32. 
Common costs mean that cross-subsidization is vir- 
tually impossible to detect and eliminate. It is the 
existence of significant common costs which differen- 
tiates telephone companies from electric utilities. 


Competitive Activity: Local telephone companies 
expanding into program packaging typically would 


255 


face existing and potential providers of video services 
such as cable, wireless cable (MMDS), video rental 
outlets and direct broadcast satellite (DBS) provid- 
ers. Because they would face competition, their ex- 
pansion would reduce the market shares of rivals, 
including ones that may have lower costs or superior 
products. 

Imperfect Regulation: Local telephone companies 
can reasonably expect to be able to obtain approval 
for an increase in their regulated telephone prices 
based on the higher costs that result from cross- 
subsidizing video transmission activities. In other 
words, regulatory agencies are not able to monitor 
the regulated telephone services and video services 
perfectly. 

Vertical Relationship: Local telephone companies 
also have a substantial opportunity to engage in 
cross-subsidization when, as here, the competitive ac- 
tivity (program packaging) will be selling to the 
regulated entity. This “self-dealing” situation invites 
overcharging for input services in order to increase 
the apparent costs and therefore the permitted price 
of the regulated service. Moreover, in order to mini- 
mize the effectiveness of regulators in monitoring 
such transfer pricing, the telephone company will 
have incentives to deny the programming to compet- 
ing distributors. 


History of Anticompetitive Behavior by Telephone 
Companies 


30. Concern on the part of the FCC and the Congress 
with the anticompetitive potential in telephone com- 
pany provision of video services did not arise in a 
vacuum. The 1960s and 1970s were a period of 
great turmoil in telecommunications policy—one in 
which the federal government was promoting with 


31. 


256 


increasing urgency a role for competition. The 
long-standing telephone monopoly was falling into 
disfavor. The government was acutely aware that 
the Bell System opposed this policy. The local tele- 
phone companies owned then by AT&T engaged in 
exactly the practices described above in various 
nascent competitive markets for equipment and serv- 
ice, including anticompetitive activities against cable 
television companies. See United States v. Western 
Electric, Plaintiff's First Statement of Contentions 
and Proof 202, 239 (November 1, 1979). One re- 
sult was the antitrust action that culminated in the 
dissolution of the Bell System. The Bell System was 
dissolved, amid prophylactic restrictions on the abil- 
ity of local telephone companies to enter competitive 
markets, in 1984, the very year that § 533(b) was 
codified. 

Thus, Congress had ample evidence that telephone 
companies respond to the incentives they face and 
engage in anticompetitive behavior. In United States 
v. Western Electric, the government's antitrust suit 
charged that, through inflated prices between verti- 
cal affiliates, cross-subsidization, and discrimination 
against rivals, the Bell System had violated the anti- 
trust laws by (i) charging anticompetitive service and 
equipment prices to the customers of its regulated 
local telephone services, and (ii) using its status as 
a regulated monopoly in local telephone service to 
foreclose competition in and monopolize the poten- 
tially competitive long-distance telephone service and 
telephone equipment markets. The government called 
as witnesses several former Federal Communications 
Commission officials who testified to the ineffective- 
ness of regulation in preventing such abuses. The 
government’s view was that regulators could not 
regulate the Bell System adequately. The Justice 
Department stated at the time that: 


257 


At the heart of the government's case in United 
States v. AT&T was the failure of regulation 
to safeguard competition in the face of the 
powerful incentives and abilities of a firm en- 
gaged in the provision of both regulated mo- 
nopoly and competitive services. Neither of 
these problems [cross-subsidization and discrimi- 
nation] has thus far proven amenable to suc- 
cessful regulatory solution. Indeed, the very 
basis for divestiture is that the anticompetitive 
problems inherent in the joint provision of regu- 
lated monopoly and competitive services are 
otherwise insoluble. U.S. Department of Justice, 
Response to Public Comments on the Proposed 
Modification of Final Judgment, 47 Fed. Reg. 
23, 320-336, May 27, 1982, quoted in United 
States v. Western Elec. Co., Inc., 673 F. Supp. 
525, 568, DDC 1987. 


32. Opinions by the U.S. District Court for the District 
of Columbia on related issues, such as attempts to 
remove line of business restrictions agreed to by the 
Regional Bell operating companies in the consent 
decree in United States v. Western Electric, have up- 
held repeatedly the validity and applicability to pre- 
sent circumstances of the economic theory I have 
articulated, and that was relied upon by the Depart- 
ment of Justice. According to the Court, regulators 
were indeed unable effectively to monitor the be- 
havior of telephone companies: * 


. . . . [T]Jhe Court concluded following the close 
of the Department’s case, and in accordance 
with the arguments presented by the Depart- 
ment, that “the Commission is not and never 
has been capable of effective enforcement of the

trie, 673 F. Supp. 5625 (DDC 1987), footnotes omitted.

laws governing AT&T's behavior,” and that ac- 
cordingly AT&T had been able to violate the 
antitrust laws in a number of ways over a long 
period of time with respect to interexchange 
(long distance) services and the procurement of 
equipment. ... 673 F. Supp. 531. 


[T]he FCC was unable to prevent or to remedy
