Further, the Court has noted that the telephone com- 
panies retained the local monopolies that permitted 
leveraging to other, competitive markets and which 
are critical to the economic basis for restricting tele- 
phone company participation in related markets: 


The Court found that, were the Regional Com- 
panies allowed to be present in the interex- 
change market while they also maintained mo- 
nopoly control of the local telephone markets, 
they would be able to pursue precisely the same 
course as had the Bell System: (1) to discrimi- 
nate in a variety of ways against their non- 
monopoly competitors through judicious use of 
the local monopoly; and (2) to “subsidize their 
interexchange prices with profits earned from 
their monopoly services.” 673 F. Supp. 542. .. 
[T]heir monopoly bottlenecks are as solid and 
pervasive as they were when the decree was en- 


259 


tered. It is equally clear that nothing has oc- 
curred to change the decree conclusion that 
those in control of the local bottlenecks have 
the incentive and ability to use their monopoly 
power anticompetitively in the interexchange 
market. .. . 673 F. Supp. 546. 


Moreover, the Court found the telephone companies 
have the power to “raise rivals’ costs,” just as eco- 
nomic theory predicts: 


The manufacturing restriction was based in sub- 
stantial part on evidence presented by the De- 
partment of Justice at the trial of this case in- 
dicating that the Bell System had improperly 
monopolized the market for telecommunications 
equipment, in that its local Operating Com- 
panies purchased such equipment primarily from 
Western Electric Company, the System’s manu- 
facturing affiliate, and “engaged in systematic 
efforts to disadvantage outside suppliers.” 673 
F. Supp. 552 . . . As testimony and other evi- 
dence demonstrated, the Operating Companies 
managed, by one stratagem or other, to purchase 
Western Electric’s products, even when those 
products were more expensive or of lesser qual- 
ity than alternative goods available from un- 
affiliated vendors. 673 F. Supp. 553... The 
Bell System subsidized the prices of its equip- 
ment with the revenues from the Operating 
Companies’ monopoly services. The effect of this 
practice, as with respect to cross-subsidization 
generally, was (1) to permit the Bell Sys- 
tem to undercut other producers of equipment 
(which lacked such a subsidy), and (2) un- 
fairly to burden the consumers with excessive 
rates for the monopoly services they were fur- 
nished by the Operating Companies. . . . 673 
F. Supp. 553-54. 


33. 


260 


In sum, the Court affirmed both the conceptual and 
empirical validity of the very economic theory I have 
articulated in this affidavit, and confirmed that the 
conditions were present to justify a restriction on the 
freedom of regulated local telephone companies to 
enter competitive markets: 


[MJany of the current operations of the Re- 
gional Companies take place in unregulated 
markets. This complex mixture of regulated 
and unregulated activities provides these com- 
panies both with a powerful temptation and 
with ample opportunity to commit anticompeti- 
tive abuses in the competitive markets and to 
subsidize their competitive operations with pro- 
fits earned in the monopoly markets. 673 F. 
Supp. 569. 


The anticompetitive problems associated with tele- 
phone company participation in competitive markets 
did not end with the dissolution of the Bell System 
in 1984. Because the local telephone companies re- 
mained regulated monopolies, they continued to seek 
opportunities to earn greater profits by breaking out 
of their regulatory constraints. For example, an audit 
of BellSouth by a NARUC task force in 1985 found 
cross-subsidies of new, competitive businesses from 
regulated telephone activities. Special Accounting 
Task Force of the Southern Public Service Commis- 
sions, Report on the BellSouth Corporation, (May 16, 
1985) at 11. These practices by BellSouth were 
found to have continued in 1990. A California Pub- 
lic Utilities Commission audit of Pacific Telesis in 
1986 also found cross-subsidies of unregulated busi- 
nesses. California Public Utilities Commission, Audit 
Report on Pacific Telesis, (July 11, 1986) at 15. A 
NYNEX purchasing subsidiary overcharged regulated 
phone operations $118 million between 1984 and 
1988 and was fined $1.4 million by the FCC. FCC, 


261 


Review of Affiliate Transactions Between NY NEX'’s 
LECs and Materiel Enterprises, (1989). US West 
admitted in 1991 to favoring its competitive switching 
offerings in granting access to its local network and 
paid a $10 million fine. Memorandum of the United 
States in Support of Motion and Stipulation for 
Entry of an Enforcement Order, United States v. 
Western Electric Co., Enforcement Order, February 
15, 1991. The Georgia Public Service Commission 
determined that Southern Bell Telephone, a telephone 
company of BellSouth Corporation, used its monopoly 
control of the local exchange market to impede com- 
petition in the voice messaging services market by dis- 
criminatorily denying competitors access to the local 
exchange network, and that “substantial issues of 
predatory pricing and cross-subsidy [had] been raised” 
with regard to the pricing of the service. Order of 
the Commission, In the Matter of the Commission's 
Investigation Into Southern Bell Telephone and Tele- 
graph Company's Provision of MemoryCall Service, 
Docket No. 4000-U, at 67-68 (June 4, 1991). In 
1992, Southern Bell agreed to pay approximately 
$15.2 million to more than 900,000 customers to 
resolve a grand jury investigation into charges that 
Southern Bell, inter alia, had intentionaly overbilled 
customers through the fraudulent sale of optional tele- 
phone services. In accepting the settlement, the grand 
jury issued a report recommending “stricter con- 
trols on the cost allocation procedures . . . to prevent 
the unlawful subsidy of the unregulated side by the 
regulated side.” Press Release, Department of Legal 
Affairs, Florida Office of Statewide Prosecution (Oct. 
9, 1992). A 1992 audit commissioned by the Louisi- 
ana Public Service Commission identified “numerous 
instances where BellSouth affiliate transactions were 
structured to benefit the Company’s nonregulated ac- 


34. 


35. 


262 


tivities.” Report to the Louisiana Public Service Com- 
mission, Ratemaking and Financial Audit of South 
Central Bell Telephone Company, Docket No. U- 
17949, Subdocket A, at ix (Aug. 1992). Indeed, the 
anticompetitive activities of the divested Bell Regional 
companies led the District Court to conclude in 1987: 


The Regional Companies are of course limited 
in the breadth and scope of the anticompetitive 
activities in which they are able to engage inas- 
much as the most effective vehicles for such 
activities are beyond their reach due to the exist- 
ence of the core restrictions of the decree. What 
is startling, however, is given the relative paucity 
of the field available for such acts, in how many 
ways these companies appear nevertheless to 
have managed to discriminate and to cross- 
subisdize. United States v. Western Electric, 
673 F. Supp. 525, 580, footnote omitted. 


Continued Existence of Anticompetitive Threat 


The conditions sufficient to create the significant po- 
tential for anticompetitive harm by the expansion of 
local telephone companies into competitive activities 
still exist today with respect to telephone company 
entry into video programming services. For example, 
despite the emergence of competition for some local 
telephone services, basic local telephone service re- 
tains substantial monopoly characteristics. Stipulation 
131. 


Moreover, the market power of local telephone com- 
panies remains constrained by cost-of-service regula- 
tion. It has been argued that new forms of incentive 
regulation have reduced or eliminated the incentive of 
local telephone companies to engage in anticompeti- 
tive cross-subsidization. This type of regulation typi- 


36. 


263 


cally imposes price caps on regulated services and 
allows carriers greater profits if they are able to reduce 
their costs. If price caps, fixed in real terms, were 
kept in place for a long time, this type of regulation 
would not permit prices to increase if telephone 
companies’ costs increased. In theory, price-cap regu- 
lation applied in that way could reduce or eliminate 
the incentive to shift costs to regulated services be- 
cause those higher costs would no longer be recovered 
in higher prices for monopoly service. See Braeutigam 
and Panzar, Diversification Incentives Under “Price- 
Based” and “Cost-Based” Regulation, 20 RAND Jour- 
nal of Economics 373 (1989). 


In practice, however, telephone price-cap regulation 
does not ignore the profits and costs of the regulated 
firm. Telephone price caps typically are set, and 
periodically reset, on the basis of historical account- 
ing costs. (In contrast, the price caps recently estab- 
lished by the FCC to govern cable television service 
rates are based not on historical costs but on compari- 
sons with the rates charged by cable systems that face 
“effective competition”. Implementation of Sections 
of the Cable Television Consumer Protection and 
Competition Act of 1992: Rate Regulation, Report 
and Order, Docket MM-92-266- (released May 3, 
1993), —FCC Red—(1993). Moreover, if profits 
are high, regulators will be under political pressure to 
reduce allowed prices. Conversely, low profits will 
bring pressure on regulators to allow higher prices. In 
other words, accounting costs and profits are likely in 
the real world to affect the price caps, and telephone 
companies are likely to take that into account in 
setting their course of conduct. But once regulatory 
constraints on prices are again tied to costs, they can 
be affected and, therefore, partially evaded by tele- 
phone companies’ behavior. The regulated telephone 


37. 


264 


companies will retain the incentive to shift costs to 
regulated monopoly services and away from competi- 
tive activities. See Braeutigam and Panzar at 389. 
Further, these eventualities are reasonably foreseeable 
and thus likely to affect behavior in the present. 


I conclude that under current conditions the prohibi- 
tion on telephone company provision of cable tele- 
vision services remains necessary to prevent the likely 
monopolization of those services by local telephone 
companies. If the prohibition were removed, I believe 
it is likely that local telephone companies will use 
their regulated telephone revenues to cross-subsidize 
video programming transmission services and use their 
resulting control of essential facilities to discriminate 
against their video programming competitors. My 
conclusions about the risks today from telephone com- 
pany provision of cable television services are echoed 
by the District Court’s warnings in 1987 about tele- 
phone company expansion into related fields: 


The Regional Companies are now requesting 
entry into far broader competitive fields of 
endeavor than any in which they were permitted 
to function during the last three years. Based 
upon their performance during that three-year 
period, it would not be unreasonable to assume 
that cross-subsidization would also take place 
with respect to these new markets, and that it 
would occur on a far wider and more destructive 
scale than heretofore. That is so if only because 
cross-subsidies are much more easily concealed 
where—as between local exchange service and 
interexchange, telecommunications manufactur- 
ing, and information services—there are many 
common costs that can be attributed, almost at 
the companies’ unfettered choice, to any of the 


38. 


265 


various activities, than where cross-subsidization 
is attempted between exchange service and ven- 
tures foreign to telecommunications. United 
States v. Western Electric, 673 F. Supp. 525, 
582-83. 


The Court in 1991 once again affirmed the economic 
theory and the necessary underlying conditions for 
restricting telephone company expansion into competi- 
tive areas: 


In the opinion of the Court, informed by over 
twelve years of experience with evidence in the 
telecommunications field, the most probable con- 
sequences of such entry by the Regional Com- 
panies into the sensitive information services 
market will be the elimination of competition 
from that market and the concentration of the 
sources of information of the American people 
in just a few dominant, collaborative conglom- 
erates, with the captive local telephone monopo- 
lies as their base. Such a development would be 
inimical to the objective of a competitive market, 
the purpose of the antitrust laws, and the eco- 
nomic well-being of the American people. 
United States v. Western Elec. Co., Inc., 767 
F. Supp. 308, 326 (DDC 1991). 


Less Restrictive Alternatives 


Because restrictions on the ability of any firm to 
enter new markets should be avoided whenever pos- 
sible under the very principles that competition policy 
seeks to achieve, it is useful to consider whether there 
is some less restrictive alternative to the ban on tele- 
phone-cable cross-ownership that achieves similar pro- 
tection for competition. For the reasons given below, 
I believe that there is not. 


266 


39. There are two candidates for the role of less re- 
strictive alternative. First, either the antitrust au- 
thorities or regulators can deal with problems of cross- 
subsidy, discrimination and the like on 4 case-by- 
case basis, seeking to impose appropriate sanctions 
when such behavior is discovered. For the very rea- 
sons given above, however, this approach is likely to 
be extremely ineffective. Regulated utilities in gen- 
eral and telephone companies in particular have an 
enormous advantage over regulators, an advantage 
attributable to their control over information about 
costs and technologies. Judge Greene found that reg- 
ulators in fact were unable to control AT&T's anti- 
competitive behavior. Moreover, as indicated above, 
the Bell Regional operating companies have continued 
since divestiture to engage in cross-subsidies and 
other anticompetitive behavior despite regulatory and 
antitrust oversight, and in spite of the fact that their 
incursions into related competitive activities have been 
severely curtailed by the Consent Decree in Western 
Electric. It would not be reasonable or economical of 
judicial resources, nor would it likely be effective in 
preventing anticompetitive abuses, to require repeated 
case-by-case enforcement of antitrust laws against so 
pervasive and likely a pattern of conduct. 


40. Second, there are quasi-structural approaches such as 
that of the “fully separated subsidary”. The idea here 
is to permit monopoly utilities to enter competitive 
businesses, while minimizing the regulator's difficulties 
in monitoring anti-competitive behavior by requiring 
that all the facilities, operations, personnel, books and 
records of the two activities be kept wholly separated. 
This approach might be successful in dealing with ex- 
tensions to businesses that do not require the use of 
common facilities or vertical transactions with local 
telephone service, but it cannot work with respect to 


41. 


42. 


267 


telephone company provision of video programming 
services. Indeed, in two efforts to settle the antitrust 
case that led to the breakup of the old Bell System, 
the government and AT&T negotiated what became 
known as “Quagmire I” and “Quagmire II,” in which 
attempts were made to specify all the behavioral and 
institutional safeguards that would be necessary to 
preserve competition if AT&T were to remain a com- 
bination of regulated local monopolies and competi- 
tive equipment and long distance businesses. These 
attempts failed because both parties realized that the 
concept was unworkable. B. G. Cole, ed., After 
the Breakup: Assessing the New Post-AT&T Divesti- 
ture Era, 8, 22, 37 (1991). 


No amount of accounting separation combined with 
regulation and monitoring can deal effectively with 
the issue of common facilities and common costs that 
would exist if telephone companies provided video 
service in their own service areas. As indicated 
above, plaintiffs intend to offer competitive video 
transmission services and monopoly telephone serv- 
ices over common facilities. Plaintiffs do not plan to 
separate these transmission services from each other 
through separate subsidiaries nor could they. There- 
fore, separate subsidiaries offer no protection from 
anticompetitive cross-subsidization which would re- 
sult in telephone company monopolization of video 
transmission services. 


Another intractable problem with respect to cross 
subsidization by telephone companies of video pro- 
gramming services is improper allocation of the costs 
of financal risk-taking. As Professor Brennan has 
put it, 
Raising capital through borrowing may be a 
source of cross-subsidy. The regulator may 


at 


in Unregulated Markets: Understanding the 
Divestiture in U.S. V AT&T, 32 Antitrust Bull. 
741, 763 (1987). 


abuses by AT&T involved exactly the same issues of 
discrimination in favor of AT&T’s “separate” sub- 
sidiaries, Long Lines (AT&T's interexchange sub- 
sidiary) and Western Electric (AT&T’s manufactur- 
ing subsidiary). Despite regulatory attempts to cur- 
tail these anticompetitive abuses, only divestiture and 
isolation of the monopoly local telephone companies 
from these related competitive activities was sufficent 
to eliminate them. 

Finally, separate subsidiaries could not protect against 
“self-dealing” that could artificially raise video pro- 


45. 


269 


gramming costs. One strategy available to an inte- 
grated telephone company providing video program- 
ming services is to produce or have produced for it 
programming that it does not sell to other distrib- 
utors. This was one strategy AT&T employed with 
Western Electric in selling exclusively to its affiliated 
local telephone companies equipment at inflated 
prices. Indeed, Plaintiffs are contemplating such ex- 
clusive programming for their Alexandria system. 
Horner Affidavit at ¢ 5. With no market transactions 
available to test the reasonableness of the prices 
charged for that programming, separate subsidiaries 
offer no protection from the evasion of regulation 
through the payment of excessive prices to unregu- 
lated program production affiliates. 


Cable Monopolies 


The Plaintiffs in this matter assert with vigor that 
cable television companies are monopolists, and that 
the national policy in favor of competition would be 
advanced by permitting the entry of telephone com- 
panies. Hazlett Affidavit at {4 30-31. There is no 
doubt that cable television companies have market 
power. But so do many firms in the economy; market 
power is a matter of degree. Schurz Communica- 
tions, Inc. v. F.C.C., 982 F.2d 1043 (7th Cir. 
1992) at 1052. 


Plaintiffs’ argument is wrong-headed even if cable 
systems are monopolists. Competition is indeed pro- 
moted when barriers to entry are reduced. But com- 
petition is not promoted when regulated monopolists 
are permitted to leverage their economic power from 
one market to another, regardless of the structure 
of the secondary market. A I have noted above, 
such leveraging in the end generally harms consum- 
ers in both markets. The wisdom of excluding tele- 


47. 


270 


phone companies from the video programming busi- 
ness must be assessed independently of whether cable 
television companies are presently in possession of 
market power. In any event, actual and potential 
technologies such as direct broadcast satellites and 
wireless cable present far more immediate and legiti- 
mate competitive threats to the cable industry than 
telephone technologies. The special threat of tele- 
phone entrants is the ability, not necessarily to pro- 
vide a superior service, but to engage in profitable 
long-term predation and discrimination, regardless of 
the quality of their service. 


Future Considerations 


In the future, conditions may change sufficiently to 
warrant removal of the prohibition of telephone com- 
pany ownership of cable television systems. The 
market for local telephone service is beginning to 
face emerging competition for some business cus- 
tomers because of bypass, cellular, fiber optic ring, 
and other new technologies. DBS or other new de- 
velopments may make it impossible for local tele- 
phone companies to monopolize transmission. It is 
important that the restriction on telephone provision 
of video services be kept in place, however, to ensure 
that the trend toward greater competition for local 
telephone services is not reversed. Significantly, the 
nascent competition that faces telephone companies 
is concentrated in their services to business custom- 
ers. Cable probably is the only serious source of 
potential competition for residential telephone serv- 
ice; as I have noted above, the reverse is not true. 
It would be unwise economically to risk eliminating 
that potential threat by exposing cable television 
competitors today to anticompetitive behavior by 
local telephone companies. In the future, when it is 
possible to conclude with a reasonable degree of 


271 


confidence that the trend toward competition for 
local telephone services cannot be reversed by anti- 
competitive behavior on the part of local 
companies, removal of the prohibition would be ap- 
propriate. 
/s/ Bruce M. Owen 
Bruce M. Owen

REPLY AFFIDAVIT OF ALFRED E. KAHN 


STATE OF New YORK 
COUNTY OF TOMPKINS 


(1) My name is Alfred E. Kahn. I am Robert Julius 
Thorne Professor of Political Economy, Emeritus, at Cor- 
nell University and Special Consultant to National Eco- 
nomic Research Associates, Inc. My business address 
is 308 North Cayuga Street, Ithaca, New York 14850. 

(2) Among the experiences of mine pertinent to my 
submission in this proceeding are that I was Chairman of 
the New York State Public Service Commission between 
1974 and 1977 and of the Civil Aeronautics Board in 
1977-78; I am the author of the two-volume The Eco- 
nomics of Regulation, published originally by John Wiley 
& Sons in 1970 and 1971 and reprinted in 1988 by The 
MIT Press; I have written and testified extensively on the 
subject of telecommunications regulatory policy and pub- 
lished a book and numerous articles on antitrust policy. 
I was a member of the Attorney General’s National Com- 
mittee to Study the Antitrust Laws and the National Com- 
mission for the Review of Antitrust Laws and Proce- 
dures. I have been advisor on telecommunications policy 
to Governor Carey, of New York State, recently com- 
pleted service as a member of the Ohio Blue Ribbon 
Panel on Telecommunications Regulatory Reform, and 
am at present a member of the New York State Tele- 


273 


communications Exchange. I attach a copy of my full 
resumé as an Appendix to this affidavit. 

(3) The purpose of my affidavit is to appraise and re- 
spond to the government’s proffered justification for the 
ban against the plaintiffs providing their video program- 
ming to the public within their telephone service areas— 
namely, that the prohibition is necessary to prevent the 
telephone companies from “cross-subsidizing” cable opera- 
tions with revenues from their monopoly telephone opera- 
tions. The result of such a cross-subsidy, the government 
contends, would be that the cable companies could or 
would be driven out of business, leaving the telephone 
company as the monopoly provider of both cable and 
telephone services. 

(4) This contention of the government of the likely— 
or even possible—effects of a lifting of the ban rests 
on a number of key premises. The most important of 
these are that authorization of the telephone companies 
or their affiliates to create or assemble video program- 
ming for sale to the public, in addition to their present 
ability to carry the signal of others, would materially 
increase the asserted danger of cross-subsidization; that 
regulators would freely permit the recoupment from 
politically-sensitive services such as basic local residential 
service, of costs that could or should have been assigned 
to video; and that the telephone companies would, as a 
result, be able to drive their cable competitors from the 
market. All of these beliefs are in my opinion erroneous. 

(5) The asserted danger of cross-subsidization, as de- 
scribed by both the Department of Justice and the NCTA, 
arises almost exclusively from the fact that the telephone 
network is evolving into a facility capable of carrying 
voice, data and video images. Cable systems too are 
developing these same capabilities. This kind of joint or 
common use is not an evil in itself: on the contrary, 
it is a source of efficiency—a clear exemplification of 
what economists refer to as economies of scope. It does 


274 


increase the importance of the issue, particularly in a 
regulated industry, of how the common costs are to be 
distributed among those several services. This is not a 
new regulatory problem in telephony: regulators have 
long ago developed methods of separating the costs of 
telephone service subject respectively to state and to fed- 
eral regulation. They have also devised and prescribed 
methods of assigning or allocating joint and common costs 
among the various individual services subject to their 
respective jurisdictions. Intervenors have the right to con- 
test these methods in regulatory proceedings and rights 
of appeal if they are not satisfied with the agency’s de- 
terminations. 

(6) As the foregoing discussion makes clear, the dan- 
ger of cross-subsidization typically arises in situations in 
which significant portions of the costs of competitive 
and monopoly services are common to both. If some 
of these costs are improperly shifted from the former to 
the latter and recovered in the charges for the monopoly 
services, this could indeed confer an unfair advantage 
on the telephone company in its competitive markets, 
and could conceivably enable it to drive its competitors 
out of business. The question is whether this hypotheti- 
cal scenario fits the facts and the probabilities associated 
with the legal issue now before this Court. I conclude 
emphatically that it does not. 

(7) First, it cannot be overemphasized that what is at 
issue in this case is the plaintiffs’ authority to engage in 
video programming, not their already acknowledged au- 
thority to provide video transmission. It is difficult to 
conceive that there could be any substantial costs com- 
mon to the transmission of video and other telephone 
signals, on the one side, and the creation and packaging 
of video programming on the other. Numerous authori- 
ties, including the defendants, have actually conceded this 
fact. For example, NCTA, through economist Dr. Owen, 
admits that the defendants’ cross-subsidy scenario requires 


a 
. 
Hd 
_ 
z 
; 
* 
> 
2. 
5 
e 
= 
Pg 
- 
4 
& 
va 
7e 


275 


the presence of common costs’ and that there are no 
substantial costs common to both video transport and 
programming.” And the Department of Justice has con- 
ceded elsewhere that “information services that involve 
primarily the provision of content or the processing of in- 
formation . . . would be likely to share fewer facilities 
or personnel with [telephone company] local exchange 
operations [than transmission services]. . . . [T]he po- 
tential for misallocation of costs in violation of alloca- 
tion regulations is minimal and even more easily detected 
by regulators where there is a relative paucity of joint 
and common costs between telephone exchange opera- 
tions and ventures in the new market.”* The statutory 
ban at issue here applies not to a service that, in the 
words of the Department, involves “primarily the . . . 
content of information”; it applies to a service that in- 
volves only content. There is therefore no rational con- 
nection between the defendants’ cross—subsidy argument 
and the ban against telephone companies providing video 
programming. 

(8) In the interest of a complete response, however, 
I have considered whether, if the prohibition at issue here 
is lifted, the telephone companies might, as Dr. Owen 
contends, be in a position somehow to cross-subsidize 
their video transmission services at the expense of other, 
monopoly services. In my view this is highly unlikely— 
unlikely that they would be permitted, and even if per- 


1E.g., Owen Aff. at 15. 


2 E.g., Owen Aff. at 4 (“there are no obvious economies of scope 
or of vertical integration arising from both laying fiber optic cable 
and producing or buying and then packaging video program- 
ming”) ; see also NCTA Mem. 23, United States v. Western Elec. 
Co. (D.D.C. filed Oct. 17, 1990), (“it may well be, as the Huber 
Report says, that opportunities for cross-subsidization may be few 
in the creation of programming”). 


*Mem. of the United States at 34, United States v. Western 
Electric Co., Inc., (D.D.C. filed Aug. 22, 1990). 


276 


mitted, able to do so. As for the first, 1 am advised 
by counsel that video transmission services are subject 
to federal, not state regulation. The principal other serv- 
ices subject to that jurisdiction are the “access” to or 
interconnection with the local exchange network that 
telephone companies provide to long distance carriers 
such as AT&T for the origination and termination of long 
distance calls. The FCC’s method of regulating these 
charges does not automatically permit raising them when- 
ever the providers’ overall earnings decline; and its cost 
allocation rules, to which I have already referred, pro- 
hibit that kind of cross-subsidization. 

(9) As to the ability of the telephone companies to 
raise their rates for these access services, these charges 
constitute approximately 40% of the long distance car- 
riers’ total operating costs. Entirely apart, then, from 
whether the FCC would permit the requisite increase, 
the resistance on the part of the customers would be in- 
tense, and effective. These are very sophisticated carriers, 
with every incentive to avoid improper cost shifting to 
their injury. And they have the protection, increasingly, 
of competition, and will have it even more in the future, 
as alternative providers of access services have emerged 
to handle the origination and termination of long-distance 
traffic, particularly of large governmental and business in- 
stallations. The recently announced multi-billion dollar 
TimeWarner/US West transaction is premised on the fact 
that cable systems, which now reach 96 percent of all 
households, can be modified to provide this same access 
service, in competition with local telephone companies, for 
small as well as large customers. MCI, similarly, has an- 
nounced its engagement in active negotiations with cable 
companies, with the same purpose in mind. For these 
many reasons I conclude that there is no significant danger 
of successful cross-subsidization even of video transmission 
by telephone services—whether or not the telephone com- 
panies are permitted to engage in video programming. 


277 


(10) Recovery of any such subsidies at the intrastate 
or local level is similarly highly unlikely, and becoming 
increasingly so every day. Entirely apart from the ques- 
tion of the willingness of state regulators to permit it, local 
service is becoming increasingly competitive. NCTA itself 
describes the cable industry as a “serious source of poten- 
tial competition for residential telephone service.” * Fur- 
thermore, the divestiture of AT&T has spawned a host of 
actual and potential competitors previously all part of that 
single corporate entity. Southwestern Bell, for example, 
has acquired cable systems in the Washington area, includ- 
ing Northern Virginia, which position it to provide not 
just video but competitive access services of the type con- 
templated also by the U.S. West/Time Warner transaction. 
Southwestern Bell also provides wireless services in this 
area. AT&T has already purchased a multi-billion dollar 
stake in wireless services competitive or potentially com- 
petitive with the incumbent local telephone companies; and 
the trade journals report that MCI likewise intends to enter 
the local exchange business with wireless technology. The 
FCC is actively promoting expansion of this competition— 
for example, by allocating additional spectrum to new wire- 
less services and requiring the telephone companies to pro- 
vide interconnection with all competitive access providers. 

(11) At least equally important, state regulators have 
historically been very reluctant to raise residential telephone 
rates, for obvious political reasons. Consequently, even if 
the costs of video programming could be misassigned 
to transmission services, and misassigned again over the 
federal-state jurisdictional boundary to local exchange 
services, it is certain that the regulators would resist the 
requisite compensatory price increases. If the state regula- 
tory commissions may be said to have had one overriding 
goal historically, it has been to hold down the price of 
basic residential telephone service; and they have done so 


* Owen Aff. at 31. 


278 


to the point of holding those prices inefficiently low and 
requiring inefficiently high charges for “vertical” or “en- 
hanced” services. Far from permitting cross-subsidization 
of competitive by monopoly services, regulators have typi- 
cally handicapped telephone companies in the competitive 
markets—inefficiently so. 

(12) As the defendants themselves point out,’ the cross- 
subsidization they predict is possible only to the extent a 
telephone company’s services are subject to cost-based, 
rate-of-return regulation. The kind of recoupment they 
posit was never automatic, even under that traditional reg- 
ulatory practice: rates were altered only periodically and 
raised only when achieved rates of return fell outside an 
acceptable range. 

(13) In both 1970, when the FCC first adopted the 
prohibition at issue here, and 1984, when Congress en- 
acted it, telephone companies were in fact universally sub- 
ject to traditional rate-of-return regulation. Today, in 
contrast, the FCC and a majority of state regulators have 
substantially modified that form of regulation in favor of 
price freezes or caps, which fix the price of services rather 
than telephone company profits, or other “incentive” regu- 
latory arrangements that permit company profits to fluctu- 
ate within a wider than traditionally permitted range, with 
or without automatic sharing with ratepayers of profit sur- 
pluses or deficiencies. All of these plans, by further 
weakening any previous assurances of automatic recovery 
from less competitive services of net revenue losses from 
competitive ones, have the effect of weakening any incen- 
tive or ability the telephone companies may previously 
have had to cross-subsidize competitive services at the 
expense of others less subject to competition. Indeed, pre- 
vention of cross-subsidy has been one central, explicit rea- 
son for the adoption of these reforms. The very fact that 
it is the telephone companies themselves that have been 
the principal proponents of these plans further contradicts 


5 E.g., Owens [sic] Aff. at 15. 


279 


the defendants’ contentions that they would respond to 
removal of prohibitions on their entering various competi- 
tive businesses with a strategy of cross-subsidy. Under the 
various forms of incentive regulation, the incurrence of 
“losses” on competitive business would simply decrease 
their profits. Significantly, even NCTA concedes that a 
pure price cap arrangement would “reduce or eliminate the 
incentive to shift costs to regulated services.” ° 

(14) There are some respects in which the arguments 
of the defendants do not rest entirely on the presence of 
common costs, on the one side, and of the opportunity to 
recover the burdens of cross-subsidy via cost-plus regula- 
tion on the other. For example, one of the amici on the 
side of the cable industry argues that the telephone com- 
panies would have an unfair advantage by virtue of their 
ability to “cherry pick personnel” from their telephone op- 
erations for the benefit of their video programming serv- 
ices." But, as the DC Circuit has observed with admirable 
conciseness, “[rJunning a local [telephone] exchange does 
not require reporters, copy editors, joke writers, financial 
analysts, astrologists, or other information-content pro- 
viders” “to which list one might add screen writers, 
Hollywood stars and game show hosts. 

(15) Dr. Owen’s affidavit on behalf of NCTA also 
cites, as an asserted unfair advantage of telephone com- 
panies, their ability to raise capital for the combined en- 
terprise on more favorable terms than their cable competi- 
tors. First of all, the opinion he expresses that the overall 
cost of capital of well-managed cable companies is higher 
than of telephone companies is a mere undocumented 
assertion on his part. In any event, the sufficient response 


* Owen Aff. at 23. 


* Brief of Amici Curiae Consumer Federation of America, et al., 
at 22 (dated May 21, 1993). 


* United States v. Western Electric Co., 900 F. 2d 288, 308 n. 28 
(D.C. Cir. 1990). 


280 


is that nowhere in the economist’s conception of the pre- 
requisites of effective competition or in the antitrust laws, 
to my knowledge, has the mere possible ability of one com- 
pany to raise capital on more favorable terms than another 
been regarded as providing a sufficient basis for excluding 
that company from the opportunity to compete with others 
putatively less advantageously situated in that respect. 
(16) Finally, Dr. Owen’s asserted concern that a tele- 
phone company could engage in “self dealing” by paying 
a programming affiliate inflated prices for its program- 
ming has the facts completely backwards. It is not the 
telephone company that proposes here to purchase pro- 
gramming from an affiliate. It is the programming affiliate 
that would buy video transport service from the telephone 
company, at tariffed rates. And of course the affiliate 
would have every incentive to produce and acquire pro- 
gramming as economically as possible, since it would be in 
the business of offering it to subscribers, for whose patron- 
age it would be competing with the cable companies. 
(17) Finally, I find the ultimate essential component 
of the successful strategy of cross-subsidy and predation 
hypothesized by the defendants in this proceeding—namely 
the permanent removal of competitors—highly improbable 
in the present circumstances. The cable companies have 
installed networks capable of serving 96% of all house- 
holds. That capacity is not going to go away. The mar- 
ginal cost of operating these systems must be relatively 
low, leaving their operators with latitude for price reduc- 
tions more than sufficient to discourage any would-be 
predator. It is the cable companies that are the dominant 
incumbents in the video entertainment market, with access 
or contractual rights to the most popular entertainers and 
shows. Their facilities also have the potential to be up- 
graded to provide two-way communications services, which 
could produce an enormous supplemental revenue stream. 
In the circumstances I find it simply inconceivable that 
they will or could be driven out of business by the mis- 


There is no economic basis for the expectation that 
permitting the telephone companies to provide video pro- 
gramming directly to the public within their service areas 
would put them in a position to deny the cable com- 
panies a fair opportunity to compete or, through cross- 
subsidization, to drive them from the market. 


/s/ Alfred E. Kahn 
ALFRED E. KAHN

AFFIDAVIT OF JEAN POOL 


1. I am Senior Vice President, Director of Local 
Market Broadcast for J. Walter Thompson. I manage a 
negotiating unit comprised of 136 specialists located in 
seven major markets across the U.S. We purchase com- 
mercial airtime on radio, television, and local cable sys- 
tems on behalf of J. Walter Thompson clients including 
Bell Atlantic. I am knowledgeable about the practices of 
the cable industry with respect to the selling of airtime. 

2. On May 25, 1993, we made an inquiry to Jones 
Intercable to determine whether it would be possible to 
lease a channel on its cable system in Alexandria, Vir- 
ginia. Jones said it has no channels available for leased 
access in Alexandria. Jones, however, will lease time 
slots, subject to availability, in half hour increments. 

3. Jones Intercable, as is the case with all other 
local cable companies, reserves the right to view proposed 
programming prior to air. This is typically how all broad- 
cast stations operate also. 

4. Based on my experience buying time on cable sys- 
tems, I believe that Bell Atlantic would be unlikely in 
most communities to be able to lease even one channel 
from the local cable operator. Cable systems typically 
are already filled to capacity with programming and are 
unwilling to bump existing programs to make room for 
leased access users. In most instances, therefore, Bell 
Atlantic would be limited to leasing short, individual 


285 


time slots subject to availability and subject to the edi- 
torial preview of the cable operator. 

I declare under penalty of perjury under the laws of 
the United States of America that the foregoing is true 
and correct. Executed on 6-2-93. 

/s/ Jean Pool

AFFIDAVIT OF ROBERT L. TOWNSEND 


1. My title is Director of Bell Atlantic Video Services 
Company (“BVS”). I have worked in the cable TV Indus- 
try for over a decade. Before joining BVS, from 1989 to 
1992, I was Senior Vice President of a subsidiary of the 
Home Shopping Network, which is a producer of video 
programming sold to cable systems. Prior to that, from 
1981 to 1989, I was Vice President, Sales, Marketing, and 
Programming of New York Times Cable TV. In addi- 
tion, I have served as a member of the board of directors 
of the Cable Television Administration and Marketing 
Society (“CTAM”) and was President of the Northeast 
Region of CTAM. I have substantial experience—from 
the perspectives of both a cable operator and an inde- 
pendent video programmer—in buying and selling video 
programming. 

2. I submit this affidavit in response to the govern- 
ment’s claim that the prohibition in this case does not 
“unduly burden” BVS’s ability to provide video program- 
ming in its telephone affiliates’ service areas. Gov't 
Mem. 66. 

3. The government claims that, despite the prohibi- 
tion, BVS could sell programming to existing television 
networks and could acquire a local television broadcast- 
ing station. Gov't Mem. 66. The Newspaper Association, 
unlike the government, claims that BVS could acquire 
broadcast stations only outside its telephone affiliates’ 


287 


service areas. NAA Mem. 12. On the other hand, the 
Newspaper Association claims that BVS could lease 
channel capacity from existing cable operators. NAA 
Mem. 11. The NCTA claims that BVS could offer pro- 
gramming to existing broadcast television stations, cable 
operators, movie theaters, or video stores. NCTA Mem. 
56. The National Association of Broadcasters claims that 
BVS is barred from all in-region video programming. 
NAB Mem. 32. Despite the differences in these parties’ 
views regarding what is permitted, they all—except for 
the broadcasters—suggest that there exist ample alterna- 
tive means for BVS to distribute its video programming 
even within its telephone affiliates’ service areas. 

4. Assuming for purposes of this affidavit that the 
proposed alternatives are all legally permitted, they in fact 
do not provide BVS a meaningful opportunity to provide 
video programming of the type and in the quantity that 
it seeks to provide. The problems with these alternatives 
are discussed below. 

5. Many of the alternatives would subject BVS’s 
programming to the editorial discretion of the existing 
program distributors. Cable operators and _ television 
broadcast stations have no obligation to purchase BVS’s 
programming. BVS’s ability to get its programming 
shown in any particular geographic area would be entirely 
subject to the whim of cable companies and broadcasters. 

6. NCTA states that BVS could simply create and 
market the “Bell Atlantic Hour” for carriage on local 
cable systems. NCTA Mem. 56. NCTA chose this ex- 
ample very carefully. As described in Jean Pool’s affi- 
davit, the “Bell Atlantic Hour” is near the maximum 
amount of air time that a typical cable system will sell 
to an independent programmer. But even shorter time 
slots can be difficult to arrange if the content of the pro- 
gramming is disagreeable to the cable operator. In No- 
vember 1991, one of Bell Atlantic’s companies, New Jer- 
sey Bell, produced a 30-second commercial advertisement 


288 


regarding a bill pending before the New Jersey legislature. 
Suburban Cable, a large cable operator in Northern New 
Jersey which opposed the bill, initially refused to carry 
the commercial. After a period of negotiation, Suburban 
Cable agreed to carry the commercial but insisted on 
running its own commercial immediately afterward re- 
sponding to New Jersey Bell’s commercial. The result 
was to confuse and adulterate the message New Jersey 
Bell was trying to present. In a subsequent complaint 
proceeding before the New Jersey Board of Regulatory 
Commissioners, Suburban Cable defended its actions as 
“editorial discretion.” Answer to Petition at 3, BRC 
Docket No. CC91121783 (Dec. 20, 1991). 

7. Suburban’s standard term sheet (copy attached) 
provides that “Suburban Cablevision shali have the right 
to approve or disapprove all materials submitted for 
cablecasts” and that its “right of disapproval shall be 
exercised in its sole discretion as to any matter it deems 
objectionable for any reason whatsoever.” Suburban’s 
term sheet is typical. The standard term sheet (copy at- 
tached) currently in use by Mega Advertising, which 
arranges airtime on behalf of cable systems in the Wash- 
ington, D.C. metropolitan area, provides that “[aJll pro- 
grams” are subject to “prior approval.” Programs will 
not be aired if, in Mega Advertising’s “sole discretion,” 
they are “determined to be unsatisfactory, unsuitable or 
contrary to the public interest.” 

8. None of the proposed alternatives would permit 
BVS to provide more than a channel or two of program- 
ming. For example, buying a local broadcast television 
station, as the government suggests, would allow BVS to 
program one channel. Most cable operators today offer 
30 to 53 channels. BVS’s programming plan for Alex- 
andria includes approximately 100 channels. To fit within 
the alternatives, BVS would have to abandon 99% of 
its programming plan. 

9. Moreover, relegating BVS to begging and borrow- 
ing space from the existing program distributors would 


289 


not promote programming diversity. Rather it would 
ensure tnat there is no net increase in the amount of 
programming. Whatever programming BVS would show 
would simply displace other programming. 

10. None of the alternatives would permit BVS to 
offer video on demand service. This service requires the 
technical ability to address individual subscribers and to 
deliver to subscribers specific programs that they order. 
This service also requires a return communication capa- 
bility so that the subscriber can select a video, pause it, 
restart it, and perform other functions to interact with the 
video. To my knowledge, there is no cable system or 
broadcast station in the Bell Atlantic region that em- 
ploys the type of technology needed for this service. Re- 
quiring BVS to provide programming over the existing 
media, therefore, has the effect of barring this new 
service. 

11. Video on demand is qualitatively a different type 
of programming than traditional broadcast or cable. 
From the customer’s perspective, video programming that 
a broadcaster or cable operator has pre-selected is not a 
substitute for programming that the customer selects 
herself. 

12. Finally, the Newspaper Association’s suggestion 
that BVS lease channels from existing cable operators is 
not a viable alternative. Leased access has been available 
since 1984, but programmers have made “scant use of 
the leased access channels.” Report and Order, In the 
Matter of Implementation of Sections of the Cable Tele- 
vision Consumer Protection and Competition Act of 
1992, Rate Regulation, MM Docket 92-266, at 318 (re- 
leased May 3, 1993). There have been severe problems 
with leased access arrangements. Among other things, 
cable operators charged high rates, subjected program- 
mers to the cable operators’ editorial control, and pro- 
vided no mechanism for billing subscribers on behalf of 
the leased access programmers. The House Report ac- 


290 


companying the 1992 Cable Act concluded that “leased 
access has not been an effective mechanism for securing 
access for programmers to the cable infrastructure or 
cable subscribers.” H. Rep. No. 102-628 at 39. Pur- 
suant to the 1992 Cable Act, the FCC recently issued 
rules attempting to rectify some of the deficiencies in 
leased access. However, the FCC cautioned that its rules 
are to be “understood as a starting point that will need 
refinement.” Report and Order, MM Docket 92-266, at 
306 (released May 3, 1993). Time Warner has filed a 
First Amendment challenge to the leased access require- 
ments on the ground that they would interfere with its 
“editorial discretion.” Complaint for Declaratory and 
Injunctive Relief at 44-45, Time Warner Entertainment 
Co. v. FCC, No. 92-2494, (D.D.C. filed Nov. 5, 1992). 

13. In any event, leased access would allow only a 
small amount of programming. A cable system the size 
of Jones’s system in Alexandria would be required to make 
available a total of four channels. If multiple program- 
mers seek access to these channels, under the FCC’s rules 
BVS “will only be allowed to lease up to one channel's 
capacity.” Report and Order, MM Docket 92-266, at 
310 (released May 3, 1993). Leased access, therefore, 
involves most of the problems of the other alternatives. 

I declare under penalty of perjury under the laws of 
the United States of America that the foregoing is true 
and correct. 


Executed on 6/3/93. 
/s/ Robert L. Townsend 
ROBERT L. TOWNSEND

SUPPLEMENTAL AFFIDAVIT OF WILLIAM E. LEE 


1. In my affidavit of April 27, 1993 I demonstrated 
that the creation, selection, arrangement, packaging, and 
provision of video programming for multichannel deliv- 
ery, whether performed by a cable company or by Bell 
Atlantic Video Services (“BVS”), involves editorial deci- 
sions that are functionally indistinguishable from those 
involved in newspaper editing. None of the parties chal- 
lenging the plaintiffs’ motion for summary judgment have 
disputed that point. However, they have sought to distin- 
guish telephone companies from newspapers, suggesting 
newspaper do not receive government-bestowed benefits 
as telephone companies do. In this supplemental aff- 
davit, I briefly describe the extensive and valuable benefits 
conferred upon newspapers by federal, state, and local 
governments. 

2. In its Memorandum, the United States claims that 
the plaintiffs depend upon federal and state government 
for acquisition and maintenance of their monopoly of 
local telephone service. Mem. of United States at 44. 
See also Mem. of NCTA at 4 & 43 (local telephone com- 
panies are the recipient of a valuable government grant). 
The United States also claims that the plaintiffs depend 
upon government for use of rights-of-way. Mem. of 
United States at 60. The United States argues that the 
print medium “has never enjoyed any remotely similar 
set of government-bestowed benefits.” /d. at 44 n.41. 
In their brief, the Newspaper Association of America 
and the Virginia Press Association make similar claims 
concerning government-conferred monopoly status, but 


294 


add that telephone companies use the electromagnetic 
spectrum as well as public rights-of-way. Brief Amici 
Curiae of NAA and VPA at 23-24. 

3. Among the benefits conferred upon newspapers by 
the government are the following: 

a. Competition between separately owned local daily 
newspapers is exceedingly rare. In 1992, daily news- 
papers were published in 1,513 U.S. cities. Only 37 
cities had two or more separately owned, competing dail- 
ies; 19 of these cities had newspapers published under 
joint-operating agreements which allow papers to merge 
activities such as advertising and printing. Newspaper 
Association of America, Facts About Newspapers 92 at 
26 (1992). In Citizen Publishing Co. v. United States, 
394 US. 131 (1969), a joint-operating agreement was 
found to be a violation of the antitrust laws because it 
involved price fixing and profit pooling, and restricted 
entry into the market. Congress responded by conferring 
upon the newspaper industry one of the few limited ex- 
emptions from the antitrust laws. The Newspaper Preser- 
vation Act, 15 U.S.C. §§ 1801-1804, permits newspapers 
that maintain separate editorial staffs to combine their 
printing, distribution, advertising, circulation, and busi- 
ness departments. The joint-operating agreements author- 
ized by the Act eliminate advertising competition among 
the jointly operated papers, and create cost savings that 
place actual and potential competitors at a marked dis- 
advantage. Many commentators have criticized the Act 
as being anticompetitive. See, e.g., Flynn, Antitrust and 
the Newspapers: A Comment on S. 1312, 22 Vand. L. 
Rev. 103 (1968); Knox, Antitrust Exemptions for News- 
papers: An Economic Analysis, 1971 Law & Soc. Ord. 
3; Comment, The Newspaper Preservation Act, 32 U. 
Pitt. L. Rev. 347 (1971). As the Court of Appeals for 
the Ninth Circuit wrote, Congress was aware of the Act's 
anticompetitive effects, but made a policy determination 
that other considerations, such as preserving editorial di- 


295 


versity, were paramount. Committee for an Independem 
P-l v. Hearst Corp., 704 F.2d 467, 481-82 (9th Cir. 
1983). 

b. Newspapers receive preferential rates and expedited 
delivery from the United States Postal Service. See United 
States Postal Service, Domestic Mail Manual at 371-491 
(1992) (discussing second-class mail policies and pro- 
cedures). This preferential treatment, designed to en- 
courage the circulation of newspapers, originated with 
Benjamin Franklin and has continued to the present. See 
generally W. Fuller, The American Mail: Enlarger of the 
Common Life at 122-27 (1972) (discussing 19th century 
postal laws favoring newspapers). Prior to the reorganiza- 
tion of the Post Office in 1970, rates for mail delivery of 
newspapers were heavily subsidized; second-class mail con- 
tributed only 3% of postal revenues but accounted for 
11% of total postal volume. G. Cullinan, The Post Office 
Department at 240 (1968). The amount of the subsidy 
has been reduced as the reorganized Postal Service at- 
tempts to become self-supporting, but Congress continues 
to appropriate funds to subsidize second-class mail. 
Rambo, “Newspaper and the Postal Service,” Presstime, 
Jan. 1986, at 20. 

c. The importation of newsprint is duty-free. United 
States International Trade Commission, Supplement 1 to 
the Harmonized Tariff Schedule of the United States at 
48-5 (1992). The absence of a duty significantly benefits 
newspaper publishers for two reasons. First, American 
newspapers import as much as 60% of the newsprint they 
use, primarily from Canada. Deutsch, “Bags and Boxes 
Full of Profits,” New York Times, Aug. 14, 1988, at sec. 
3, p. 1. Second, newsprint accounts for as much as 33% 
of the cost of publishing a daily newspaper. C. Fink, 
Strategic Newspaper Management at 78 (1988). This 
is second only to labor in publishing expenses. Jd. at 77- 
78; J. Udell, The Economics of the American Newspaper 
at 118-24 (1978). 


296 


d. Newspaper employees involved in delivery are ex- 
empt from certain provisions of the Fair Labor Standards 
Act, 29 U.S.C. § 213 (d). State labor laws also exempt 
newspaper carriers from collective bargaining, and mini- 
mum-wage and age requirements. See, e.g., West's Rev. 
Code Wash. Ann. § 49.46.010(f) (Supp. 1986); Pa. Stat. 
Ann. tit. 43 § 33.105(3) (Purdon Supp. 1985); Mass. 
Ann. Laws ch. 49 § 69 (Michie/Law Co-op. 1976). Al- 
though the percentage of youth newspaper carriers has 
been declining in recent years, in 1992, youth carriers 
constituted 65.7% of total U.S. daily carriers. Newspaper 
Association of America, Facts About Newspapers 92 at 
25 (1992). 

e. Newspapers are extensive users of the FCC-regulated 
spectrum. First, newspapers today receive information via 
communications satellites. This information includes news 
stories, photographs, and graphics from services such as the 
Associated Press, United Press International, and Reuters. 
Bacon, “Satellites Make Big Impact at Newspapers,” Press- 
time, Aug. 1988, at 6. The use of communications satel- 
lites has also fostered the growth of supplemental news 
services such as Scripps Howard News Service, Los An- 
geles Times-Washington Post News Service, New York 
Times News Service, and Knight-Ridder/Tribune News 
Information Service. W. Agee, P. Ault & E. Emery, /n- 
troduction to Mass Communications at 148 (10th ed. 
1991). Additionally newspapers receive national adver- 
tisements via satellite from the AD/SAT company. /d. 
at 430. Second, newspapers send finshed pages to printing 
plants via terrestial microwave links or communications 
satellites. For example, the Washington Post sends pages 
to printing plants in the District of Columbia and Spring- 
field, Virginia via microwave; the New York Times links 
its New York offices with a printing plant in Edison, New 
Jersey via satellite. National newspapers, such as the Wall 
Street Journal, Christian Science Monitor, USA Today, 


297 


and the New York Times national edition, send pages to 
printing plants, throughout the country via satellite. See, 
e.g. “Nationalizing the New York Times,” Editor & Pub- 
lisher, Jan. 21, 1984, at 36-37 (describing the technology 
used in satellite transmission of newspaper pages). Third, 
newspapers are experimenting with use of the spectrum to 
reach the consumer. For example, Knight-Ridder, one of 
the nation’s largest newspaper chains, is developing a hand- 
held notebook computer designed to receive newspaper 
content via satellite. Reilly, “Publishers Design Electronic 
Newspapers to Keep Control of Information Delivery,” 
Wall Street Journal, Feb. 26, 1993, at B1. 


t. Most states exempt newspapers from sales tax. A 
1986 study of newspaper taxation found only 12 states 
imposing taxes on newspaper circulation. “Six States 
Now Tax Some Form of Advertising, but the Number 
Taxing Newsprint and Ink Declines,” Presstime, Feb. 
1986, at 42. Only two states tax the purchase of mate- 
rials such as ink and newsprint used to produce news- 
papers. /d. 

g. Some states have enacted retraction statutes provid- 
ing special protection for newspapers in defamation ac- 
tions. These statutes preclude the award of a class of 
damages, or mitigate damages. Three of the 33 states 
with retraction statutes apply this protection only to news- 
papers; 8 limit it to newspapers and periodicals; 14 cover 
both print and broadcast media, and 8 cover any defend- 
ant. R. Sack, Libel, Slander, and Related Problems at 
379 (1980). 

h. Newsracks are often placed on city property such 
as sidewalks and the United States Supreme Court re- 
cently noted the importance of this form of distribution. 
City of Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, Inc., 61 
U.S.L.W. 4272, 4273 n.10 (U.S. Mar. 24, 1993) (“al- 
most half of the single copy sales of newspapers are now 
distributed through newsracks”). In general, newspaper 


298 


publishers do not pay municipalities a fee for placement 
of newsracks on public property. 

4. As this affidavit reveals, the claim that the news- 
paper industry does not receive valuable and extensive 
benefits from the government is clearly false. Government- 
conferred benefits pervasively affect newspaper publishing 
on a daily basis and increase newspaper profitability. 

I declare under penalty of perjury under the laws of 
the United States of America that the foregoing is true 
and correct. Executed on June 3, 1993. 


/s/ Wm. E. Lee 
WILLIAM E. LEE 


299 


IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE EASTERN DISTRICT OF VIRGINIA 
(Alexandria Division} 


[Caption Omitted in Printing] 


AFFIDAVIT OF H. R. STALLARD 


1. I am the President and Chief Executive Officer of 
The Chesapeake and Potomac Telephone Company of 
Virginia (“C&P”). I joined the company in 1959 as a 
Staff Assistant-Engineer and had various positions of in- 
creasing responsibility until I was appointed to my present 
position in 1988. This affidavit responds to several mat- 
ters raised by the government, NCTA, and others who 
filed briefs opposing our provision of video programming. 

2. Rate regulation. Bruce Owen’s affidavit claims that 
“local telephone companies characteristically are subject 
to cost-based regulation.” This was true in 1970, but the 
fact is that telephone regulation is moving away from 
cost-based formulas toward priced-based systems that re- 
ward companies if they reduce costs and penalize them if 
costs increase. C&P’s interstate telephone services are 
subject to regulation known as “price caps.” Under a 
formula established by the FCC, C&P’s charges for inter- 
State services may not exceed a specific “cap.” Each 
year, the cap is adjusted by the actual amount of infla- 
tion minus a fixed downward adjustment of 3.3% for 
assumed productivity gains. The resulting formula en- 
sures that prices decrease year after year in real, inflation- 
adjusted terms. 

3. At the state level, C&P is currently regulated 
under an experimental regulatory plan effective in 1989 


300 


which incents the company to avoid rate increases alto- 
gether. Under this Plan, C&P has not increased its basic 
schedule of rates. In fact, C&P has not increased its rate 
schedules since January, 1984, and C&P has actually 
reduced rates significantly since that time. The State 
Corporation Commission is currently evaluating the effec- 
tiveness of its Experimental Plan and is considering modi- 
fication to it. The Virginia General Assembly enacted 
legislation, effective July 1, 1993, that specifically em- 
powers the Commission to adopt new forms of regulation 
which are not cost-based. (A copy of the legislation is 
included in plaintiffs’ document appendix, vol. IV, tab 
52.) The legislation requires that any new form of regu- 
lation provide safeguards to “assure that there is no cross 
subsidization of competitive services of a telephone com- 
pany by monopoly services.” C&P will ask the State 
Corporation Commission under this legislation to modify 
regulation in such a way that C&P’s rates for basic service 
will be capped for a number of years and can be in- 
creased thereafter only at a fraction of the increase in 
the rate of inflation. We will specifically request that 
the Commission adopt this regulatory plan so as to 
decouple the relationship between prices of regulated 
basic services and their costs. 

4. Owen and others claim that C&P will subsidize its 
Alexandria video transport network by raising rates. How- 
ever, as I have stated, the curent [sic] regulatory envi- 
ronment in Virginia will not permit this to happen. More- 
over, when C&P filed this lawsuit, it committed to the 
State Corporation Commission that it will not, in any 
event, seek to increase rates for basic telephone services 
as a result of its investment in Alexandria to enhance 
its telephone network to carry video. (See my letter of 
December 17, 1992, to the Commission and the Decem- 
ber 21, 1992, response from Hon. Preston C. Shannon, 
attached. ) 


301 


5. Owen’s cross subsidy theory would also apply to 
other competitive services that C&P and its affiliates are 
permitted to provide. C&P and its affiliates provide a 
number of information services and a number of other 
services which are competitive with those offered by non- 
regulated or lightly-regulated firms. Yet, nowhere in the 
government's document submission nor in the submis- 
sions of the government’s amici is there any allegation 
that C&P has engaged in cross subsidy. For example, the 
provision of access service from a customer’s location to 
a long distance company’s switch is increasingly competi- 
tive. But C&P is not increasing any of its basic service 
rate schedules to offset competitive losses or to subsidize 
competitive access services. In fact, basic rates have been 
stabilized or decreased. 

6. Transactions between C&P and BVS. Owen claims 
that BVS intends to sell video programming to C&P. 
Such sales allegedly would allow C&P “to increase the 
apparent costs and therefore the permitted price of the 
regulated service.” Owen claims that these sales will 
create “a substantial opportunity to engage in cross- 
subsidization.” Owen, however has the facts back- 
wards. BVS will not be selling programming to C&P; 
instead, C&P will be selling tariffed video transport to 
BVS as well as to other unaffiliated programmers on the 
same terms. BVS’s programming will not be part of 
C&P’s costs of regulated service. 

7. The government claims that there are state law 
obstacles to C&P’s provision of tariffed video transport 
services to BVS. C&P, however, currently provides tar- 
iffed services to various corporate affiliates. For example, 
C&P provides tariffed telephone services to its affiliate, 
Bell Atlantic Mobile, which provides cellular telephone 
service. C&P has neither sought nor obtained approval 
for the provision of these services from the State Cor- 
poration Commission under the statutory provisions the 
government cites. I am advised by counsel that such 
approval is not required for the provision of tariffed 


302 


services to affiliates and, to my knowledge, the State 
Corporation Commission has never required such ap- 
proval. Nor has the fact that some of C&P’s affiliates 
engage in non-telephone businesses, which C&P itself 
might be barred from engaging in, ever presented an 
obstacle to C&P’s provision of tariffed services to them. 

8. Pole attachments. The government alleges that 
“within six months of entering the cable television market, 
C&P could cripple the only competition” by terminating 
its agreements with cable operators respecting pole at- 
tachments. This prediction is not reflected in experience. 
C&P is in competition with cable companies today. None- 
theless, as far back as C&P’s records go, C&P has not 
terminated an agreement for pole attachments or conduit 
with a cable company that was providing cable service 
to customers. 

9. To eliminate any uncertainty about C&P’s willing- 
ness to renew its agreement with Jones Intercable respect- 
ing pole attachments and conduit in Alexandria, C&P has 
offered to extend the agreement for the 20-25 year term 
of Jones’s renewal of its cable franchise. (See the at- 
tached letter from James E. Howerton to Sherry Gilmore 
of Jones Intercable. ) 

10. Franchise. The government and others claim that 
the ban on video programming is justified by the fact that 
telephone companies have franchises to provide telephone 
service. C&P has certificates of convenience and neces- 
sity, issued by the State Corporation Commission, to 
provide local exchange and toll telephone services in 
many areas of the Commonwealth, including the City of 
Alexandria, Virginia. (The Alexandria certificate is in- 
cluded in plaintiffs’ document appendix, vol. IV, tab 53). 
These certificates are not conditioned on C&P or its 
affiliates refraining from providing video programming. 

11. Not all telephone companies have franchises, and 
some telephone companies have franchises that are non- 
exclusive. For example, C&P’s affiliate, The C&P Tele- 


303 


phone Company of Maryland, has no franchise. Another 
of C&P’s affiliates, The Chesapeake and Potomac Tele- 
phone Telephone Company, which provides telephone 
service in the District of Columbia, has a franchise that 
is non-exclusive. 

12. Alleged protection from competition. The govern- 
ment and others also claim that the ban on video pro- 
gramming is justified by the fact that the government is 
supposedly protecting telephone companies from com- 
petition. In fact, the federal government has spent the 
last two decades promoting competition in the telephone 
business, preempting state law where necessary. 

13. In 1970, C&P was the sole provider of telephone 
handsets and other customer telephone equipment in the 
parts of Virginia where it provides telephone service. 
In the late 1970s, the FCC mandated competition for 
equipment. The Bell Companies continue to provide 
telephone equipment but, according to statistics compiled 
by the trade association of equipment suppliers, they col- 
lectively supply only about 10% of U.S. purchases. 

14. In 1970, C&P was the sole provider of pay tele- 
phones in the parts of Virginia where it provides tele- 
phone service. In 1984, the FCC opened up competition 
for pay telephones and preempted conflicting state laws. 
Today, the number of pay telephones provided by com- 
petitors is growing at the rate of 20% per year and the 
number of C&P’s pay telephones is declining. 

15. In 1970, C&P was the sole provider of local tele- 
phone services in the parts of Virginia that it serves. This 
was largely true even in 1984. In 1991, the FCC an- 
nounced that it would promote competition in local 
telephone services used to originate and terminate inter- 
state calls by requiring C&P and other telephone com- 
panies to provide new forms of interconnection with the 
competitive access providers (“CAPs”). Today, C&P 
faces competition from CAPs such as Metropolitan Fiber 
Systems, which provides services in Northern Virginia. 


304 


And in Richmond and in Norfolk, cable companies serv- 
ing those areas are rapidly positioning themselves to pro- 
vide competitive access service. 

16. In 1970, there were virtually no mobile radio 
services in competition with local telephone services. In 
the early 1980s, the FCC began issuing spectrum licenses 
to cellular radio providers. In 1984, 32 systems had been 
licensed, serving some 92,000 subscribers. Today, as the 
parties have stipulated, licenses have been issued nation- 
wide, there are 11,000,000 cellular subscribers, and the 
number of subscribers is growing 40% per year. 

17. The FCC has begun licensing additional radio- 
based telephone service providers. The amount of spec- 
trum to be licensed by the FCC is many times the amount 
allocated to current cellular providers and would be suffi- 
cient to provide telephone service to everyone in the 
Nation. As the parties have stipulated, the FCC has 
granted 203 experimental licenses for personal communi- 
cation services (“PCS”), thirty five of them to cable 
companies. Jones Lightwave, an affiliate of Jones Inter- 
cable, holds an FCC experimental license for a PCS trial 
in Alexandria, Virginia. Permanent PCS licenses may be 
issued within a year. Under a spectrum auction bill 
that passed a House Subcommittee recently, the FCC 
would have 270 days from final passage of the bill to 
begin issuing PCS licenses. The same bill would preempt 
state entry and rate regulation of commercial mobile 
services including cellular service and PCS.

the United States of America that the foregoing is true 
and correct. Executed on 6-4-93.

BVS will buy those transport services and provide pro-
